1FLAVOR INFUSION: WHAT, WITH WHAT, AND HOW
2Part 1
3 
4At first glance, this topic may seem very simple, as if there is not much to discuss. But! You might be surprised to learn that even when I was running a masterclass at a large-scale production facility, my recommendation to switch to cold aromatization came as a surprise to the team.
5 
6In reality, aroma and taste are inseparably connected and should always be considered as a whole.
7 
8Flavor–aroma pairing is a fascinating field you inevitably encounter when working in pastry arts. Once baking basic shortbread cookies or classic apple cakes is no longer inspiring, a natural desire arises to explore new or unconventional flavor combinations and to introduce subtle aromatic notes.
9 
10A well-chosen aroma and the dessert’s aftertaste can calm a person or, on the contrary, make them feel more energized; it can bring comfort or encourage reflection. For example, vanilla conveys warmth and a sense of calm, cinnamon evokes the feeling of a cozy autumn, while lemongrass and lavender have soothing properties.
11 
12To transfer aroma from one ingredient to another, they must be combined under specific conditions:
13 
14🔸 mixed together (stirred or blended)
15🔸 chilled together
16🔸 heated together
17🔸 simmered together
18 
19What can be aromatized?
20In mousses:
21 
22🔺 the liquid base (cream / milk)
23🔺 the sugar syrup used for whipping egg whites (if the mousse is based on Italian meringue)
24🔺 mousses may also be aromatized using pastes
25 
26📍 Only thick pastes are used — those that do not compromise, but rather improve the dessert’s texture:
27— nut pastes, praline paste, certain fruit pastes
28 
29In ganaches:
30 
31🔺 cream
32🔺 fruit purée
33🔺 water
34 
35In berry fillings:
36 
37🔺 the berries themselves or berry purée are aromatized using the cold method or through vacuum infusion
38In sponge cakes and other baked goods exposed to high temperatures:
39🔺 the aromatizing ingredient is added directly to the recipe
40Pt 2
41In the previous part, we discussed WHAT can be flavored. Today, let’s talk about WHAT TO USE to do that.
42 
43Most likely, the first thing that comes to mind is flavorings. Both natural or nature-identical flavorings, as well as synthetic ones, can help add aroma to desserts.
44 
45This method has 3 obvious advantages:
46 
471️⃣ it doesn’t require much time or effort — add a few drops, stir, and it’s done;
48 
492️⃣ it helps in situations where you aren’t allowed to add extra ingredients in order not to change the texture;
50 
513️⃣ it’s convenient when you need an unusual flavor (bubble gum, California strawberry, tiramisu).
52 
53In addition to flavorings, you can enhance the aroma of your desserts with:
54 
55🔸 vanilla (pods / paste / extract)
56🔸 spices and seasonings (pepper, cinnamon, cardamom, anise, coriander, star anise, cloves, ginger)
57🔸 herbs and flowers (tarragon, basil, dill, thyme, rosemary, lavender)
58🔸 tea (black, matcha, red, green)
59🔸 fruit pastes
60🔸 citrus zest
61🔸 freeze-dried berries
62🔸 tonka beans
63🔸 coffee beans
64🔸 alcoholic beverages (rum, brandy, liqueurs, infusions)
65🔸 syrups
66🔸 natural essential extracts and oils
67 
68And this list is far from complete!
69 
70We’ve figured out WHAT to use. Now let’s talk about HOW to use it. There are several flavoring and infusion methods:
71 
72🔺 cold infusion
73🔺 hot infusion
74🔺 flavoring by simmering
75🔺 vacuum sealing
76 
77COLD INFUSION
78 
79Cold infusion involves adding a specific flavoring component (spices, herbs, zest, etc.) to a liquid (cream, milk, berry purée) and then infusing it in the refrigerator for at least 12 hours.
80 
81This is the longest method — flavor develops slowly and is less intense than when using the hot infusion method.
82To completely extract aroma from fresh herbs, you’ll need 3–5 days, and from dried ones — about 20 days.
83 
84Part 3
85 
86Continuing to explore the topic of flavor infusion, today we’ll look at the hot method, which I use quite often when developing recipes.
87THE HOT METHOD
88 
89Many of you have probably already guessed that the hot method involves heat treatment—and you’re absolutely right.
90 
91Hot infusion means bringing a liquid to a boil together with spices, herbs, or other flavoring ingredients, and then letting it steep for 30–60 minutes.
92 
93You can use cream, milk, or berry purée as the base for infusion. For example, you can infuse cream with dried lavender flowers, as I did in the Blueberry–Lavender Cheesecake, or create a rich coffee aroma by infusing cream with ground coffee (the Coffee–Baileys Cheesecake).
94 
95It’s very simple. But there are a few secrets I’d love to share with you.
96 
97SECRETS OF THE HOT METHOD
98 
99📌 Heat the liquid for infusion over medium or low heat so it warms up gradually.
100 
101📌 During the infusion process, try to use as little extra equipment as possible when preparing ingredients. For example, it’s better to zest citrus directly into the cream rather than onto a board first and then transferring it. Spices, herbs, and citrus contain a large amount of essential oils, which evaporate easily.
102 
103📌 Once the liquid reaches a boil, remove the saucepan from the heat immediately. At excessively high temperatures, flavoring ingredients may release compounds that negatively affect the taste and aroma.
104 
105📌 After heating, it’s best to cover the liquid with plastic wrap directly on the surface. If you use a lid, excess condensation may form and drip back into the liquid, and a film can develop on the surface that you’ll later have to remove.
106 
107📌 After the infusion time, strain the liquid through a sieve or cheesecloth.
108 
109📌 Once completely cooled, the infused liquid can be stored in the refrigerator for the same shelf life as the base product. However, keep in mind that the aroma will gradually fade with each day of storage.
110 
111 
112FACTORS THAT AFFECT STARCH FUNCTIONALITY
113 
114In previous posts, we discussed different types of starch and how starch works. Today, let’s focus on what specifically affects the rate of starch thickening.
115 
116📚 So, the factors that influence the gelatinization temperature (t°) of starch include:
117 
118— TYPE OF STARCH — each type of starch has an optimal amount of heat required for proper gelatinization.
119 
120The time required for complete gelatinization of root starches is always shorter than that required for corn starch. In most cases, unmodified root starches should not be brought to a boil. When cooked for too long, unmodified root starches develop an excessively elastic or stringy texture.
121 
122— AMOUNT OF TENDERIZERS — sugars and fats slow down the rate at which starch granules absorb water and swell.
123 
124The slower they absorb water, the longer it takes for starch granules to gelatinize. In fact, if enough sugar is present, it can completely prevent starch gelatinization. This is one of the ways sugars and fats tenderize baked goods: they reduce the amount of gelatinized starch, which is responsible for structure formation. Sugar also increases the transparency of starch-thickened mixtures.
125 
126— AMOUNT OF ACID — acids hydrolyze (break down) large starch molecules into smaller ones, reducing their thickening power.
127 
128Acids also weaken starch granules, allowing them to gelatinize faster and more easily. In fact, if enough acid is present, gelatinization occurs so rapidly that the starch mixture may appear not to thicken at all.
129STARCH. How does it work?
130 
131The process depends on the temperature setting. When starch granules are placed in cold water, its molecules attract water molecules and the granules swell just slightly. However, when the water is heated, the starch granules undergo an irreversible process called GELATINIZATION.
132 
133There is an optimal amount of heat for proper thickening and gel formation:
134 
135— too little heat → too few granules swell
136 
137— too much heat → too many granules break down
138 
139Undercooking or overcooking starch results in insufficient thickening and gel formation.
140 
141UNDERCOOKING starch causes additional problems as well:
142 
143— because raw granules are hard and dense, undercooked starch feels gritty in the mouth
144 
145— undercooked starch is opaque and has an unpleasant starch taste
146 
147— if stored for a day or longer, undercooked starch mixtures tend to release droplets or even pools of water around the gel
148 
149There are many factors affect the gelatinization t° of starches. The higher the gelatinization t°, the longer starch takes to gelatinize and, thus, the greater the risk of undercooking.
150 
151Here are the factors that affect starch gelatinization t°:
152 
153• type of starch used
154 
155• amount of fats and sugar
156 
157• amount of acid
158ALL ABOUT STARCH: TYPES AND USES
159 
160A starch molecule consists of two polysaccharides—amylose and amylopectin, which are themselves made of glucose.
161 
162Different types of starch have unique properties. There are four main types of starch:
163⁃ Grain starches (corn, rice, wheat, waxy corn)
164⁃ Root starches (potato, tapioca)
165⁃ Modified starches
166⁃ Instant (quick-action) starches
167 
168 
169 
170All starches start as either grain or root starches. Instant and modified starches are made from these by chemical treatment or physical processing.
171 
172ROOT starches differ from GRAIN starches because they contain fewer amylose molecules and more amylopectin:
173⁃ they don’t have a grainy taste
174⁃ they have better clarity
175⁃ they form a softer gel
176 
177MODIFIED starches are designer starches created by manufacturers to achieve specific desired properties:
178⁃ increased resistance to excessive heat and acid, which usually thins products thickened with starch
179⁃ better stability during freezing, which usually causes starch gels to shrink, stick together, or spread
180⁃ to change texture
181⁃ to speed up or slow down gelatinization
182 
183However, the primary reason for using modified starch is its enhanced stability.
184 
185INSTANT starches thicken and form gels without heating. To make instant starch, the manufacturer pre-cooks (gelatinizes) it and then dries it, so the granules absorb water without heat.
186 
187They are perfect for thickening heat-sensitive products. For example, the bright green color and delicate flavor of kiwi balls are preserved when thickened with instant starch.
188THICKENING and GEL FORMATION. What’s the difference?
189 
190We all use starch and gelatin to make desserts, but few people really know how they work.
191 
192Let’s break it down.
193 
194Thickeners and gelling agents (thick. & gell.) work by absorbing or holding a large amount of water. Fun fact: the most common thick. & gell. in baking is a plain old egg! 🥚
195 
196Thick. & gell. — like gelatin, plant gums, and starches — all have one thing in common: they’re made of very large molecules. Some, like starches and gums, are polysaccharides. Others, like gelatin, are proteins.
197 
198POLYSACCHARIDES are giant molecules made of many sugar units (saccharides) linked together.
199 
200PROTEINS are giant molecules made of many amino acids linked together.
201 
202THICKENING happens when water and other molecules or particles in your recipe move very slowly.
203 
204This happens when:
205⁃ Large molecules touch each other and tangle freely
206⁃ Water is absorbed and trapped by swollen starch granules
207⁃ Air bubbles (in foams) or fat droplets (in emulsions) slow down water movement
208 
209GEL FORMATION occurs when water and other molecules in the recipe cannot move at all.
210 
211This happens when large molecules bind tightly or tangle into a network that traps water and other substances.
212 
213Some gelling agents (like agar-agar) are so powerful that a gel can form even when water makes up over 99% of the product!
214 
215Some ingredients can do both — thicken in small amounts and form gels in larger amounts — like gelatin, corn starch, and pectin.
216 
217Other ingredients only thicken. They never form gels, no matter how much you add. They simply make the mixture thicker and stickier — similar to guar gum or waxy cornstarch.
218SHELF LIFE OF FROSTINGS
219 
220✅ Preparing frostings
221 
222I constantly remind my students that making frosting is a process inseparable from assembling the cake.
223 
224All whipped (aerated) frostings — buttercream, ganache, sour-cream or cream-cheese frosting — must be used right after preparation.
225 
226‼️ They cannot be stored and shouldn’t be prepared in advance.
227 
228Butter-based frostings can be kept in the fridge at 2–6°C (~35–43°F) for no more than 5 hours.
229 
230✅ Storing finished products that contain frostings
231 
232The container must be clean, dry, and free of foreign odors.
233 
234All baked goods must be stored in the fridge at 2–6°C (~35–43°F) after being made.
235 
236The shelf life of frostings depends on their composition (eggs, alcohol, purées, fat content of dairy products, etc.) and storage conditions (room-temp display, fridge, and how often it is opened).
237 
238Buttercream frosting: 24 to 72 hours depending on ingredients (24 hours if eggs, like “Charlotte”).
239 
240Buttercream can be frozen for up to 1 month.
241 
242Meringue frosting: up to 72 hours in the fridge, up to 12 hours at room temp (~20–22°C/~68–72°F).
243 
244Custard-based frosting: at 4–6°C (~39–43°F) it keeps 48–72 hours (2–3 days). At room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F) only 6–12 hours because it spoils quickly. Cool quickly to 4°C (~39°F) after cooking to prevent bacterial growth, especially in the 23–50°C (~73–122°F) range.
245 
246Sour-cream frosting: in the fridge 4–6°C (~39–43°F) - 12–36 hours depending on fat content and ingredients. At room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F) - up to 6 hours. If used in cakes, keeps up to 3 days in the fridge at 4–6°C (~39–43°F).
247 
248Cream-based frosting: depends on ingredients and preparation method. Cream-cheese, sour-cream-and-cream, and butter-and-cream frostings keep up to 36 hours in the fridge at 4–6°C (~39–43°F).
249 
250Cheesecake-style or cream-cheese frosting: up to 48 hours in the fridge at 4–6°C (~39–43°F). Cream cheese should not sit at room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F) for more than 2 hours.
251 
252Ganache: depending on composition, from 72 hours in the fridge 4–6°C (~39–43°F) to several months at room temp (~23–50°C/~73–122°F).
253Mascarpone vs. Cream Cheese: Key Differences
254 
255Despite their similarities and wide use in cooking, mascarpone and cream cheese differ in production methods, composition, and properties.
256 
257Understanding these differences helps you choose the right product each time and substitute it when needed.
258 
259Mascarpone
260 
261🔸Origin: traditional Italian cheese
262🔸Fat content: 70–75%, almost as rich as butter
263🔸Flavor and aroma: delicate, creamy, with a buttery note; almost neutral, enhancing other flavors harmoniously
264🔸Texture: very soft, silky, prone to separating if overwhipped
265🔸Production: typically made by adding acid to warmed heavy cream. The combination of acid and heat coagulates the casein, forming a fine, smooth cheese mass
266🔸Uses: classic ingredient in tiramisu; used in mousses, creams, and desserts where high fat content and velvety texture are desired
267 
268Cream Cheese
269 
270🔸Origin: industrially developed product, common in the US and Europe
271🔸Fat content: on average 30–35%
272🔸Flavor and aroma: mild with noticeable tang and slight saltiness, which helps balance the sweetness in desserts
273🔸Texture: denser and more stable compared to mascarpone
274🔸Production: made by adding lactic acid bacteria and often enzymes to milk or cream. Once the liquid whey is drained, the cheese is processed until it reaches the desired consistency
275🔸Uses: base for cheesecakes, frostings, and fillings; provides structure and holds its shape well
276 
277Mascarpone offers a rich flavor, creaminess, and a buttery texture, but is less stable during processing.
278 
279Cream cheese ensures density and structure, adds a light tang that balances sweetness, but is less intensely creamy.
280 
281To sum up, the choice depends on the desired result:
282• Mascarpone is ideal for delicate frostings and mousse-based desserts
283• Cream cheese is best for recipes where stability, shape, and flavor balance are important👌
284Why baking soda ≠ baking powder? 🤔
285 
286And how to know which one you need for what you’re baking?
287 
288✔️Baking soda
289 
290Works only in combination with an acid—kefir, lemon juice, honey, etc.
291 
292The reaction starts immediately as soon as the ingredients are combined. Carbon dioxide begins to release, so the dough must go straight into the oven. If you wait too long, the baked goods won’t rise properly.
293 
294✔️Baking powder
295 
296It’s a combination of baking soda and a dry acid.
297 
298It comes in several forms, the most common being double-acting. Baked goods made with double-acting baking powder need heat to fully activate the leavening. A small amount of carbon dioxide is released when mixing; the rest is released during baking. This removes the need for rushing to portion and bake to ensure the dough rises just as good.
299 
300✔️Ammonium (ammonium carbonate)
301 
302Lesser known, but a very effective leavening agent.
303 
304Perfect for thin and dry baked goods— various cookies, including gingerbread cookies, wafers. Gives incredible crispness and lightness. However, it’s not used in large cakes or loaves: the smell of ammonia can stay in the finished product.
305 
306❗️Substitution
307 
308•Baking soda for baking powder—1 : 3 or triple the amount of baking powder.
309 
310•Baking powder for baking soda—3 : 1 or use 1 part soda + 2 parts cream of tartar for every 3 parts baking powder.
311 
312👉So if you’re making sponges or cakes with acidic ingredients, you can use baking soda.
313 
314👉If you need more control and stability—choose baking powder or a combo of soda + baking powder.
315 
316👉For thin crispy cookies or gingerbread dough—ammonium will be your secret ingredient.
317Why does crème anglaise turn out silky and delicate for some, but curdled and eggy for others?
318👉 And which cooking method is the safest?
319 
320Crème anglaise—also known as vanilla custard sauce—serves as the base for many desserts, including Bavarian creams, ice cream, and crèmeux. It’s made from milk, sugar, and egg yolks that are gently heated until lightly thickened.
321 
322⚡ Here precision is key: the temperature should not exceed about 82°C (~180°F), otherwise the yolks will scramble. Always cook slowly and attentively.
323 
324💡 Technically, it can be cooked up to 85°C (~185°F) without curdling, but it’s safer to stop slightly earlier, as the sauce continues to heat even after being removed from the stove due to the residual heat in the pan.
325 
326🥄 Main tips for success:
327 
328Whisk yolks with sugar right away—if you let them sit, the sugar will draw out moisture, leaving lumps of dried yolk.
329 
330Heat the milk almost to boiling so the process goes faster.
331 
332Temper the yolks by slowly pouring in the hot milk in a thin stream while whisking constantly.
333 
334Cook only over low heat, stirring continuously.
335 
336Test doneness in two ways:
337• With a thermometer—82°C (~180°F).
338• By the spoon test—the sauce should lightly coat the back of a spoon instead of running off like milk.
339 
340Keep in mind this is a very light sauce, so don’t expect it to thicken too much.
341 
342Immediately strain the sauce into a bowl set over an ice bath to cool quickly. Stir occasionally to ensure even cooling.
343 
344💡 If the sauce curdles, sometimes you can rescue it. Quickly add 30–60 ml (2–4 tbsp) of cold milk, transfer to a blender, and blend on high speed until smooth again.
345 
346Cooking methods:
347 
348▶️ Steaming—slowest, but the safest method.
349▶️ Bowl in simmering water—a balance of speed and control.
350▶️ Direct heat—best for experienced chefs, since the risk of ending up with scrambled eggs is much higher.
351Butter — the ideal fat for any baked goods, but along with its advantages, it also comes with some obvious drawbacks.
352 
353Let’s break it down.
354 
355Advantages of butter:
356• Pleasant mouthfeel;
357• Rich, creamy flavor.
358 
359Drawbacks of butter:
360• Expensive — several times pricier than alternative fats;
361• One of the trickiest fats to work with due to its narrow plasticity range—too hard to work with straight from the fridge, but it softens and melts very quickly in a warm room.
362 
363❗️The best temperature for creaming butter lies in a very narrow window: 18–21°C (~64–70°F).
364 
365• Spoils faster than other fats, especially unsalted butter. It’s highly prone to bacterial spoilage if not kept refrigerated for short storage or frozen for longer storage. Spoiled butter tastes sour, like bad milk, or develops a sharp rancid flavor.
366 
367One of the trickiest parts of working with butter—especially for beginners—is finding the right consistency for creaming.
368 
369❗️Cold butter (below 16°C/~60°F) is too firm to trap nice, even air pockets, while overly warm butter (above 24°C/~75°F) is too soft.
370 
371However, just as important as the butter temperature is, the room temperature is also crucial.
372 
373In summer, even if you start with butter at the perfect (~21°C/~70°F), after just a few minutes of mixing, the warmth of the bowl and additional friction heat can quickly raise the butter’s temperature to 24–25°C (~75–77°F). At that point, it creams very poorly.
374 
375❗️That’s why in summer, I always begin creaming butter at no higher than 16–17°C (~60–63°F).
376 
377Also, pay attention to mixing speed—it should be a moderate (medium) speed.
378 
379If you whip on high speed, the friction heats the ingredients too much. You’ll trap fewer air pockets, and the ones that do form will often be too large and uneven.
380ugar: Caramelization and the Maillard reaction—what they are and how they differ
381 
382In the culinary world, these two chemical processes play a key role in creating the color, aroma, and taste of many desserts. They are similar, but have important differences that every professional should understand ⬇️
383 
384Caramelization is the process of heating sugar, during which it melts, darkens, and develops a characteristic sweet, nutty flavor and aroma. It is a chemical reaction that occurs at high temperatures (over 160–170°C), when sugar breaks down into different compounds, creating new flavors and colors.
385 
386The Maillard reaction is a chemical interaction between sugars and amino acids in proteins (for example, from flour, eggs, milk), which starts at lower temperatures and produces more complex roasted flavors such as cocoa, coffee, and nuts.
387 
388Even a small amount of protein greatly accelerates this reaction, which is why baked goods with dairy products and eggs brown faster.
389 
390The Maillard reaction can occur slowly even at room temperature — for example, in powdered milk or white chocolate, which change color and taste over time.
391 
392❕ A few more important points:
393 
394• Monosaccharides (fructose, glucose) brown faster than disaccharides (sucrose), so invert syrup speeds up browning.
395 
396• Among sweeteners, the browning speed usually goes (from fastest to slowest):
397fructose > glucose > lactose > maltose > sucrose > isomalt.
398 
399• Minerals such as copper and iron, even in trace amounts, significantly enhance browning. They are present in some water, unrefined syrups (molasses, honey, maple syrup), and salt.
400 
401• The pH of the environment also matters: alkalis (such as baking soda) raise pH and increase browning, while acids (kefir, sour cream, cream of tartar) slow it down.
402 
403• Higher baking temperatures intensify browning, which is why the crust on bread or sponge cake is always darker than the inside.
404UGAR: HOW IT WORKS IN BATTER
405 
406All sugars are hygroscopic, meaning they have the ability to attract and retain water.
407 
408That’s why sugar plays a key role in:
409✔️creating a tender texture
410✔️retaining moisture
411✔️extending shelf life
412 
413CREATING TENDER TEXTURES
414 
415Once dissolved, sugar prevents:
416• gluten formation
417• protein coagulation
418• starch gelatinization
419 
420In other words, sugar slows down structure formation — and because of that, the batter has more time in the oven to rise, and the baked good remains soft and tender after baking.
421 
422Why does this happen?
423 
424Because gluten, proteins, and starch need water to form structure, and sugar — as a hygroscopic substance — absorbs and binds water, leaving less available liquid for the structure-builders.
425 
426Sugar raises the temperature at which proteins coagulate and starch gelatinizes, which delays structure formation.
427 
428👉 As a result, the more sugar in a recipe, the more tender the baked good becomes.
429 
430❕BUT it’s important to find the right balance
431 
432▪ If there’s too much sugar, structure barely forms: the product either doesn’t rise, or rises — and quickly collapses when cooled.
433 
434▪ If there’s too little sugar, structure forms too early — before steam has time to expand the dough. The baked good ends up with poor volume, and its surface tears because steam breaks through the prematurely formed crust.
435 
436RETAINING MOISTURE AND EXTENDING SHELF LIFE
437 
438Sugar’s hygroscopicity is the main reason why baked goods stay soft even after a few days.
439 
440🔹 The most effective for this is fructose — it retains moisture better than any other sugar.
441 
442High-fructose syrups also work well, including:
443 
444– invert syrup
445– honey
446– molasses
447– agave syrup
448 
449They retain moisture better than regular granulated sugar and are often used in recipes where lasting freshness is important.
450Hot Cream & Cool Nerves: Everything You Need To Know About Caramel 👌
451 
452Caramel can be temperamental, but it’s not nearly as scary once you know a few important things. All it really "asks" for is attention, consistency, and a little respect for physics!
453 
454❕ Here are some essential tips 👇
455 
456• To avoid crystallization, glucose syrup is often used—about 10–20% of the sugar amount. It helps keep the mixture smooth. If you don’t have syrup, it’s very important that your cookware is perfectly clean.
457 
458• Another critical factor is the heavy whipping cream. It must be hot. Cold cream coming into contact with dry caramel causes a shock reaction: the mixture seizes, splatters, and forms lumps. To avoid this, add very hot cream gradually, in small portions, while constantly stirring.
459 
460• In large-batch recipes, it’s helpful to divide the sugar into several parts. For example, melt part of it first, then gradually add the rest. This helps better control color, temperature, and prevents uneven melting.
461 
462• Caramel can be made in two ways:
463 
464dry (no liquid added)
465 
466or wet (with a small amount of water).
467If you opt for the wet method, remember: do not stir the syrup, as this may trigger crystallization.
468 
469• Judging the degree of caramelization by the color in the saucepan isn’t ideal—it often looks darker than it actually is. To see the true shade, let some caramel drip off your spatula. That way, you can get a better sense of the real color.
470 
471• It’s best to add the butter once the caramel has cooled slightly (to about 45–50°C/113-122°F). This reduces the risk of splitting and gives you a smooth, silky texture with no oil on the surface and a gentler, creamy flavor.
472 
473Nothing supernatural here—just a clear understanding of how caramel behaves at each stage.
474 
475Factors affecting meringue stability: TEMPERATURE, DENSITY OF EGG WHITES, WHIPPING TIME
476 
477Lets put the final touches on our topic of Meringue ✨
478 
479🔸 Temperature
480 
481Egg whites straight from the fridge don’t whip well.
482The ideal temperature for whipping is room temperature, around 21 °C (~70°F).
483 
484Warm egg whites capture air better, foam faster, and create a more stable structure.
485 
486🔸 Density of egg whites (freshness)
487 
488Runny (older) whites are easy to whip, give more volume,
489❕but the foam is less stable and collapses quickly.
490Fresh whites are thicker, and harder to whip,
491❕but the foam structure is strong and stable.
492 
493📌If volume is important—it’s better to use older eggs.
494📌If you need stability—choose fresh ones, and leave the older whites for regular baking.
495 
496🔸Whipping time
497 
498Both extremes—underwhipping and overwhipping—hurt meringue stability.
499 
500⏩Underwhipped whites—proteins don’t aggregate enough to form a solid film.
501This kind of foam is unstable and spreads quickly.
502⏩Overwhipped whites—with too long or aggressive whipping, the proteins denature and over-aggregate. The film around each air bubble becomes overstretched and loses elasticity.
503 
504In other words, overwhipping has a curdling/separating effect on the egg white structure, similar to overheating.
505The result is tightly bound, inelastic clumps of protein sitting in expelled liquid. This mass is no longer usable—you’ll have to throw it away.
506 
507🔸Other factors
508 
509⏩Salt
510Decreases foam stability. It shouldn’t be added during whipping.
511 
512⏩Whisk type
513Thick wires—create large bubbles, less stable foam.
514Thin whisk—creates small, uniform bubbles, more stable foam.
515TOP 7 TIPS FOR BAKING SPONGE CAKES
516 
5171.The batter should be a smooth, homogeneous emulsion
518 
519Properly mixed batter is an emulsion of water in fat; which means that water is held in the form of tiny droplets surrounded by fat.
520Proper emulsification ensures the right texture.
521 
5222.All ingredients should be at 21–22°C (~70–72°F)
523 
524At this temperature, eggs are more stable, gain more volume, and emulsify better with fats.
525Even one cold ingredient can cause the batter to split.
526 
5273.Proper preparation of ingredients, tools, and a preheated oven — “mise en place”, as French chefs say 👌
528 
529Always carefully read the entire recipe before you start the process.
530 
5314.Opening the oven door too early or moving the pans during baking may cause the sponge to collapse
532 
533This leads to a temperature drop and can damage the proteins and starches that are not yet fully set.
534 
5355.Thin sponge layers should be baked at higher temperatures
536 
537Baking thin sponge layers at low temperatures increases moisture loss, thus drying the sponge.
538Tall sponge layers are always baked at lower temperatures, allowing them to cook all the way to the center while gently browning on the outside.
539 
5406.Properly baked pound cake sponges slightly pull away from the sides of the pan, while egg-foam sponges should be soft to the touch and spring back when gently pressed
541 
542You can use a bamboo skewer to check for doneness, but the best method is by look and touch.
543For thin layers, gently lift the edge with an offset spatula — browning on the bottom is a sign of doneness.
544 
5457.Baked sponge layers should cool completely on a wire rack
546 
547Wrapping warm sponges traps too much moisture, making the layers sticky later on.
548Factors Affecting Meringue Stability:
549 
550❕FATS & ACIDS
551 
552LIPIDS
553Everyone knows that if even a drop of yolk gets into your egg whites, it’s best not to whip them into meringue.
554 
555But do you know why? What exactly does that yolk do that even beginner bakers are well aware it’s the sworn enemy of perfect meringue?
556 
557Let’s break it down⬇️
558 
559Lipids — fats, oils, and emulsifiers — interfere with aeration. Depending on their type and amount, lipids can either slow down or completely prevent aeration.
560 
561This is especially true for the lipids found in egg yolks, particularly lecithin. Even a small amount of yolk can prevent egg whites from whipping properly.
562 
563Lipids disrupt aeration by coating the proteins, thus preventing them from unfolding and bonding. But they do more than that — lipids compete with proteins for space on the surface of air bubbles. Since lipids on their own can’t form a strong, cohesive, and elastic network like egg whites can, the bubbles they coat expand quickly and then collapse.
564 
565PRO TIP
566To avoid problems with fats, oils, or emulsifiers when making meringue, always use a clean bowl, carefully separate every trace of yolk from the whites, and avoid plastic bowls and utensils — they tend to absorb grease and are difficult to fully clean.
567 
568ACID
569 
570Acid helps stabilize the meringue by lowering the pH. Cream of tartar is the most commonly used acid, but lemon juice or vinegar can also stabilize the foam.
571 
572Add acid early in the whipping process — it may take a bit longer to whip, but the resulting protein network will be more elastic and resistant to overwhipping, folding, piping, and baking. The meringue will also be whiter.
573 
574 
575Factors Affecting Meringue Stability: SUGAR
576 
577Meringue stability is vital. A stable meringue is dense and thick, yet elastic and springy, so it can easily withstand mixing, piping, and baking
578 
579Often, the very thing that increases meringue’s stability also reduces its volume and tenderness. As always, the goal of a pastry chef is to balance these opposing characteristics — stability with volume and delicacy
580 
581So, what exactly affects the stability of egg white foam?
582 
583• sugar
584• lipids (fats, oils, emulsifiers)
585• acidity
586• temperature
587• protein density
588• whipping time
589• salt
590• type of whisk
591 
592Sugar
593 
594Sugar greatly stabilizes meringue, although it also slows down the whipping process and slightly reduces the volume. In French meringue, where room-temperature egg whites are whipped with sugar, the sugar stabilizes it best when added slowly and in the proper amount
595 
596Sugar should be added gradually to give the sugar crystals time to dissolve and avoid weighing down the foam. In addition, if sugar is added too quickly, the protein molecules may not unfold properly.* As a result, we may end up with a softer meringue or, in extreme cases, egg whites that won’t whip at all
597 
598*Remember from the previous post: the unfolding of protein molecules is one of the two key conditions for creating egg white foam
599 
600Sugar stabilizes meringue by slowing down the unfolding and aggregation (binding) of protein molecules. This contributes to stability by protecting against overwhipping
601 
602Another way sugar stabilizes meringue is by dissolving into the liquid film of the foam, creating a thick, viscous syrup that protects air bubbles from collapsing
603 
604Sugar syrup also adds a satiny shine to the surface of the meringue
605 
606USEFUL TIPS
607 
608➡️ Undissolved sugar crystals weigh down the meringue and weaken it. After baking, these crystals attract moisture and can sometimes form unappealing syrup beads on the surface of the meringue
609To minimize beading and volume loss, use powdered sugar instead of granulated sugar. Always add it slowly, allowing it to fully dissolve between additions
610 
611➡️ Since sugar slows down whipping, be sure to add it only after the egg whites have reached the “beer foam” stage
61210 Critical Mistakes a Beginner Pastry Chef Might Make
613 
614❌ Pouring sugar over the eggs for whipping, then running off to the store because you ran out of flour
615 
616❌ Reducing the amount of sugar in a recipe to make the dessert less sweet
617 
618❌ Adding extra baking powder—after all, it surely won’t hurt, and might even make the pastry fluffier
619 
620❌ Beating butter with sugar on high speed to speed up the process
621 
622❌ Adding pectin to purée without sugar or dumping it in all at once to save time
623 
624❌ Using mascarpone instead of cream cheese—what’s the difference, right?
625 
626❌ Trying to melt a large portion of sugar in a small saucepan
627 
628❌ Buying heavy cream without checking the fat content and ingredients
629 
630❌ Substituting or using chocolate in a recipe without paying attention to the cocoa butter percentage
631 
632❌ Trusting recipes from the internet and thinking, “Surely they wouldn’t write complete nonsense”
633Have you ever wondered how liquid egg whites turn into foam during whipping?
634 
635When you whip eggs, two things happen simultaneously.
636 
637Air bubbles are beaten into the liquid, and the proteins in the egg whites denature—or unfold.The unfolded proteins move quickly through the liquid to the surface of the air bubbles.
638 
639Once there, neighboring proteins bond—or aggregate—around the bubbles, forming a film-like network. Surrounded by these strong, flexible films, the air bubbles are less likely to burst, allowing more bubbles to be beaten in.
640 
641What happens to egg white proteins during whipping is similar, though not identical, to what occurs with them during heating.In both cases, protein molecules unfold and bond, forming a type of structure.
642POOR CAKE TEXTURE?
643CREAMING THE BUTTER: SIMPLE RULES
644 
645The process of making batter often begins with creaming the butter and sugar. Few people are aware of this, but this step is crucial for achieving the desired texture.
646 
647After all, it’s during creaming that air bubbles get trapped in the batter. A fine-pored texture is the result of many small, uniform air bubbles. Large and uneven bubbles in the batter lead to a coarse texture.
648 
649⚠️The correct ingredient temperature 🌡️ and mixing speed are two essential conditions for forming perfect air pockets.
650 
651Cold butter is too firm to form a large number of tiny air bubbles, while overly warm butter is too soft.
652 
653The mixer speed should be moderate. If you cream at high speed, friction will quickly warm the butter. Fewer air bubbles will form, and they’ll be large and uneven.
654 
655 
656A WHISK, A PADDLE, OR A DOUGH HOOK?
657At first glance, the answer seems simple: ✔️ A whisk for whipping ✔️ A paddle for mixing soft dough or batter ✔️ A dough hook for kneading stiff dough
658 
659But in practice, as I’ve observed, these distinctions often confuse students. Choosing the correct attachment isn’t always straightforward!
660 
661For example, should you use a whisk or a paddle attachment when creaming butter for a sponge cake or cupcake?
662 
663What about making frosting or shortbread dough?
664 
665Is a whisk better when mixing the cheese filling for a cheesecake, or should you opt for a paddle attachment? Perhaps a paddle with flexible silicone edges?
666 
667And what if you’re using a hand mixer—does that change the choice entirely?😳
668 
669Let’s break it down entirely because selecting the properattachment is crucial to achieving perfect desserts.
670 
671WHISK
672 
673The whisk is specifically designed for whipping, a process where the goal is to incorporate as many tiny air bubbles as possible into the mixture.
674 
675You’ll need a whisk for tasks like whipping whole eggs for sponge cakes, beating egg whites for meringue, preparing egg yolks for sponge cake or frosting, whipping cream or any cream-based frosting, preparing marshmallows, mousse, or soufflés with gelatin.
676 
677Seems straightforward, right? But here’s a twist…
678 
679Have you ever noticed that some Kenwood whisks have thin rods while others have thicker ones? 🤔
680Part 2
681 
682Yes, the thickness of your whisk’s rods truly matters. A whisk with thick rods cannot whip meringue or cream to achieve the correct consistency.
683 
684Foams whipped with thick rods will have large, uneven, and unstable air bubbles. This results in sponge cakes with poor volume and mousses that lack tenderness and airiness.
685 
686Therefore, if your stand mixer only comes with a whisk with thick rods, I recommend purchasing one with thin rods separately. Until then, you’ll need to whip meringues and cream using a hand mixer.
687 
688So, what is a whisk with thick rods good for? It’s perfect for preparing frostings made with cream cheese or mascarpone combined with heavy whipped cream.
689 
690PADDLE – This attachment is used for mixing batters, soft doughs and for creaming butter.
691 
692Yes, creaming! While we often use the term “whipping,” in baking literature, butter is always “creamed,” not whipped.
693Part 3
694 
695First of all, I want to sincerely thank you all for the incredible engagement on the previous post. Your comments and ❤️s truly inspire me to keep sharing more valuable content with you.
696 
697Today, let’s talk about the CREAMING METHOD—a fundamental technique in baking that involves softening a solid fat, such as butter or shortening, into a smooth, homogeneous mass before combining it with other ingredients. This method is commonly used when making buttercream, cake and cupcake batters, and cookie dough.
698 
699During creaming, fat is whipped together with sugar/dry ingredients until the mixture becomes light and fluffy, and increases in volume. This happens because tiny air bubbles are incorporated into the mass and retained within the semi-solid fat. These air bubbles remain in the batter and expand during baking, contributing to the leavening and fluffy texture of the final product.
700 
701So, if our goal is to introduce air into the butter—just like we do when whipping cream—why don’t we use a whisk?
702 
703The answer lies in the nature of the ingredients. A whisk is perfect for incorporating air into liquid ingredients like eggs or cream. Butter, however, is best creamed while it’s in a solid but pliable (plastic) state.
704 
705If you try using a whisk, it will likely become clogged with butter, causing the mixer to work “idle”. That’s why a paddle attachment is more suitable for creaming butter.
706 
707But you might rightfully point out that a paddle attachment can also just smear the butter along the sides of the bowl, causing the mixer to run idle as well.
708 
709And you’d be absolutely right—especially when working with a small amount of butter and using a paddle that doesn’t have flexible silicone edges. In that case, you’ll often need to stop the mixer periodically to scrape the butter off the sides and bottom of the bowl manually.
710 
711This is where the paddle attachment with soft silicone edges becomes a game changer…
712HOW TO WORK WITH SHORTCRUST PASTRY EFFECTIVELY?
713Part 1🔥
714 
715Working with shortcrust pastry is a very detailed topic, but I have compiled key points to help you achieve good results.
716 
7171. Proper Mixing is Key to Success
718 
719The success of your pastry largely depends on correct mixing techniques. If the dough is overmixed or mixed improperly—using the wrong tool or speed—you may end up with a tough product instead of a delicate, crumbly texture. This happens due to the overdevelopment of gluten when water from the butter and eggs interacts with the flour.
720✔️Recommendation: Mix the dough as quickly as possible using the correct technique to isolate the proteins and minimize gluten formation.
721 
7222. Chilling the Dough
723 
724Chilling the dough is crucial. After mixing, the dough should rest to ensure the flour is fully hydrated and the fat solidifies.
725✔️Recommendation: Refrigerate the dough for several hours before use or before freezing. If you freeze it immediately, the water will freeze without properly hydrating the flour.
726 
7273. Working Temperature is Key to Pliability
728 
729The dough should be elastic and soft, but not overly pliable. Before rolling out chilled dough, work it gently with a rolling pin or by hand to enhance its flexibility.
730 
731🔜 In the upcoming post, I will discuss the following topics:
732• Rolling the dough;
733• Chilling after rolling;
734• Baking.
735Part 2 🔥
736 
7374. Rolling and Stretching
738 
739📏 For optimal results, roll out the dough between two sheets of parchment paper or plastic wrap. Once the dough is soft and pliable, use gentle stretching motions.
740❗️Important: Roll to a thickness of 3 mm. To ensure even thickness, place two strips of the same height on either side of the dough.
741 
7425. Chilling After Rolling
743 
744❄️ After rolling, allow the dough to cool again so it firms up and retains its shape when transferred to a mold or baking sheet.
745⚠️ Caution: Unevenly rolled dough may bake inconsistently, leading to burnt thin areas and undercooked thicker sections.
746 
7476. Baking
748 
749🔥 Shortcrust pastry can be baked at temperatures ranging from 160 °C to 220 °C (~320 - 428 °F) with 190 °C (~374 °F) being ideal. Utilize perforated baking sheets or trays to prevent air pockets.
750✔️Tip: Employ two perforated silicone sheets for consistently shaped cookies.
751 
752 
753HOW TO MAKE CUT CAKE SLICE LOOK UNFORGETTABLE? 🍰
754 
755We’re all familiar with the classic cake assembly process: cake layer, frosting, filling, cake layer. However, did you know that by slightly altering this technique, you can create something truly spectacular that will leave everyone in awe?
756 
757Here are some creative ways to make your cake slices look truly impressive:
758 
7591️⃣ DUAL-COLORED FROSTING
760Use two different shades of frosting and pipe them in concentric circles. The colors can be either complementary or contrasting. For a striking visual effect, try assembling a Coffee-Baileys cake.
761 
7622️⃣ INNOVATIVE FILLING TECHNIQUE
763Instead of layering the filling on top of the frosting, pipe it inside of it, or alternate filling rings with frosting rings on one layer. The result will be visually stunning! We recommend trying this method with cakes like Pistachio-Raspberry and Black Currant-Coffee.
764 
7653️⃣ VERTICAL CAKE ROLL
766For a unique twist, assemble your cake vertically by rolling the layers into a large spiral. This technique works best with thin, flexible cake layers and is guaranteed to impress. Try this with the Raspberry-Lychee-Rose honey cake or the Mega Chocolate Cherry honey cake.
767 
7684️⃣ CONTRASTING FILLINGS
769Incorporate multiple fillings with contrasting or complementary colors, such as jelly with frosting or jelly with mousse. Experiment like this with the Raspberry-Rose honey cake for a striking presentation.
770 
771Guidelines for Storing, Sweetening, and Mixing Heavy Whipped Cream 👩🏻‍🍳
772 
773Continuing our discussion on working with heavy whipped cream, here are some essential tips👇🏻
774 
775SWEETENING 🧁
776The classic method for sweetening heavy whipped cream is adding sugar near the end of the whipping process when the cream forms soft peaks.
777 
778👆🏻Alternatively, you can add sugar at the beginning of the whipping process. This will significantly increase the whipping time, so it’s best to do this with a mixer rather than by hand.
779 
780❕ For sweetening, use very fine granulated sugar or sifted powdered sugar for better stability.
781 
782WHIPPING AND MIXING 🥣
7831. Stop whipping once the heavy whipped cream forms peaks that hold their shape. 👆🏻If you continue whipping, the cream will first become grainy and then separate into butter and whey.
784 
7852. Avoid overbeating heavy whipped cream that will be mixed with other ingredients. During mixing, the cream will continue to whip, potentially leading to overwhipping.
786 
7873. Add flavorings only after the heavy whipped cream has been whipped.
788 
789STORAGE❄️
790If the heavy whipped cream won’t be used immediately, cover it and store it in the refrigerator.
791Top Tips for Whipping Cream 👩🏻‍🍳
792 
793👇🏻 Heavy whipped cream is not only one of the most popular frostings but also a crucial component in many desserts, such as mousses.
794 
795💬If you find this topic useful, hit the ❤️, save the post, and share any challenges you face when working with heavy whipped cream in the comments.
796 
797When preparing heavy whipped cream, follow these expert guidelines:
798 
7991. Age Matters: Use a heavy whipping cream that is at least one day old. Very fresh, heavy whipped cream may not whip as well.
800 
8012. Keep It Cool: Thoroughly chill the heavy whipped cream and all equipment, especially in warm weather. Heavy whipped cream that isn’t sufficiently cooled can be difficult to whip and may separate easily.
802 
8034. Right Tools, Right Speed: Use a whisk with thin rods and set your mixer to medium speed for whipping.
804 
8055. Don’t Overwhip! Properly whipped cream should double in volume and form soft peaks.
806 
807As you whip, fat particles begin to cluster. If you continue whipping to stiff peaks, more fat particles will bond together, making your whipping cream thicker but without any increase in volume.
808 
809❕Completely overwhipped cream results in the fat separating from the liquid. In other words, you’ll have butter and the whey.
810 
811❌Heavy whipped cream with very stiff peaks will be difficult to incorporate into a mousse and may cause overmixing.
812 
813✅Correctly whipped cream will ensure stable volume and the ideal final texture in your mousse.
814Tempering Gelatin: A Step-by-Step Guide
815 
816You asked for it, and here it is 🔥 Be sure to save this post for easy reference 👌🏻
817 
818❕ When working with stabilizers, paying close attention to product temperatures, the quantities used, and the tempering process is crucial.
819Here’s a step-by-step guide to ensure success👇🏻
820 
8211. Soak the Gelatin
822✔️ For sheet gelatin, soak in ice water for 15 minutes.
823✔️ For powdered gelatin, soak in the liquid specified in the recipe for 5 minutes. If the recipe doesn’t specify the amount/type of liquid to use, use water in an amount six times the weight of the gelatin.
824 
8252. Dissolve the Gelatin
826☀️ For warm processes (e.g., Bavarian cream), add the soaked gelatin to the warm mixture, such as a Crème Anglaise.
827❄️ For cold processes (e.g., fruit mousse), reheat the gelatin in a steam bath or microwave until fully dissolved.
828 
8293. Incorporate Gelatin
830Add 10% of the mousse mixture to the dissolved gelatin.
8314. Heat the Mixture
832Heat to 38-40 °C (~100-105 °F).
8335. Combine with Mousse
834Gradually return the heated gelatin mixture to the mousse, mixing gently in smooth, circular motions.
835 
836❕Whether your mousse is stabilized with cocoa butter or gelatin, there are key indicators that the process was executed correctly👇🏻
837 
838✔️Your mousse should be:
839• Smooth
840• Free of lumps
841• Shiny
842• Elastic
843🌡These characteristics indicate that the cocoa butter was at the proper temperature and did not crystallize during mixing.
844Common Mistakes When Working with Mousses🥣
845 
846Mousses generally consist of three key components:
847 
848✔️ Base (such as crème anglaise, custard, ganache, cheese mixture, or fruit purée)
849✔️ Aerator (such as egg foam or whipped cream)
850✔️ Stabilizer (such as cocoa butter, gelatin, or agar).
851 
852Mistakes can happen within each component individually or across multiple components simultaneously.
853 
854The most frequent errors include:
855 
856▫️ Underwhipped or overwhipped aerators, such as creams and meringues.
857▫️ Incorrect temperature conditions when incorporating stabilizers, such as cocoa butter or gelatin.
858 
859✔️ For instance, chocolate mousse should be smooth, shiny, and elastic after the addition of chocolate. This texture indicates that the cocoa butter was at the correct temperature and did not crystallize during mixing.
860 
861✔️ When using stabilizers, it’s crucial to pay close attention to their temperature, quantity (as specified in the recipe), and the tempering process.
862 
863By the way, are you familiar with the correct way to temper gelatin for mousse?
864Sponge Cake Defects and How to Avoid Them
865❤️ Thank you for your comments and engagement. 
866This topic is highly relevant and requires a detailed examination of various nuances.
867 
868🤝As promised, I have identified the three most common defects and will tell you how to resolve them👇🏻:
8691. SPONGE CAKES COLLAPSE DURING BAKING OR COOLING:
870✔️The cakes may have been moved around before the structure was set (coagulation of proteins and gelatinization of starch). Avoid opening the oven or moving the cakes during the initial stages of baking.
871✔️Incorrect proportions—insufficient drying agents or too much liquid in the batter.
872✔️Excessive chemical leavening agent. Ensure all ingredients are accurately measured.
873 
8742. CAKE TOP CRACKS DURING BAKING:
875✔️Baking temperature is too high.
876✔️Too much flour or the flour is too strong.
877✔️Insufficient liquid ingredients.
878✔️Incorrect kneading technique.
879 
8803. UNEVEN FORM:
881✔️Incorrect batter kneading technique.
882✔️Uneven oven heating—try baking in Convection mode.
883✔️Deformed/misshapen baking rings.
884✔️Uneven oven grates—ensure the grates and baking trays are level.
885✔️Uneven distribution of batter—ensure the batter is evenly spread before baking.
886✔️Uneven distribution of baking powder—sift the baking powder with the dry ingredients.
887 
888🤝 In our Exquisite Cake course, we will thoroughly examine the correct batter-kneading techniques and discuss all potential defects of sponge cakes and how to avoid them.
889AVOID DOING THIS WHEN WORKING WITH FROSTING🍰
890 
891Most frostings and mousses are typically composed of three main components:
892✔️ Base (such as crème anglaise, custard, ganache, cheese or fruit puree)
893✔️ Aerators (egg foam, whipped cream)
894✔️ Stabilizers (cocoa butter, gelatin, or agar)
895 
896⚠️Errors can occur when working with these components individually or in combination.
897Here are some common mistakes to watch out for and some quick notes on how to correct them:
898 
899▫️INSUFFICIENT FAT CONTENT AND IMPROPERLY CHILLED CREAM
900Whipping cream should have a fat content of at least 30–33%, ideally 33–35%, and be cooled to approximately 4°C (~39°F).
901 
902▫️INCORRECT TEMPERATURE OF BUTTER FOR WHIPPING
903Butter that is either too cold or too warm can lead to improper whipping results. This is a frequent yet often overlooked mistake among students.
904 
905▫️UNDERWHIPPED OR OVERWHIPPED FROSTING
906It’s easy to overbeat frostings such as Chantilly cream, mascarpone-based frostings, and whipped ganaches, especially those made with dark chocolate.
907 
908▫️SEPARATED BUTTERCREAM
909This can occur for two reasons: differing temperatures of the ingredients or improper proportions of fat and water needed to achieve a perfect emulsion.
910Did you know that the ingredients in every cake recipe can be categorized into four main groups based on their functions?
911 
912The “perfect recipe” is achieved when these four categories are balanced.
913 
914Understanding the primary functions of these ingredients and their correct ratios not only allows you to modify recipes to suit your taste but also to evaluate and correct untested recipes.
915 
916Interested? Let’s dive into it.
917 
918✔️Tougheners or Stabilizers:
919These ingredients, including flour and eggs, provide a stable texture.
920 
921Insufficient structure formers result in a delicate sponge cake that crumbles in your hands. Have you experienced this? I certainly have.
922 
923✔️Softeners or Tenderizers:
924These ingredients, such as sugar, fat, and chemical leavening agents, ensure softness by shortening protein fibers.
925 
926A lack of softeners leads to a dense, hardened cake that is challenging to cut with a knife. Does this sound familiar?
927 
928✔️Moisturizers:
929Ingredients like water, milk, syrups, and eggs provide moisture.
930 
931Interestingly, some ingredients serve multiple functions. You didn’t think it was all so straightforward, did you?
932 
933If a cake is too dry, increasing the amount of moisturizers can help. However, be cautious, as most ingredients have multiple roles. For example, adding more eggs to a dry cake could result in an even drier and tougher texture.
934 
935✔️ Dryers:
936Ingredients such as flour, starch, and cocoa absorb moisture.
937 
938The idea of a well-balanced recipe formula lies in the proportionate amounts of stabilizers that will balance out the softeners and dryers that will balance out the moisturizers. For instance, increasing stabilizers requires a corresponding increase in softeners.
939Have been delving into the theory of flour for a while.
940 
941I have known about the power and importance of gluten (protein) for quite some time. But this time it’s a completely different level.
942 
943In nutshell. In addition to protein (~12%), the main part of flour is starch (~71%).
944 
945On which type of wheat for making flour is used (hard or soft kernel) will depend not only the quantity and quality of protein, the texture of the flour, but also the number of broken and damaged starch granules.
946 
947That is, the harder the wheat kernel, the more difficult it is to grind it into fine flour and the more starch granules will be damaged in the process.
948 
949And damaged starch granules absorb much more water than intact ones, which slows staling.
950 
951Damaged granules are also more susceptible than intact ones to breakdown down by amylase, which further slows staling.
952 
953In addition, as the amylase breaks down starches into sugars, yeast fermentation can increase.
954 
955That’s why flour for bread is milled from hard red wheat, which not only has a high protein content, but also many damaged starch granules.
956 
957Bread flours are typically used for pan breads, rolls, croissants and sweet yeast doughs.
958How to cover a cake with crumbs neatly?
959 
960We’ve repeatedly been asked this question in DM, so we decided to make a short video in which we would share the main tips of this simple technique:
961 
962☝️first, grind the dry crumbs in a food processor, wet — using hands, and then pass them through a large sieve so that the crumbs become fine and uniform;
963 
964☝️secondly, in order for crumbs to hold well on the sides of the cake, you should do this technique immediately after the final leveling. If using ganache, work especially quickly;
965 
966☝️thirdly, start applying crumbs from the very bottom, gradually moving up. Scoop up a large handful and gently press it to the surface. Repeat until the desired part of the cake is covered. After that, work with a small spatula — scoop up the crumbs from the cake board and pull it up with a light, careful movements. In this way, you will close the gaps and remove the excess, making the coating as uniform as possible;
967 
968☝️and, fourthly, work on a turntable and do not forget to put a large sheet of parchment paper next to it, so that cleaning the kitchen does not take half a day later.
969 
970That’s all! Nothing complicated, and the cake already looks festive.
9712 SECRET INGREDIENTS FOR THE PERFECT CARROT SPONGE
972 
973Traditionally, the carrot sponge is made solely with vegetable oil, yielding a soft and tender yet excessively crumbly layer that defies slicing — it simply falls apart when you pick it up.
974 
975Being a perfectionist at heart, I couldn't accept this and embarked on a mission to create a sponge cake that retained its structure flawlessly.
976 
977And the journey of experimentation began.
978 
979I tested a 50:50 blend of butter and vegetable oil, resulting in a delicious but overly dense sponge reminiscent of a tea cake. While the issue of density was resolved, a new challenge emerged — how to seamlessly marry density with tenderness.
980 
981Indeed, how?
982 
983Initially, my instinct was to reduce the butter content in the recipe. However, after a moment of contemplation, an alternative idea surfaced — to partially substitute the oil with a small quantity of kefir. And there it was! The outcome: a tender yet structurally resilient sponge layer!
984 
985In essence,
986 
987🥛 THE FIRST SECRET INGREDIENT 🥛
988 
989is kefir.
990 
991🥕 AND THE SECOND SECRET INGREDIENT 🥕
992 
993is carrot juice.
994 
995🔸 similar to kefir, carrot juice contributes a substantial liquid content, serving to bind the sponge together.
996🔸 + it functions as a potent natural colorant
997🔸 + it imparts a deep, rich taste to the sponge
998 
999The result is a carrot sponge that strikes a perfect balance — moderately tender, slightly dense, and incredibly vibrant and delectable!
10004 PRIMARY WAYS IN WHICH FAT CONTRIBUTES TO LEAVENING
1001 
10021️⃣ All plastic fats contain tiny air bubbles, and some fats (such as butter and margarine) contain droplets of water.
1003 
1004The real leavening agents are air and water (steam), while fat serves an auxiliary function by helping trap and preserve air within the dough.
1005 
10062️⃣ Loosening of puff pastry
1007 
1008Solid fat layers create cavities in the dough upon melting, subsequently filled by hot steam. As the steam expands due to heat, it pushes the layers of dough apart.
1009 
10103️⃣ During the whipping process, additional air bubbles get into the plastic fats.
1011 
1012The air bubbles are surrounded and protected by numerous tiny, hard fat crystals that prevent them from bursting.
1013 
10144️⃣ Emulsifiers have the ability to trap and retain large volumes of air, especially when using high-ratio shortenings.
1015 
1016For instance, during the whipping process of plastic shortening, air is trapped. With the help of an emulsifier, it is first retained in the fat and then in the dough in the form of very small, strong bubbles evenly distributed throughout the dough.

1020 
1021When aspiring to create a dessert worthy of being deemed a confectionery masterpiece, it is important to consider these subtleties and nuances. Thus, explore an assortment of flavor combinations featuring citrus fruits:
1022 
1023🟠 Orange — passion fruit, grapefruit, kumquat; star anise, thyme, mint; almonds, pistachios, pine nuts; Cointreau liqueur, Kirschwasser, Armagnac brandy; mascarpone, chocolate, coffee.

1032HOW TO MAKE PUFF PASTRY FOR PIES TENDER
1033 
1034Concluding the analysis of the second fundamental function of fats in baking — providing flakiness — I will now share another secret for you to take note of.
1035 
1036And this secret will concern puff pastry for pies.
1037 
1038The perfect dough for the pie base should possess both tenderness and flakiness simultaneously. How can we achieve such a seemingly contradictory texture?
1039 
1040To achieve flakiness, incorporate relatively large pieces of fat. To guarantee the dough’s tenderness, follow these tips:
1041 
1042👩🏻‍🍳 use weak, low gluten flour
1043 
1044👩🏻‍🍳 use a minimal amount of flour to dust the work surface during the kneading process
1045 
1046👩🏻‍🍳 if necessary, increase the amount of fat in the formula
1047 
1048👩🏻‍🍳 make sure to use a minimal amount of water
1049 
1050👩🏻‍🍳 do not overdo it with mixing the dough after adding water
1051 
1052👩🏻‍🍳 let the dough chill in the fridge for several hours or overnight to allow water to migrate freely in the dough
1053DETERMINING THE DONENESS OF HONEY CAKE LAYERS
1054 
1055Delicate and seemingly innocuous, honey cake layers demand attention in the baking process, much like any other type of cakes.
1056 
1057When preparing honey cake layers, there are two potential, yet undesirable mistakes:
1058 
1059▪️ Underbaking them
1060▪️ Overbaking them
1061 
1062Determining the doneness of your cakes is simple — just observe their appearance! Let’s explore cakes made from three types of honey dough as an illustration (for a refresher on honey dough types, refer to the post from April 23).
1063 
1064⚫️ CAKES FROM STIFF OR SOFT HONEY DOUGH
1065 
1066🔸 Properly baked
1067 
1068have a consistent golden or caramel color
1069 
1070▪️ for chocolate cakes, assess doneness based on the baking time of classic cakes, as their dark color makes visual cues challenging
1071 
1072🔸Underbaked
1073 
1074appear flat, resembling pancakes, indicating insufficient protein and starch denaturation, causing the cakes to collapse
1075 
1076🔸 Overbaked
1077 
1078result in excessive dryness, with the cake edges prone to crumbling
1079 
1080▪️ after soaking, a honey cake with overbaked layers remains moist and tender, while one with underbaked layers tends to be excessively moist and prone to sogginess
1081 
1082⚫️ CAKES FROM LIQUID HONEY DOUGH (VERTICAL LAYER HONEY CAKES)
1083 
1084🔸 Properly baked
1085 
1086display softness, tenderness, and high pliability, making them easy to roll into a roll
1087 
1088🔸Underbaked
1089 
1090reveal characteristic dark spots, indicating depressions formed due to incomplete protein denaturation
1091 
1092🔸 Overbaked
1093 
1094develop dry areas that crumble, making it challenging to roll such cakes into a roll
1095 
1096Always assess the doneness based on the first cake layer — allow the cake to cool completely, examine its appearance, and adjust the baking time or mode if necessary.
1097THE PHYSICS OF FLAKY PUFF PASTRY FORMATION DURING BAKING
1098 
1099Puff pastry consists of multiple, invisible to the eye, but intact layers of dough, separated from each other by equally thin and intact layers of pliable fat.
1100 
1101As the oven temperature rises, the layers of fat gradually melt. Simultaneously, water within the fat transforms into steam, expanding as the temperature ascends. This steam fills the voids left by the melting fat.
1102 
1103The melted fat acts as a barrier, delaying the escape of steam from the dough. Consequently, under the pressure of the expanding steam, the layers of dough puff up, creating the characteristic flakiness. Meanwhile, the lipids in the fat cause the dough to fry, resulting in a delectably golden-brown and crispy exterior.
1104 
1105Towards the end of the baking process, the dough stabilizes, yielding a final product with numerous delicate layers.
1106 
1107⚠️ The rising of baked goods occurs in the interstices between the layers of dough; however, the individual layers of dough themselves remain flat.
1108 
1109Hence, in areas where the layers of dough make contact, there is minimal flakiness and rising.
1110 
1111Unintentional occurrences of this issue may arise due to:
1112 
1113▪️ uneven rolling of fat between dough layers
1114▪️ tears in the dough
1115▪️ cuts in the dough
1116▪️ premature melting of fat before baking, causing the dough layers to fuse
1117 
1118For easier rolling of fat without damaging dough layers, it is crucial to use pliable fat of similar density to the dough.
1119 
1120If opting for butter, it should be slightly cooled (~16–18 °C) and, optionally, mixed with a small amount of flour.
1121 
1122To prevent tearing, the use of strong flour is recommended, with adequate resting time for the dough between lamination sessions.
1123 
1124Sometimes the dough is intentionally pierced, or small cuts are made in a random fashion to avoid excessive separation. Additionally, pressing along the edges of puff pastry can prevent it from complete separation into individual thin layers during baking.
