1160BLUE SPOTS BETWEEN HONEY CAKE LAYERS AND FILLING
1161 
1162On multiple occasions, concerned students have messaged me images of honey cakes exhibiting blue or bluish spots, accompanied by the question: "Marusya, is it safe to eat this? What's going on?"
1163 
1164❓ Can this cake be consumed?
1165✅ Absolutely
1166 
1167❓ What causes the appearance of blue spots?
1168❌ Blue spots indicate incomplete chemical reaction of baking soda in the cake layers
1169 
1170To be more specific, these blue spots don't develop within the cake layers themselves but rather at the junctions where the cake and its filling meet.
1171 
1172If baking soda fails to fully react, either during batter preparation or cake baking, the acid in berry fillings and/or sour cream frostings (lactic acid) reacts with the incompletely reacted baking soda in the cakes. Consequently, localized blue spots emerge.
1173 
1174❓ How to eliminate the risk of blue spots
1175 
1176Our primary objective is to ensure that most of the baking soda undergoes the reaction during the batter preparation, and only a small percentage is left for a swift and final reaction in the oven — higher temperatures speed up the process.
1177 
1178But to be on the safe side, after adding baking soda to the heated honey mixture, we can incorporate lemon juice or citric acid to monitor the reaction.
1179 
1180✅ If foaming occurs, albeit weakly, it indicates that the baking soda has already reacted adequately - > remove the mixture from the heat and add flour.
1181 
1182❌ If the mixture foams vigorously, signaling an incomplete reaction between baking soda and the acid in the honey, take corrective measures - > add a small amount of lemon juice or further heat the mixture until the reaction weakens.
1183 
1184❗️Do not allow the mixture to overheat; otherwise, during the addition of flour to the batter, it may partially cook, resulting in a 'rubbery' texture. Similarly, when incorporating eggs into an overheated mixture, there is a risk of protein coagulation. To prevent this, use a water bath and/or actively stir with a whisk while introducing eggs.
1185A FORMULA FOR SIMPLE YET STYLISH CAKE DECORATION
1186 
1187If the idea of slicing into your cake feels like committing a culinary crime, it signifies that what is in front of you is not just a dessert to satisfy sweet cravings, but rather a genuine masterpiece.
1188 
1189But how can you ensure that your cake captures the attention of even the most discerning aesthetes?
1190 
1191Today, I’m unveiling a formula for a simple yet sophisticated and striking cake decoration. It involves six key variables.
1192 
11931️⃣ Ensure that decorative elements mirror the essence of the cake. If the cake features violet frosting and blueberry-lemon confit, opt for violet flowers, lemon slices, and blueberries. For a dessert showcasing sour cherries and chocolate, use nappage-coated sour cherries and handmade chocolates, etc.
1194 
11952️⃣ Conceptualize an appealing shape; in my case, it’s a wreath.
1196 
11973️⃣ Sart the formation of the wreath with the largest decorative elements, such as a lemon slice.
1198 
11994️⃣ Arrange the largest decorative elements in a circular fashion, maintaining spacing between them and positioning them opposite each other.
1200 
12015️⃣ Fill any remaining gaps after placing the larger elements with smaller ones, like blueberries and violet flowers.
1202 
12036️⃣ The size and/or height of the decorative elements should correspond to the dimensions of your cake — the taller and wider the cake, the larger and/or taller the decorative elements should be.
1204 
1205📎 given that most of my cakes measure 14–16 cm in height, I arrange small decorative elements, such as blueberries and raspberries, in 2 or even 3 layers for added visual impact
1206 
1207📎 for convenience, you can initially sketch the placement of all decorative elements on paper before arranging them on the cake, using the drawing as a guide
1208THE MAIN RULE FOR ACHIEVING PERFECT PUFF PASTRY
1209 
1210or
1211 
1212FUNCTIONS OF FATS IN BAKING
1213part 1
1214 
1215Have you ever thought about why we add butter into cupcake batter? And why do we whip it?
1216 
1217One of the most important functions of fats in baking is to impart softness and tenderness to the baked goods. Today, I'll explain how this works.
1218 
1219Fats serve to coat and envelop the structure formers (gluten proteins, egg whites, and starch granules). They prevent their hydration and the development of structure. Essentially, fats act to shorten the gluten strands, thus preventing the formation of an extensive, interconnected gluten network.
1220 
1221In baking, this quality is often referred to as ‘shortness’, a term that emphasizes the desired tenderness in the final product.
1222 
1223Fat's impact on baked goods' softness and tenderness depends on:
1224 
1225▪️amount of fat
1226 
1227The higher the fat content, the softer and crumblier the product will be.
1228 
1229All-purpose shortening (confectionery fat) or 100% fat vegetable oils are much more effective at shortening gluten strands compared to, for example, 82% butter, which contains residual water.
1230 
1231▪️fat consistency
1232 
1233The softer and more liquid the fat, the better it softens the batter, resulting in crumblier and more tender baked goods.
1234 
1235Liquid fats, like vegetable oil, blend more readily with the batter, enveloping the flour particles. Soft whipped butter is more effective at softening than unwhipped butter.
1236 
1237▪️size of fat particles
1238 
1239The more thoroughly the fat is incorporated into the flour, the more tender and crumblier the product will be. This occurs as the fat particles become smaller and better cover the structure-forming particles.
1240 
1241▪️presence of emulsifiers
1242 
1243Like thorough dough mixing, emulsifiers assist in even fat distribution throughout baked goods and are highly effective at enveloping the structure-forming particles.
1244 
1245▪️leavening ability of fat or emulsifier
1246 
1247The greater the leavening ability of the fat, the more it contributes to softening. The rising of baked goods during baking stretches and thins the cell walls, weakening them.
1248 
1249Hence, vegetable oil, devoid of leavening properties, softens pie batter but yields denser cakes and tea cakes.
1250part 2
1251 
1252Exploring the intricacies of baking is a must for dedicated confectioners who value a thoughtful and precise approach in their culinary endeavors. Understanding the fundamentals of confectionery and the underlying physics of the baking process is essential to avoid unnecessary or incorrect actions in the kitchen.
1253 
1254Let’s begin examining the second function of fats in baking.
1255 
1256PROVIDING FLAKINESS
1257 
1258🖊 Flakiness is the ability of baked goods to form numerous thin, flat, and often crispy layers.
1259 
1260🖊 Puff pastry is a culinary product made from numerous thin layers of dough separated by layers of fat, achieved through meticulous folding and rolling. Some puff pastry types have over 80 layers.
1261 
1262VARIETIES OF PUFF PASTRY BY PREPARATION METHOD
1263 
1264🔸 classic
1265🔸 chopped
1266🔸 easy-make
1267 
1268In classic puff pastry, fat is repetitively folded and rolled together with the dough (individual puff pastries, croissants, Danish pastries).
1269 
1270In chopped – solid pieces of fat are chopped alongside flour until they resemble crumbs (pies).
1271 
1272In easy-make puff pastry, solid fat, often in a frozen state, is grated coarsely and then swiftly mixed with flour (Napoleon cakes, individual puff pastries).
1273 
1274Irrespective of how fat is incorporated into the dough, whether in pieces or as a single rolled-out layer, the key to forming a quality, flaky puff pastry lies in the subsequent processes of rolling and lamination.
1275 
1276Furthermore, to ensure top-notch puff pastry, the plasticity of the fat is of utmost importance. It is crucial that the consistency of the fat matches that of the dough to prevent it from breaking through the dough layer or being squeezed out.
1277 
1278To prevent the fat from melting entirely during rolling and to maintain its necessary plasticity, it is a common practice to work with the dough at a temperature not exceeding 18 °C.
1279part 3
1280 
1281The main rule for achieving perfect puff pastry is as follows:
1282 
1283☝️the later the fat melts in the oven, the better the flakiness☝️
1284 
1285In chopped pie dough and easy-make puff pastry, the pieces of fat must be well chilled and large in size to ensure that the layers of dough and fat do not mix together.
1286 
1287Hence, when working in a warm pastry shop, the fat should be thoroughly chilled before starting the puff pastry preparation (for easy-make puff pastry, consider freezing it) so that the fat does not completely melt and combine with the flour during the kneading process.
1288 
1289For the same reason, using a mixer is not recommended, as it quickly combines the flour and fat into a homogeneous mass, which diminishes the formation of flakiness during the baking process.
1290 
1291It is also essential to use ice-cold water when kneading puff pastry to prevent the butter in the dough from heating up and melting prematurely.
1292 
1293Similarly, it’s imperative to thoroughly chill the dough before rolling it out. The colder the dough and the lower the room temperature, the flakier the final product will be.
1294 
1295Notice how the flaky texture of the dough cannot be matched by its tenderness, a result of the careful combination of fat and flour.
1296 
1297In short, the ability of fat to provide flakiness to baked goods depends on:
1298 
1299▪️ fat hardness: the harder the fat and the higher its melting point, the greater the flakiness
1300 
1301▪️ fat pieces size: larger fat pieces in the dough lead to better flakiness
1302MULTI-LAYER CHEESECAKES – 3 RULES YOU SHOULD KNOW
1303 
1304 
1305Wouldn’t you agree that cheesecakes with multiple layers never fail to pique one’s interest and leave a lasting impression?
1306 
1307Today, we embark on a brief yet vital educational journey to explore the intricacies of working with these delightful layers.
1308 
1309To achieve that breathtaking multi-layer cheesecake, keep these straightforward rules in mind:
1310 
13111️⃣ when adding the second and subsequent cream cheese layers, use a pastry bag and apply the mixture in a spiral motion, starting from the center and gently progressing toward the edge, layer by layer, ensuring the cheesecake crust is generously filled
1312 
13132️⃣ add a thicker cream cheese layer as the base, and a thinner layer on top
1314 
13153️⃣ if you find that some of your cream cheese layers are too runny, place them in the fridge for several hours to allow them to set
1316HOW AND WHY TO DRAIN SOUR CREAM
1317 
1318#marusya_theory_tips
1319 
1320Dear friends, I’ve received numerous inquiries regarding drained sour cream and where to obtain it🤷‍♀️
1321 
1322Allow me to shed light on this for my beloved followers😘
1323 
1324❓What is drained sour cream
1325 
1326🖊 Drained sour cream is sour cream with artificially increased fat content, achieved by removing some of the moisture, specifically whey, during the draining process.
1327 
1328❓Why drain sour cream
1329 
1330To obtain a harmonious flavor and prolong the shelf life of honey cakes, I recommend using store-bought sour cream.
1331 
1332To get a reliably stable sour cream frosting that is both easy to work with and delightful to the palate, you should aim for sour cream with a fat content of approximately ~36–40%.
1333 
1334However, most store-bought sour creams typically have a maximum fat content of 30%. Consequently, to increase the fat content of sour cream, we must engage in the process of draining.
1335 
1336❓How to drain sour cream
1337 
1338▪️ To obtain drained sour cream, place a strainer over a bowl, ensuring there’s adequate space at the base of the bowl to collect any excess liquid.
1339▪️ Next, place a double layer of cheesecloth within the strainer, pour 30% sour cream into it, and allow the whey to drain for 6–8 hours in the fridge. During this process, 30% sour cream should lose approximately 18% of its whey, thereby increasing its fat content to approximately ~36%.
1340 
1341📎 if the highest fat content available to you is 20–25%, place the sour cream in the fridge to drain for an entire night or a minimum of 10–12 hours. The resulting frosting might not be as stable as with 30% sour cream, but is still suitable for cake assembly.
1342 
1343And now, the question that has piqued the interest of many.
1344 
1345❓Which sour cream brand do I prefer
1346 
1347▪️ President, and only President 🤍❤️
1348ALL ABOUT CAKE FILLINGS
1349I noticed your keen interest in the topic of fillings, so as promised, I’m going to provide you with an in-depth exploration of the 3 most popular fruit fillings: coulis, confit, and compote.
1350 
1351Distinguishing between these fillings might be tricky initially, but I’m here to clarify. Save or write it down for future reference.
1352 
1353🟡 COULIS
1354 
1355The term “coulis” comes from French, meaning “sauce” or “to rub”. Now, here’s an ⚠️IMPORTANT⚠️ point – coulis can serve a dual role. It can be an independent sauce poured over a finished dessert or function as a filling within a cake.
1356 
1357In the former case, coulis takes the form of a berry or fruit sauce made by blending or “rubbing” the fruits, sometimes thickened with pectin or starch. In the latter case, it is a jelly-like, dense, and stable filling, made from fruit or berry juice, sugar, and a thickening agent. No matter how it’s used, coulis consistently imparts a rich flavor derived from the base fruit or berry.
1358 
1359🟡CONFIT
1360 
1361The French term "confiture" is derived from "confire," meaning "cooking in sugar."
1362 
1363Confiture, or simply confit, is a filling made by simmering fruits or berries in their own juices along with sugar or sugar syrup. Typically, it features whole berries or fruit pieces, although puree can also be used. Its most distinctive characteristic, setting it apart from coulis, is its consistency – confit closely resembles jam, while coulis has a more jelly-like texture.
1364 
1365✒️ the process of boiling fruits or berries in their own juice with sugar or sugar syrup is also known as "candisation"
1366 
1367🟡 COMPOTE
1368 
1369Compote can be either a standalone dessert or a filling inside other desserts.
1370 
1371When enjoyed as a dessert, it consists of berries or fruit pieces simmered in a sugar syrup, sometimes infused with spices. As a filling, it involves cooking berries or fruit pieces with sugar and adding one of thickeners (starch, gelatin, pectin, or agar-agar). The resulting texture is notably dense, akin to jelly or marmalade.
1372Part 3
1373CARAMEL: DRY, FRUIT, CREAMY AND SALTY
1374 
1375Caramel desserts have always been popular in the world of sweets. Naturally, everyone fine-tunes their caramel intake based on their DNA-encoded cravings for sweetness, but no one can resist the allure of its deep, enveloping aroma!
1376 
1377This allure is particularly pronounced during the fall season when our taste buds gravitate towards warming flavors. So, on this splendid September day, let’s delve into the world of caramel. Let’s begin, as usual, with a definition.
1378 
1379🖊Caramel (Spanish "caramelo”, from the late Latin "cannamella," meaning "sugar cane") is a confectionery creation or an ingredient used in confections, formed by heating sugar or boiling a sugar solution with glucose or glucose-fructose syrup.
1380 
1381This caramel is often referred to as 🟠DRY.
1382 
1383👩🏻‍🍳for those who may have puzzled over the phrase "wait until the sugar has completely melted to form DRY caramel" in my recipes, it’s essential to clarify that ‘dry’ implies the absence of additives, consisting solely of sugar or a combination of sugar and syrup.
1384 
1385When you introduce hot cream and room-temperature butter into dry caramel, you transform it into 🟠CREAMY CARAMEL.
1386 
1387🖊room temperature butter is typically around 14–16 °C.
1388 
1389If you decide to add a pinch of salt to the mixture after incorporating the cream, 🟠SALTED CARAMEL emerges.
1390 
1391But what about incorporating fruit, you may wonder?
1392 
1393🟠FRUIT OR BERRY CARAMEL is the delightful result of replacing some or all of the cream with fruit or berry puree.
1394 
1395You can also experiment with additional ingredients such as chocolate or nut butter. In general, I’ve covered the main types of caramel, leaving the rest to your creative whims. Sometimes, gelatin is also introduced to caramel as a stabilizing agent.
1396 
1397That essentially sums up the core of caramel. Well, almost. I almost forgot to mention that this post also goes by another name:
1398 
1399part 4
1400 
1401Coulis, compote, caramel, confit... What other fillings can we explore for cakes? Naturally, curd comes to mind.
1402 
1403▪️DEFINITION
1404 
1405Curd is a filling that closely resembles custard and can also stand alone as a confectionery creation. It is made by cooking fruit or berry puree or juice with eggs and butter.
1406 
1407▪️MAIN INGREDIENTS
1408 
1409🔸egg yolks
1410🔸sugar
1411🔸fruit base
1412🔸starch
1413🔸butter
1414 
1415▪️COOKING PROCESS
1416 
1417Begin by beating sugar with egg yolks, then incorporate juice or fruit puree and starch. Boil the mixture until it thickens, then remove it from the heat and gently stir in the butter.
1418 
1419▪️CURD VS CUSTARD
1420 
1421In fact, curd is essentially custard with a unique twist — it is made using fruit or berry puree instead of milk or cream.
1422 
1423▪️HOW TO MAKE YOUR CURD SPECIAL
1424 
1425🔸add the zest of yuzu, orange, lime, lemon...
1426🔸pour in a splash of liqueur like Limoncello, Cointreau, Curaçao, or Crème de Pêche...
1427🔸incorporate some white, dark, or milk chocolate
1428🔸infuse it with a sprig of thyme, rosemary, or lemongrass
1429🔸enhance the flavor with real vanilla, cinnamon, turmeric, or other spices
1430 
1431▪️IMPORTANT POINTS REGARDING CURDS
1432 
1433🔸to simplify the cooking process, start by dissolving the starch in a few tablespoons of juice beforehand
1434🔸for the best results when making curds, opt for farm-fresh egg yolks
1435part 5
1436It’s been a while since we delved into the world of cake fillings. Inspired by yesterday’s photos of chocolate desserts, I’ve decided to continue our exploration, this time focusing on crémeux.
1437 
1438Shall we begin?
1439 
1440Crémeux is a timeless French dessert which consists of five key ingredients: milk, sugar, eggs, cream, and chocolate. Beyond its role as a standalone dessert, it shines as a filling for cakes, pies, or individual cakes.
1441 
1442Traditionally, crème anglaise serves as a base for crémeux, as you may recall from our earlier #marusya_about_crèmeanglaise series. Crème anglaise includes:
1443 
1444▪️milk / milk and cream / milk and fruit puree
1445▪️egg yolks
1446▪️sugar
1447 
1448With the addition of cream and chocolate, crème anglaise transforms into a luxurious cake filling.
1449 
1450However, confectionery artistry allows room for creativity in the composition of crémeux.
1451 
1452In fact, crème anglaise as the base for crémeux is not set in stone. Let’s consider its function in crémeux🤔
1453 
1454As is the case with any custard, during heat treatment, all the water is bound with the help of thickeners. In a classic crème anglaise, without the addition of flour or starch, the thickener is lecithin found in egg yolks.
1455 
1456The primary objective in making crémeux is to achieve a stable filling. However, crème anglaise isn’t the sole method for providing thickness and stability. Personally, I don’t use crème anglaise in my crémeux for cakes.
1457 
1458🟤 Firstly, chocolate itself acts as a natural thickener.
1459 
1460🟤 Secondly, other stabilizers and thickeners can be used, such as gelatin or pectin. My preference leans toward gelatin when making crémeux.
1461 
1462🟤 Thirdly, and most excitingly, one can give free rein to their imagination by
1463 
1464▪️adding nut or flower pastes into the crémeux
1465▪️sprinkling the crémeux with additions like nut pieces
1466▪️infusing the cream / milk with lavender or lemongrass...
1467part 6
1468 
1469#marusya_about_fillings
1470 
1471Coulis, confit, compote, caramel, curd, crémeux – what other options exist for cake fillings? Let’s explore some choices that don’t all start with the letter "c," shall we? 😂
1472 
1473Among these options, we have:
1474 
1475🔴 GANACHE
1476 
1477🖊 Ganache, borrowed from the French, is a delectable mixture made from chocolate (whether it’s white, milk, or dark) and a liquid component. Additionally, it can incorporate butter, sugar, and an array of aromatic enhancements. The liquid component in ganache can be sourced from:
1478 
1479▪️milk
1480▪️heavy cream
1481▪️fruit or berry juice
1482▪️fruit or berry puree
1483 
1484Fundamentally, ganache is an emulsion of water and fat. To obtain a uniform texture and prevent separation, the proportion of fat must align with that of water.
1485 
1486So, when working with ganache, it’s important to meticulously maintain the balance between liquid ingredients and chocolate while using chocolate with the exact percentage of cocoa butter indicated in the recipe!
1487 
1488For comprehensive insights into ganache, including the precise ratios of liquids, chocolate, butter, and sugar, along with numerous finer details, you can delve deeper using the hashtag #marusya_about_ganache.
1489 
1490Another option for cake fillings is:
1491 
1492🔴 MOUSSE
1493 
1494🖊 Mousse (derived from the French term for "foam") is a delightful dessert or cake filling composed of:
1495 
1496▪️ a flavor base (be it fruit or berry juice, puree, grape wine, chocolate, coffee, cocoa, etc.)
1497▪️ and auxiliary components that facilitate frothing (like heavy cream and egg whites, etc.) and maintain the foamy consistency of the mousse (like gelatin or agar-agar).
1498FORMING THE PERFECT CHEESECAKE CRUST: STEP-BY-STEP GUIDE
1499 
1500Explore the beginning here #marusya_cheesecake_crust
1501 
1502If you recall my previous instructions on shaping the sides of the cheesecake crust, it’s now time to learn how to properly create the bottom and give the crust its ideal shape using a bent tablespoon.
1503 
1504Let’s get started!🚀
1505 
1506⭕️ add the remaining shortbread crumbs to those that became detached during the shaping of the sides
1507⭕️ bend a tablespoon at an obtuse angle that is comfortable for your work
1508📍a soft metal tablespoon is the best choice for forming a crust, as it easily bends at any angle
1509 
1510⭕️ using the bent tablespoon, evenly distribute the crumbs over the entire bottom and compact them tightly
1511⭕️ when compacting the crumbs along the edges of the bottom, gently press the tip of the spoon into the side, creating a small depression
1512⭕️ use the back of a spoon to compact the lower part of the side, running it around the cake ring. At this point, the tip of the spoon should slightly press the crust, forming a beautiful 90° angle
1513⭕️ compact any crumbs that have crumbled as a result of pressing the bottom
1514📍 if you notice that the crust’s side is thicker at the base, you can carefully scrape off the excess with a spoon
1515 
1516⭕️ compact the central part of the crust’s side with the middle part of the back of a spoon, running it along the cake ring
1517📍at the same time, the sides of the crust will rise slightly; this is normal
1518 
1519⭕️ compact the top part of the crust’s side with four fingers, while not forgetting to constantly press the top with the thumb of the other hand
1520 
1521The perfect cheesecake crust is now finished!
1522 
1523And here are insider tips exclusively for you:
1524 
1525💎 cheesecake crust thickness: 0.7–0.8 mm
1526💎 approximately 57% of shortbread crumbs are used for the sides, while 43% are used for the bottom (I use 240 g and 180 g)

1531How thick should an ideal base be? Thin, just to keep the cheese mass inside, or with sides 1 centimeter thick so that there is something to crunch to your heart's content?
1532⠀
1533Do you like the crust with sides or do the bare cheese sides look more appealing to you?
1534 
1535Do you top the cheesecake with fresh berries and berry sauce or salted caramel and nuts?
1536⠀
1537Share your preferences in the comments, please. Let’s see what a perfect cheesecake for the majority of us looks like.
1538⠀
1539I'll start.
1540⠀
1541I used to like a thin, perfectly flat base and a dense cheesecake mass, but over time I changed my mind.
1542⠀
1543Now I am an ardent fan of very delicate creamy filling and thick, crumbly, slightly sloppy sides with pieces of toasted crunchy nuts.
1544⠀
1545And a perfect complement to a cheesecake will 100% depend on its flavor.
1546⠀

1548HOW TO KNOW WHEN THE CUSTARD IS READY
1549 
1550#marusya_theory_tips
1551 
1552The custard is ready once it has boiled and reached 82°C. Is it so? Yes. Can we rely on this alone? Not at all.
1553 
1554Why?
1555 
1556▪️Firstly, the custard’s boiling point can vary between 82 to 85 °C based on the recipe you follow
1557 
1558▪️Secondly, measuring the temperature of boiling custard is challenging for the following reasons:
1559 
1560a) to ensure even cooking, it must be stirred continuously, making it highly inconvenient to simultaneously measure the temperature
1561 
1562b) the presence of air bubbles during boiling can interfere with accurate thermometer readings
1563 
1564▪️Thirdly, it’s easy to miss the initial boiling point of custard
1565 
1566When custard is cooked correctly (over very low heat with constant stirring), you may not notice it reaching the boiling point, as bubbles typically form after some time.
1567 
1568⁉️ So, how do you determine when the custard is ready?
1569 
1570I prioritize the thickening process. The custard should reach a complete and uniform thickness with no liquid or thicker parts remaining – it should thicken evenly throughout.
1571 
1572After achieving this complete thickness, I continue to stir constantly and let it boil for an additional one to two minutes, adjusting the duration based on the quantity of custard, to ensure there are no uncooked areas remaining.
1573ORMING THE PERFECT CHEESECAKE CRUST: STEP-BY-STEP GUIDE
1574 
1575After more than six months of experimentation and practice, I’ve developed my unique method for forming the ideal cheesecake crust.
1576 
1577"Why did you have to reinvent the wheel?" you ask. Well, because few people, besides me, were concerned about achieving a perfectly flat side and a beautiful right angle between the bottom and the side of the crust.
1578 
1579The way a pastry looks affects our taste perception. Thus, a flawless crust is a crucial element of the ultimate cheesecake. So how can it be made?
1580 
1581After six months of experiments, I’ve learned to form the perfect crust in just 5 minutes. Want me to teach you?
1582 
1583Let’s get started.
1584 
1585⭕️ take a small handful of shortcrust crumbs in your palm
1586⭕️ using your four fingers, firmly press the shortcrust crumbs against the walls of a greased cake ring, ensuring even distribution over the entire height
1587📍press the shortcrust crumbs multiple times, not just once; this involves continuous, repetitive movements
1588 
1589⭕️ simultaneously, run your thumb from the other hand around the top to create a level surface
1590⭕️ remove any crumbs that become detached during shaping and place them closer to the center of the crust’s bottom
1591⭕️ use the tips of your four fingers to press the crumbs at the very base of the wall. Press them along the entire height of the ring until the layer is uniformly thick both above and below. Maintain consistent pressure on the top with your thumb from the other hand
1592⭕️ grab the next handful of crumbs and repeat all previous steps
1593📍when forming the side, you should develop the ability to gauge its thickness with your fingers – apply a bit more pressure to the crumbs where the side is thicker, distributing them evenly around the ring’s circumference
1594 
1595In this manner, shape the sides around the entire circumference of the ring.
1596WHEN THE CHEESECAKE IS READY: EGG WHITE COAGULATION
1597part 1
1598 
1599#marusya_about_coagulation
1600 
1601Through numerous experiments and master classes, I stumbled upon a fascinating observation – cheesecakes with different fillings require varying baking times.
1602 
1603When baking 10 cheesecakes simultaneously in a convection oven and removing them all at once, there’s always an overcooked filling, another that’s perfectly done, and there are a few cheesecakes with runny centers that could have spent more time in the oven.
1604 
1605Now, one might wonder, why does this occur? After all, the cheesecakes are nearly the same in size, and the amount of cream cheese mixture in each is also similar. So, shouldn't the doneness solely depend on the product’s size, baking time, and temperature? Is there something else at play here?
1606 
1607I pondered over this question for a long time until I delved deep into the study of egg, one of the primary ingredients in confectionery.
1608 
1609Intrigued? Let's explore it together.
1610 
1611Cheesecake can be seen as an adaptation of the basic custard recipe, incorporating eggs as both a thickener and gelling agent.
1612 
1613The thickening process takes place as the temperature of the cream cheese mixture rises and the egg whites coagulate.
1614 
1615As the eggs are subjected to heat, the protein structure starts to denature and unfold. These unfolded protein molecules then interact and connect with one another, creating a strong yet highly flexible network.
1616 
1617This network effectively binds water and other liquids within the cheesecake mixture, ultimately signaling that the product is properly cooked.
1618 
1619Now, let's examine the primary factors that influence the rate of egg protein coagulation, along with the approximate temperatures at which this process takes place.
1620 
1621Would you like to read a continuation? Let me know in the comments.
1622Part 2
1623Today we continue the article on egg white coagulation.
1624 
1625Discover the beginning at #marusya_about_coagulation
1626 
1627The rate of protein coagulation, and consequently, the cooking time of cheesecake, creme brulee, or custard, depends on:
1628 
1629▪️quantity or ratio of eggs
1630 
1631Normally, egg proteins coagulate at 70 °C; however, if the eggs are mixed with milk, water, or other ingredients, the coagulation temperature rises.
1632 
1633As an illustration, the proteins in custard coagulate at 82–85 °C. The addition of milk, cream, or sugar makes it more challenging for them to collide and form bonds.
1634 
1635So, the greater the amount of eggs in the mixture, the quicker it will cook.
1636 
1637▪️cooking temperatures
1638 
1639Egg coagulation is not an instantaneous process; it requires some time to occur. The higher the temperature, the faster the mixture cooks, and therefore, the proteins fully coagulate in less time.
1640 
1641Nevertheless, when eggs coagulate too quickly, the proteins may not have sufficient time to unfold, leading to an issue where the mixture might not fully thicken.
1642 
1643For instance, if custard is cooked over high heat, not only does it risk curdling and burning, but there's also a likelihood that it won't reach the desired thickness.
1644 
1645For enhanced thickening, cook it on low heat while continuously stirring. This is why water baths are so good for making custard, pudding, and cheesecakes.
1646 
1647Water baths maintain temperatures below the boiling point (at 82–88 °C), even when the oven temperature is set at 165 °C or higher. With a water bath, the coagulation process slows down, and the baking process becomes more uniform, preventing the outer layer of the cheesecake from becoming overly tight, rubbery, or curdled while ensuring proper baking on the inside.
1648 
1649▪️part of the egg used
1650 
1651Egg yolks coagulate at higher temperatures (65–70 °C) compared to egg whites (60–65 °C), resulting in a lower risk of protein hypercoagulation (folding) when using only yolks.
1652 
1653It's because the proteins in the yolks are lipoproteins bound to fats and emulsifiers, which complicates the protein binding process.
1654part 3
1655 
1656#marusya_about_coagulation
1657 
1658In the previous parts, we’ve already discussed that the rate of protein coagulation depends on the quantity or ratio of eggs, the cooking temperatures, and the part of the egg used.
1659 
1660However, these are not the only factors.
1661 
1662The rate of coagulation, and consequently the cooking time of the cheesecake, will also be influenced by:
1663 
1664▪️ amount of sugar
1665 
1666Apart from its role in diluting protein molecules, sugar also slows down the coagulation of the egg whites, preventing the proteins from unfolding.
1667 
1668This means that sugar aids in preventing the curdling of egg whites. Hence, the egg filling in quiche, which lacks added sugar, tends to curdle after baking and release liquid.
1669 
1670Consequently, it's not surprising that sugar is considered a tenderizing agent in baking. By slowing down coagulation, sugar also retards the formation of the egg structure, as well as the networks of gluten and starch.
1671 
1672When a sufficient amount of sugar is present, it can stop coagulation entirely, potentially leading to the product appearing undercooked despite prolonged cooking.
1673 
1674▪️amounts of lipids (fats)
1675 
1676Similar to sugar, lipids (fats, oils, and emulsifiers) slow down the coagulation process of proteins through direct interaction with them.
1677 
1678This explains why custards made with cream and yolks possess a silkier, softer, and creamier texture compared to those prepared with milk and whole eggs.
1679 
1680▪️presence and amount of acid
1681 
1682Acids accelerate coagulation by reducing the temperature required for this process.
1683 
1684📎 Acids in cheesecake or custard can stem from sources such as lemon juice, various fruits, raisins, or fermented dairy products.
1685 
1686This explains why fruit and berry cheesecakes bake more quickly than their counterparts.
1687 
1688There are 3 additional factors that influence the rate of protein coagulation. Are you familiar with them?
1689part 4, the last one
1690 
1691#marusya_about_coagulation
1692 
1693So, here are the last three 🤫secret factors🤫 that affect the cooking time of cheesecake:
1694 
1695▪️presence and quantity of starch
1696 
1697Starch increases the temperature of protein coagulation, interfering with the process.
1698 
1699As an example, let’s compare the process of making custard sauce (without starch) and custard (with starch or flour).
1700 
1701The custard must be brought to boiling temperature (~85 °C) and boiled for 2 minutes or more. The custard sauce will curdle before it reaches boiling point.
1702 
1703Always make sure that custard containing starch is cooked properly. If it is not cooked long enough, not only will the starch not jellify (gelatinize) properly, but the small amount of amylase that is present in egg yolks may not be inactivated.
1704 
1705🖊Amylase is an enzyme that hydrolyzes starch to produce simple sugars. Unless completely deactivated, amylase will easily liquefy the custard overnight.
1706 
1707▪️amount of salt
1708 
1709Salt in hard water and dairy products, or adding a small amount of table salt, speeds up and enhances the coagulation of proteins in the egg.
1710 
1711Milk proteins can also interact with egg proteins to enhance gel formation. If you make the custard with water instead of milk or cream, it will turn out very liquid, barely thickened.
1712 
1713▪️presence of proteolytic enzymes
1714 
1715Enzymes break down egg proteins in the same way they break down the proteins in gelatin.
1716 
1717If you add raw pineapple with active protease to custard or cheesecake, it will not thicken. If you first boil the pineapple, thereby inactivating the protease, the intact egg proteins in the custard will coagulate and it will thicken.
1718THE RELEVANCE OF FAT CONTENT IN DAIRY PRODUCTS FOR FROSTING
1719 
1720If you are following my page or if it has appeared as a suggestion on your feed, chances are you’ve prepared cake frosting at least once in your life.
1721 
1722Whether you used my recipe or that of another professional chef, you likely observed that every dairy product — like cream cheese, heavy cream, or sour cream — comes with its designated fat content.
1723 
1724This isn’t just an arbitrary detail. The fat content of dairy products used for making frosting, which serves as an interlayer, plays a pivotal role. It significantly influences:
1725 
1726🥛 frosting stability — a higher fat content leads to greater stability in the frosting, making it easier to work with. Such frosting is particularly suitable for assembling tall and multi-tiered cakes
1727 
1728🥛permissible frosting quantity — with increased fat content, more frosting can be applied between cake layers
1729 
1730🥛 cake texture — incorporating high-fat dairy products results in well-soaked yet not overly wet cakes, achieving a balance of moderate tenderness and moisture
1731 
1732🥛 palatability — higher fat content yields a richer taste in the final product, enhancing its overall flavor profile
1733 
1734🥛 expiration dates — increased fat content extends the shelf life of the final product
1735 
1736In general, as you can see, the frosting’s fat content correlates with expanded creative possibilities and heightened pleasure for our taste buds!;)
1737 
1738You might have come across this information from me before, but in case you haven’t, I’ll reiterate:
1739 
1740🖋 FAT MOLECULES ARE CARRIERS OF TASTE TO OUR TASTE RECEPTORS
1741 
1742CULTURED BUTTER IN NAPOLEON CAKE LAYERS
1743 
1744#marusya_theory_tips
1745 
1746Lately, I’ve noticed a growing number of inquiries regarding cultured butter. Students are curious about its nature and its role in making the layers of a Napoleon cake.
1747 
1748So, let me tell you.
1749Be all ears and save the post to bookmarks.
1750 
1751The two most common butter types are sweet cream and cultured butter. So, what is the difference between them?
1752 
1753🖊Sweet cream butter is made from pasteurized cream. It has a gentle milky flavor and aroma.
1754 
1755🖊Cultured butter, on the other hand, is made from pasteurized fermented cream. It involves the use of pure cultures of lactic acid bacteria during fermentation. This variety of butter has a tangier, more complex flavor and aroma.
1756 
1757❓How to identify the type of butter
1758 
1759In cases where the packaging doesn’t specify the type of butter you have, you can distinguish between them by examining their composition. Cultured butter can be readily identified by the presence of lactic acid bacteria, whereas sweet cream butter consists solely of cream.
1760 
1761And now, let’s move on to the most interesting aspect. Why is cultured butter preferred for making flaky puff pastry for Napoleon cake? Is it due to its taste? Not entirely. We should focus on a seemingly minor yet important distinction in physicochemical properties.
1762 
1763And that is the
1764 
1765❗️MELTING POINT❗️
1766 
1767Sweet cream butter – 32-33 °C
1768Cultured butter – 34-35 °C
1769 
1770When making layers for the Napoleon cake, our aim is to maintain the butter’s low temperature for as long as possible, preventing it from melting due to ambient conditions and the warmth of our hands while grating it during dough kneading. This approach is crucial for achieving the ultra-thin and flaky puff pastry.
1771 
1772Here, a mere difference of a few degrees plays a significant role, particularly when working in a warm cooking environment.
1773 
1774📎 before grating cultured butter, I place it in the freezer overnight, and while grating, I consistently coat it with flour
1775 
1776📎 while it’s possible to make puff pastry using sweet cream butter, the resulting pastry will be less flaky
1777INTERCHANGEABILITY OF VARIETIES OF FLOUR
1778 
1779part 2
1780 
1781#marusya_60shades_offlour
1782 
1783Today we’ll analyze the seed flour.
1784 
1785🌿 FLAX FLOUR
1786 
1787Absorbency – High
1788Gluten-free – Yes
1789Interchangeability —>
1790 
1791You can substitute 10–25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.
1792 
1793❇️ Since flax flour absorbs water significantly, you'll need to increase the quantity of liquid ingredients in your recipe. Flax flour boasts a distinct and bold nutty flavor profile, making it advisable to start with 10%. From there, you can fine-tune the taste according to your preference.
1794 
1795🌿 SESAME FLOUR
1796 
1797Absorbency – High
1798Gluten-free – Yes
1799Interchangeability —>
1800 
1801Similar to flax flour.
1802 
1803❇️ Because sesame flour contains a substantial amount of fat, its texture is notably denser compared to that of all-purpose flour. Excessive use of sesame flour can result in an overly “heavy” product. Begin with a modest quantity, around 10%, and make adjustments as needed.
1804 
1805🌿 SUNFLOWER FLOUR
1806 
1807Absorbency – Medium
1808Gluten-free – Yes
1809Interchangeability —>
1810 
1811Replace no more than 25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.
1812 
1813❇️ This flour can fully replace all-purpose flour (100%) for gluten-free baking, such as bread, cookies, or muffins. In this case, one should use additional binding agents like eggs or xanthan gum to maintain the structure of tha pastry.
1814 
1815🌿 AMARANTH FLOUR
1816 
1817Absorbency – Above medium
1818Gluten-free – Yes
1819Interchangeability —>
1820 
1821Replace 25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour + take into account the absorbency and add a little more liquid.
1822 
1823🌿 QUINOA FLOUR
1824 
1825Absorbency – High
1826Gluten-free – Yes
1827Interchangeability —>
1828 
1829Replace from 25 to 50% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.
1830 
1831❇️ Quinoa flour possesses higher absorbency than all-purpose flour, potentially requiring additional liquid.
1832 
1833🌿 HEMP FLOUR
1834 
1835Absorbency – High
1836Gluten-free – Yes
1837Interchangeability —>
1838 
1839Replace up to 25% of the total weight of all-purpose flour.
1840 
1841❇️ Hemp flour is made by grinding hemp plant seeds and should not be confused with CBD (cannabidiol), which is extracted from the plant’s flowers, leaves, and stems.
1842INTERCHANGEABILITY OF VARIETIES OF FLOUR

1846🌿 ALMOND FLOUR
1847 
1848Absorbency — medium
1849Gluten-free — yes
1850Interchangeability —>
1851 
1852Almond flour can be a suitable replacement for all-purpose flour in various recipes, such as cakes, cookies, bread, and more. However, it is essential to ensure that you replace no more than 20-30% of the total mass of all-purpose flour.
1853 
1854❕When working with any nut flour, you should consider two key points:
1855 
1856🔺due to the high fat content in nuts, pastries made with nut flour tend to be greasier than those made with regular flour
1857🔺 nut flour, like other gluten-free flours, lacks the same binding properties as all-purpose flour —> you may need to use more eggs or other binders to hold your baked goods together

1893THE PERFECT CHEESECAKE — HOW TO CHOOSE A CAKE RING
1894 
1895As perfectionists, we understand the importance of every detail in making a flawless cheesecake, even seemingly insignificant factors like the cake ring.
1896 
1897Indeed, the selection of the ring holds a crucial role in achieving the ultimate perfection in baking a cheesecake. So, what key aspects demand your attention when opting for the ideal cake ring?
1898 
1899DIAMETER TO HEIGHT RATIO
1900 
1901If you’ve made a cheesecake at least once according to my recipe, then you know that I use a ring d=18 cm, h=6 cm. This specific size is not a coincidence; it has been carefully chosen to create a cheesecake that appears visually indulgent, evoking sincere delight even from the most discerning aesthetes.
1902 
1903An almost identical cheesecake prepared in a ring d=20 cm, h=6 cm, won't produce the same effect, as scrutinized by genuine perfectionists🧐
1904 
1905THICKNESS
1906 
1907min = 1 mm
1908 
1909Working with a thinner ring can be less convenient since obtaining the perfect cheesecake crust requires firm pressure of the crumble against the ring's walls. However, a thin ring tends to bend slightly, compromising the integrity of the crust, and you have to start the pressing process anew to achieve a visually appealing outcome.
1910 
1911Even the slightest difference of tenths of a millimeter can impact the thickness, so be mindful that when applying gentle pressure to the ring from both sides with your hands, it shouldn’t be compressed.
1912 
1913JOINT (WELD)
1914 
1915It should be well polished. Even if the rings are entirely identical and from the same brand, their quality can vary: one may have a flawless joint, while the other might exhibit a noticeable gap. This gap can leave an unattractive streak on the cheesecake, which is particularly distressing for us perfectionists😁
1916 
1917I recommend selecting cake rings from pastry shops rather than ordering them online. Alternatively, opt for brands with a proven track record of exceptional performance to ensure top-notch quality.
1918WHY SHOULD CREAM BE COOLED BEFORE WHIPPING?
1919 
1920#marusya_theory_tips
1921 
1922Whipped cream is an essential ingredient in most desserts. Cream with a fat content of at least 33% is considered ideal for whipping. Have you ever wondered why?🤔
1923 
1924The fats in the cream crystallize around the air bubbles that form during whipping. If the cream is low-fat, there will be too few crystals to hold air bubbles, and the cream won’t whip.
1925 
1926However, even when whipping heavy cream, instead of a stable lush foam and beautiful stable peaks, there is a risk of getting either an unstable fluid or split mass.
1927 
1928The fact is that one of the most important factors affecting the quality of whipping cream is temperature: the cream must be cooled before whipping. In short, the crystallization of fats occurs only in a cold environment – frosting made of insufficiently cold cream will deflate.
1929 
1930To understand why this happens, you need to delve into the physics and chemistry of the process.
1931 
1932📎 Cream is stabilized by milk fat, which is presented in the form of fat grains – the so-called globules, surrounded by a protective membrane. In the process of whipping cold cream, the whisk begins to destroy the integrity of the membranes that surround the globule, and partially exposes the fat component. Globules with their bare parts begin to be attracted either to each other or to air, thus forming a fat network inside which air bubbles are sealed.
1933 
1934🖇This property of the globules allows the cream to expand and turn into a light, airy mass. At higher temperatures, fat grains melt, become liquid, and repel air, so warm cream cannot whip properly, and it takes longer to reach maximum volume.
1935 
1936❌ But, the longer the cream is whipped, the more the protective membrane is destroyed. As a result, bare fat particles begin to strive only towards themselves – they displace air, clump into a dense mass, repel water, which results in the cream with a grainy texture.
1937CHOCOLATE SUGAR BLOOM
1938 
1939or
1940 
1941EVERYTHING YOU NEED TO KNOW ON HOW TO STORE CHOCOLATE
1942Part 2
1943 
1944#marusya_chocolate_storage
1945 
1946We began Part 1 of this post series by stating that chocolate is ideally stored at temperatures between 15-21°C to prevent fat bloom. Now, let’s delve into what exactly 'fat bloom' is and what causes it.
1947 
1948🖊 Fat bloom is when the unstable solid forms of cocoa butter transform into a stable yet uneven crystal structure on the surface of chocolate. Put simply, cocoa butter crystals separate from the other components and migrate to the chocolate’s surface, solidifying and creating greyish or whitish streaks.
1949 
1950This blooming happens for a number of reasons:
1951 
1952🔺the chocolate wasn’t tempered or was tempered incorrectly
1953 
1954⤴️read more about the secrets of chocolate tempering using the hashtag #marusya_about_chocolate
1955 
1956🔺 the recommended temperature range for storing chocolate (15-21 °C) wasn’t followed
1957🔺during storage or transportation, the chocolate was exposed to significant temperature fluctuations
1958🔺cocoa butter substitutes, such as coconut oil, palm oil, or other fats, were added to the chocolate
1959 
1960📍 for chocolate products
1961🔺 when molding (pouring chocolate into molds), the molds were either too cold or too hot
1962🔺 the molding process took too long and the chocolate's working temperature dropped below the allowable range
1963🔺 working with chocolate under conditions involving heat, such as using a surface that is either too cold or too hot for packaging, can result in localized blooming
1964🔺 the chocolate was placed in a storage area that was too cold before it had completely solidified (usually takes 24-48 hours for complete solidification)
1965 
1966📎 photo 4 in the carousel shows an example of chocolate fat bloom
1967 
1968Generally, chocolate affected by fat bloom remains edible, but it loses its appealing gloss and crispness, and quickly melts upon touch.
1969part 3
1970 
1971#marusya_chocolate_storage
1972 
1973In the previous part we discovered that adhering to a temperature range of 15-21 °C is a key guideline for chocolate storage in order to prevent fat bloom.
1974 
1975The second fundamental principle is to maintain the humidity level below 70% to avoid sugar bloom.
1976 
1977🖊Sugar bloom occurs when sugar in the chocolate interacts with the moisture in the surrounding environment. This leads to the formation of a white, ‘dusty’ layer that appears rough or granular in texture.
1978 
1979📎 photo 9 in the carousel showcases sugar bloom, allowing you to identify the issue if it arises
1980 
1981And now, the central question arises:
1982 
1983HOW MOISTURE COMES IN CONTACT WITH CHOCOLATE?
1984 
1985part 1
1986 
1987#marusya_chocolate_storage
1988 
1989There are a lot of controversies and rumors regarding the best way to store chocolate. This means that this topic is interesting and important. Let's sort things out!
1990 
1991Basically, the key to long-term chocolate storage comes down to just two things: temperature and humidity. Chocolate is best stored at cool ambient temperatures (15-21°C) to prevent fat bloom or melting, and in a dry place (70% humidity or below) to keep it from interacting with water and developing sugar bloom or seizing.
1992 
1993But let's dig deeper and figure out all the subtleties and nuances, as we’re used to make things clear, and hope you’ve already managed to fall in love with that too🤎
1994 
1995So, here’s the first question that we will consider:
1996 
1997🟤 HOW LONG DOES CHOCOLATE LAST?
1998 
1999Usually, chocolate has an expiration date. But it only serves as a general guideline set by manufacturers as a best before date, until which they can guarantee the best taste and texture of chocolate.
2000 
2001In fact, chocolate can be stored much longer than the use-by date indicated on the package:
2002 
2003🍫 Dark chocolate – can maintain its quality 2 years from the manufacturing date in proper ambient storage conditions, and up to 4 years refrigerated with some degradation.
2004🍫 White chocolate – can maintain its quality up to 1 year from its manufacturing date in proper ambient storage conditions, and up to 2 years refrigerated with some degradation.
2005🍫 Milk chocolate – the same as with white chocolate.
2006🟤 Firstly, chocolate has the ability to absorb moisture!
2007 
2008Dark chocolate starts absorbing moisture when the humidity level exceeds 82%, whereas milk chocolate is susceptible to moisture absorption above 78%. Thus, to preserve the delightful appearance of chocolate, it is advisable to maintain an environmental humidity level of 70% or below.
2009 
2010🟤 Secondly, condensation may develop on the chocolate.
2011 
2012This can occur due to two factors:
2013 
20141. Low storage temperature, such as when chocolate is stored in the fridge.
2015 
20162. Sudden temperature fluctuations exceeding 10°C, such as when chocolate is transitioned from a cold place to a warmer one.
2017 
2018The reasons for moisture coming in contact with chocolate can differ, but the outcome remains consistent: the sugar within the chocolate dissolves in the water and migrates to the surface. As the moisture evaporates, tiny white crystals become visible, ultimately forming the distinctive "dusty" surface.
2019 
2020So let's summarize. In order to prevent sugar bloom in chocolate, we should:
2021 
2022▪️ store chocolate in a place with a consistently stable temperature
2023▪️ avoid rapid temperature fluctuations of more than 10 °C within a short span of time
2024▪️ prevent any liquid from coming into contact with the chocolate

2117As promised, today we’ll analyze 5 most popular types of flour according to your answers:
2118 
21191. Rice flour

2124 
2125RICE FLOUR
2126 
2127Obtained by grinding white rice into a fine powder. Unlike the all-purpose flour, it doesn’t contain protein (gluten). Perfect for baking gluten-free cakes, biscuits and bread, and for making noodles. Often used as a thickener for sauces.
2128 
2129COCONUT FLOUR
2130 
2131Made by grinding sun-dried / oven-dried / or dehydrated coconut pulp into a powder. It’s a gluten-free flour with a pleasant, slightly sweet flavor. Most often used for sweet pastries, cakes.
2132 
2133Contains 4 times more fiber than the all-purpose flour —> absorbs more moisture —> requires more liquid ingredients —> more moist and dense baked goods.
2134 
2135BUCKWHEAT FLOUR
2136 
2137Made by grinding buckwheat seeds. Buckwheat is a pseudocereal that has nothing to do with wheat; it’s actually a fruit seed related to sorrel and rhubarb. Buckwheat flour is gluten-free. In recipes, it’s most often combined with regular flour and other types of gluten-free flours. Best used in yeast-free pastries.
2138 
2139OAT FLOUR
2140 
2141A gluten-free flour made by grinding whole oat groats (hulled grains) into a fine powder. It has a slightly denser texture than regular flour. Contains 3 times more fiber than the all-purpose flour. Oat flour makes delicious banana bread, muffins, and cookies.
2142 
2143TEFF FLOUR
2144 
2145One of the most unusual types of flour, obtained from ancient grain native to Ethiopia, the Eragrostis teff. Because the grains of this plant are very small, teff flour contains more bran and germ than other whole grains, making it a good source of amino acids, iron, fiber, and calcium. It has a unique taste with pleasant nutty notes. The aroma of waffles or crepes made with teff flour will make any breakfast unforgettable!
2146part 3
2147 
2148#marusya_60shades_offlour
2149 
2150In the previous parts, we introduced you to a wide range of varieties of flour, classifying it according to the raw materials (wheat, grain, bean, nut, vegetable and others), and analyzed in detail the varieties that were of most interest to you.
2151 
2152It doesn’t make much sense to write definitions for all 60 varieties (this information is publicly available on the Internet), but sharing a foolproof table, which shows whether each flour can be substituted for all-purpose flour (and vice versa) might come in handy.
2153 
2154Before proceeding to the table itself, let’s find out what are the general principles that lay the basis for this table⬇️
2155 
2156◼️ Any recipe is based on the interaction of ingredients, therefore, using the table, be sure to take into account the chemical processes occurring in each specific recipe.
2157 
2158◼️ Gluten-free flours work differently than the all-purpose flour, meaning baked goods don't rise as much as they do with gluten-containing flour. Therefore, even if the table says “100% interchangeable”, it is only true for recipes in which the dough is not supposed to proof.
2159 
2160◼️ The table also shows the absorption ability of flour (high / medium / low). Take this information into account to adjust the amount of liquid in the recipe in case you swap the type of flour
2161 
2162ℹ️ The 'low', 'medium', and 'high' value is based on all-purpose flour being medium absorbency and comparing it to that.
2163 
2164The liquid absorbency estimate is based on an amalgamation of the water absorption capacity of the flour, oil absorption capacity, swelling capacity and hydrophobia of some flours (lack of water absorption capacity). The absorbency of flour can also depend on:
2165 
2166◽️protein level
2167◽️fiber level
2168◽️fat content
2169◽️presence and level of starch
2170◽️grinding fractions
2171◽️flour brand (compositions and processing methods may vary)
2172◽️time of exposure to moisture
2173◽️exposure to high / low temperatures

2208🌱 can be used with almost any product, regardless of its acidity
