3751WHY ADD SALT TO DESSERTS?
3752part 1
3753 
3754When I began to study this question, it immediately became clear that for this topic I’ll need more than one post. You may wonder what salt has to do with dessert. Let’s have a look.
3755 
3756Firstly, in some cases it’s really impossible to make a dessert without salt. Secondly, without it, most of sweet pastries become insipid.
3757 
3758Let's figure it out.
3759 
3760FUNCTIONS OF SALT IN BAKING AND MAKING DESSERTS
3761 
37621. Tightens the gluten structure
3763 
3764Salt has a binding or strengthening effect on gluten — this allows the dough to rise and expand during baking. It’s indispensable, for example, when baking bread, pastries, croissants or sponges.
3765 
3766📍It’s impossible to get a tall sponge without adding salt to the batter. Salted batter will turn out more voluminous and stronger than unsalted.
3767 
37682. Enhances the taste
3769 
3770Salt brings out the characteristic taste of pastry it is used in. When used in moderate amounts, it does not make the product taste salty, but simply enhances its best qualities. It turns insipid two-dimensional tastes into rich, three-dimensional ones.
3771 
3772Such an effect is achieved thanks to the right combination of different tastes. For example, when salt is added, sweet flavors become less sweet and more complex, while sour flavors become less prominent.
3773 
37743. Neutralize bitterness
3775 
3776Salt neutralizes bitterness, so any bitter foods (e.g. coffee, dark chocolate, grapefruit) become more appetizing and tasty if you add a little salt.
3777 
3778📍 Grapefruit remains slightly bitter both fresh and baked even after removing the albedo (the whitish, spongy inner part of the rind of a citrus fruit). But salting the pulp helps to get rid of this specific aftertaste.
3779 
37804. Stabilizes the egg white foam
3781 
3782It’s thanks to the addition of that tiny pinch of salt that the egg whites, when whipped, start to increase in volume and become lush. Adding salt to your meringue will ensure that all of the protein chains have safely bonded together.
3783 
3784A similar effect can be obtained by adding acid (e.g. lemon juice).
3785part 2
3786 
3787Judging by your comments, this topic turned out to be interesting for many of you. Today we’ll continue to talk about the functions of salt. We have already analyzed the first four functions (check out the beginning by following the hashtag #marusya_about_salt), but that's not all😉
3788 
3789So let's continue.
3790 
3791FUNCTIONS OF SALT IN BAKING AND MAKING DESSERTS
3792part 3
3793 
3794Continuing to delve into the topic of infusion, today we take into account the hot method, that I use quite often when developing recipes.
3795 
3796HOT METHOD
3797 
3798Many of you, probably, have guessed that the hot method involves heat treatment. You’re on the right track. Hot infusion consists in bringing the liquid together with spices, herbs or other flavorings to a boil, followed by infusion for 30-60 minutes.
3799 
3800The infused liquid can be cream, milk or berry puree. For example, you can infuse the cream with dry lavender, as I did in Blueberry-Lavender Cheesecake (photo 3-6), or you can give a mouthwatering coffee aroma by hot infusion the cream with ground cofee (Coffee-Baileys Cheesecake, photo 7-8).
3801 
3802Everything is very simple, but there are secrets that I will gladly share with you.
3803 
3804HOT METHOD SECRETS
3805 
3806💎 The infused liquid must be heated over medium or low heat so that the heating occurs gradually.
3807💎 In the infusion process, I advise you to use as few dishes as possible to prepare ingredients. For example, it is better to remove the zest immediately into cream. Don’t remove the zest on the board, and then transfer it to cream from the board, as all spices, herbs and citrus fruits contain a large amount of essential oils which evaporate easily.
3808💎 After boiling, you must immediately remove the saucepan from the heat - at too high temperatures, flavors can release substances that will negatively affect the flavor and aroma.
3809💎 After heating, it is better to cover the liquid with a cling film in contact. If you cover it with a lid, excess moisture from evaporation will form on it, which will get into the liquid. Furthermore, skin which you will need to remove will be formed.
3810💎 After infusion, don’t forget to strain it through a sieve or cheesecloth.
3811💎 The infused liquid can be stored in the refrigerator after complete cooling during the shelf life of the main product. Keep in mind that the resulting flavor will evaporate with each day of storage!
3812 
3813Did you learn something new for yourself today? If yes, let me know in the comments ⬇️
38145. Balances sweetness
3815 
3816Salt helps to balance the taste of products with a pronounced sweetness, such as caramel, chocolate or meringue. Such products are usually treacly, and it’s almost impossible to add other flavor accents to them, even if you think they will work well in your pastry. Salt added in low concentrations balances the sweetness and adds depth to other flavors, so that our taste buds perceive them as moderately sweet, although the amount of sugar remains unchanged.
3817 
38186. Helps to release juices
3819 
3820Salt changes the balance of liquid in foods. It makes juicy berries and fruits release juices, leaving the fibrous material behind. If you are making a fruit dessert and you need to get the maximum juice, just add a pinch of salt to your fruits.
3821 
38227. Increases the shelf life
3823 
3824Salt is a natural preservative. It dehydrates harmful microorganisms. Due to dehydration, bacteria become inactive, their growth stops, the metabolism of their cells becomes impossible, which is why they die. Different types of microorganisms become harmless at different salt concentrations. But even in a small amount, salt increases the shelf life of pastry.
3825 
38268. Helps to maintain the recommended daily sodium intake
3827 
3828Our body needs a certain amount of salt (NaCl) to function properly, so it's natural that we tend to like the taste of salt. This does not mean that we should eat tons of salt a day, but using it in reasonable amounts in cooking and making desserts will help us stay healthy and enjoy food.
3829INFUSION: WHAT, BY WHAT AND HOW
3830 
3831#marusya_infusion
3832 
3833part 4 (the last one)
3834 
3835We have already talked with you about the cold and hot method of infusion. Today I will tell you about decoction and vacuuming.
3836 
3837DECOCTION
3838 
3839This method, of course, is very similar to hot method — the liquid is also subjected to heat treatment. However, there is one significant difference — with hot method, we only heat the liquid or bring it to a boil, but as for decoction, we boil for several minutes or even cook for up to 60 minutes to get dense aromatic decoctions.
3840Infusions are steeped and decoctions are simmered.
3841 
3842If we talk about confectionery, this method is often used in the preparation of flavored sugar syrups for coating muffins or, for example, soaking sponge cake.
3843 
3844For the preparation of fillings for honey cakes "Fig-Parmesan-Gorgonzola-Red Wine" and "Quince-Goat Cheese-Medium Sweet White Wine" we also use this method — juicy fleshy figs and fragrant quince are stewed in wine from 25 to 60 minutes until the liquid has almost completely evaporated. Due to the prolonged exposure to high temperature, we get incredibly tasty, rich and fragrant fillings!
3845 
3846VACUUMING
3847 
3848This is a method for infusion of solid ingredients (bananas, mangoes, coconut, pineapple, apples, etc.), which are subsequently used as whole or cut.
3849 
3850Thanks to vacuuming, we can give the main product a lot of flavors!
3851 
3852There are several ways:
3853 
38541️⃣ Take a dense main ingredient and pour it with juice of the infusing one, then leave to infuse in the refrigerator.
38552️⃣ Mix the main and infusing ingredients — you can even wrap apple pieces with lemon slices or thin mango strips, sprinkled with cinnamon or other spices. After that, it is necessary to place them in a bag for vacuuming and pump out the air with a vacuum cleaner.
3856 
3857As a result, you can get an orange-flavored apple, for example, or a lemon-flavored banana.
3858 
3859EVERYTHING YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT AGAR
3860part 1
3861 
3862In previous posts, we have already talked with you about gelatin - #marusya_about_gelatin and pectin - #marusya_about_pectin. Today I suggest analyzing the next, no less interesting thickener - agar, aka agar-agar, aka seaweed food thickener.
3863 
3864Agar means "seaweed" in Malaysian. Its gelling properties are said to be accidentally discovered in Japan in the 17th century by Mino Tarōzaemon. He found out that the chilled seaweed soup had a jelly-like consistency the next day.
3865 
3866Agar is extracted from red and brown algae, which undergo several stages of rinsing, drying and grinding.
3867 
3868Nowadays, this natural thickener is widely used in confectionery art. It’s used to make zephyr, marmalade, fruit pastes (pastila), sweets, jelly and mousse desserts.
3869 
3870Agar can be classified by its quality and gel strength.
3871 
3872🌿Quality
3873 
3874🔸 High grade agar - has a white or light yellow color, although it can have a slightly grayish tint
3875🔸 First grade agar - varies from yellow to dark yellow color
3876 
3877🌿Gel strength
3878 
3879🔸 600-1200 g/cm2
3880 
3881The higher the number it has, the thicker gel can be obtained. The greater the strength of agar is, the less amount of it is needed to gel the mixture.
3882 
3883📎 You can check out the table that shows how to convert between different amounts of agar-agar depending on the gel strength in the carousel.
3884 
3885Agar is often referred to as a natural substitute for gelatin. However, these two thickeners have a number of differences.
3886 
3887AGAR VS GELATIN
3888 
38891️⃣ Unlike gelatin, agar does not impart any taste, smell, or color to desserts.
38902️⃣ In terms of gelling properties, agar is 10 times superior to gelatin
38913️⃣ Unlike gelatin, agar activates in an acidic environment. Gelatin is not a good choice when it comes to preparing, for example, desserts with pineapple or kiwi - they contain enzymes that destroy proteins (87-92% of the composition of gelatin). But agar-agar perfectly gels such mixtures.
38924️⃣ Unlike gelatin, agar is thermoreversible - it can be heated and frozen as many times as you like.
3893part 2
3894 
3895Please support this useful post with your like ❤️ ー this is extremely important for us now!
3896 
3897For those interested in the answer to the question from the previous post about using agar to make the orange confit for the “Orange-Dark Chocolate" honey cake: I chose it because the filling made with gelatin began to leak out after defrosting.
3898 
3899This is due to the fact that ice crystals formed in the orange confit during freezing, which broke the texture of the jelly. As a result, the filling lost its original properties and began to leak out after defrosting.
3900 
3901Will gelatin-based filling always leak out after defrosting? No, it won’t. Moreover, sometimes after defrosting, a filling made with agar or pectin may also leak out. What does it depend on? First of all, it depends on the amount of unbound water in the composition, but this should be discussed in another post.
3902 
3903Today we’ll continue examining the agar. So, here are the RULES OF WORKING WITH AGAR:
3904 
39051️⃣ Agar does not dissolve in cold water ー it should be brought to 95-100 °C to completely dissolve, at this temperature it forms a viscous and transparent solution, which when cooled to 35-40 °C, turns into a thick and pure gel.
3906 
39072️⃣ Agar does not require pre-soaking in water or mixing with sugar. In order to introduce agar into the mixture, it is enough just to add it in there! The mixture can be hot, warm or cold ー it does not matter.
3908 
39093️⃣ Agar is a thermoreversible thickener. You can heat and freeze fillings made with it as many times as you like ー it won't lose its gelling properties.
3910 
39114️⃣ One of the main advantages of agar over gelatin is the ability to activate in an acidic environment. The advantage over pectin is that agar gels well even in the absence of sugar in the mixture.
3912 
39135️⃣ The thicker the desired consistency of the gel you want to obtain is, the stronger agar should be used and / or the more agar you need.
3914part 3
3915 
3916Today we’ll talk about how to correctly calculate the required amount of agar and what it depends on.
3917 
3918If you are making a dessert with agar using a recipe from an eminent chef, in order to get a stable result, be sure to pay attention to:
3919 
3920🔸 amount of agar — agar is a very strong thickener (10 times stronger than gelatin), so even a slight deviation of 0.1-0.2 g can change the texture of the gel
3921🔸 strength of agar — if the recipe says "5 g of agar 1200", to get a dessert "like from a chef" you need to either use agar of the same strength or use the one that is available and convert between its strength.
3922 
3923⬆️ you can find the table that shows how to convert between different amounts of agar-agar depending on the gel strength in the 1st post from the series #marusya_about_agar.
3924 
3925🔸 agar brand (if specified) — as a rule, despite the same strength, agar of different brands can slightly differ in the opacity / transparency of the gel obtained and gelling properties, so it is best to stick to a brand recommended by chef.
3926 
3927If you use a recipe from the Internet, where there’s no information about the strength of the agar used, or you want to create a dessert yourself, take into account the following tips:
3928 
3929🌿 Recommended dosage
3930 
39312-4 g of agar per 150-180 ml of liquid
3932 
3933The exact amount depends on:
3934 
3935🔺 liquid being gelled and its acidity
3936 
3937The higher the acidity of the liquid is, the more agar must be added. This is due to the fact that acid lowers the gelling properties of thickeners, including agar. For example, to gel 100 ml of a neutral liquid (e.g. water, milk) you’ll need 0.9-1 g of agar, while for 100 ml of an acidic liquid (e.g. juice) — 1.3-1.5 g of agar.
3938 
3939🔺 desired texture
3940 
3941runny texture — 0.8 g / 500 ml
3942soft texture — 1.5 g / 500 ml
3943thick texture — 5 g / 500 ml
3944very thick texture — 7 g / 500 ml
3945 
3946If this post was useful for you, leave any comment or emoticon under it — I’ll be very pleased ⬇️
3947#marusya_about_agar
3948 
3949part 4
3950 
3951Today will be the final part of a series of articles about agar. I’ll tell you about the terms and conditions of storage, as well as possible agar substitutes.
3952 
3953🌿 TERMS AND CONDITIONS OF STORAGE
3954 
3955🔸Agar
3956 
3957The shelf life of agar varies from 2 to 3 years since the date of packaging. It is indicated on the packaging and may change depending on the formula and production technology of agar by a specific manufacturer.
3958 
3959Agar should be stored in a cool, dry place, protected from sunlight, in airtight packaging.
3960 
3961🔸 Products with agar
3962 
39631️⃣ Agar-based confectionery products are stored about 24 to 36 hours at a temperature between +4 to -8°C
39642️⃣ Fruit mixtures — up to 7 days in the refrigerator
39653️⃣ Jam and other preservation — up to 1 month in a cool, dry place, protected from sunlight
3966 
3967🌿 AGAR SUBSTITUTES
3968 
3969🔸 Gelatin
3970Gelatin has a lower density after cooling than agar, so it is suitable for making more delicate desserts. If you want to get a dense and smooth texture, it is better to use agar.
3971 
3972In general, these thickeners can be considered interchangeable, but it should be kept in mind that the gelling ability of agar is much higher than that of gelatin.
3973 
3974💎 Substitute: 1 tsp. agar = 2-3 tsp. gelatin
3975 
3976ℹ️ It’s not suitable for vegetarians, as gelatin is made from the bones and tendons of animals
3977 
3978🔸 Pectin
3979 
3980Pectin is the most beneficial and equivalent substitute for agar, but there are exceptions. Both products have the vegetal nature and the only difference is that pectin contains carbohydrates, and its calorie content is higher than that of agar.
3981 
3982A prerequisite for the "activation" of pectin is the presence of sugar. Accordingly, substitute agar for pectin in the making of sugar-free desserts is impossible.
3983 
3984💎 Substitute: 1 tsp. agar = 2-3 tsp. pectin
3985 
3986🔸 Corn Starch
3987 
3988Agar-agar can be substituted for starch. However, starch makes the filling opaque, when dissolved, that significantly narrows the range of its possibilities.
3989 
3990💎 Substitute: 1 tsp. agar = 2 tsp. starch

4069BROWN BUTTER OR BEURRE NOISETTE
4070 
4071#marusya_theory_tips
4072 
4073In the comments under yesterday's post, I noticed that for some people the technique of making brown butter was new and it raised questions and doubts.
4074 
4075Therefore, today I decided to share with you my tips and tricks in order to dispel your doubts and inspire you to make incredibly delicious blackberry, bilberry and star anise cupcakes while the berry season is in full swing.
4076 
4077Brown or browned butter (fr. beurre noisette) are all different names for the same product obtained by heating regular butter - it melts, separates into butterfat and milk solids, the latter sink to the bottom of the pan and turn a deep brown color.
4078 
4079The browning (caramelization) of butter is the result of the milk proteins and sugars going through the Maillard reaction upon heating to 130 °C.
4080 
4081The recipe for brown butter can be found in the previous post. But I’d like to give you some tips so that everything goes perfectly, and you don’t get nervous.
4082 
40836 TIPS FOR GETTING THE PERFECT BEURRE NOISETTE
4084 
40851️⃣ Use a thick-bottomed saucepan to ensure even heating of the butter.
4086 
40872️⃣ The saucepan should be of a light color - it’s more difficult to notice the change in the color of the butter against a dark background, and it can easily burn.
4088 
40893️⃣ To get the perfect brown butter, I advise you to heat no more than 200 g of butter at a time in a small saucepan (d = 14-16 cm). If you need a larger amount, use a saucepan d = 22-26 cm.
4090 
40914️⃣ When the butter gets frothy, start to constantly stir it with a whisk so that the milk solids do not burn.
4092 
40935️⃣ After bringing the butter to 128 °C, remove the saucepan from the heat, while continuing to stir - within 30-40 seconds the residual heat of the saucepan will bring the butter to the desired slightly brownish color (it will heat up to 130-138 °C).
4094 
40956️⃣ Immediately after this, pour the butter into a separate container to stop the cooking process. Otherwise, the temperature will continue to rise, and as soon as it exceeds 140 °C, the butter will burn.
4096NFUSION. THE INS AND OUTS
4097 
4098#marusya_infusion
4099part 1
4100 
4101At first glance, the topic is very simple and there is nothing to talk about. But! You will be surprised when you find out that even when I was giving a master class at a large-scale production, the guys were perplexed by my advice to change the method of infusion to cold.
4102 
4103In fact, aroma and taste are inextricably linked, and they should be considered as a whole.
4104 
4105You can read more about tastes and how they are created by the hashtag #marusya_flavours
4106 
4107Flavoring is an amazing thing that you encounter when diving into the world of confectionery. After baking ordinary shortbread cookies and charlotte, you get a bit bored and you feel a desire to play with new or unusual flavor combinations, add a subtle aroma.
4108 
4109The right aroma and taste of a dessert can calm a person or, on the contrary, energize, make them happy or make them think a little. For example, vanilla - brings warmth and tranquility, cinnamon - reminds of a warm autumn. Lemongrass and lavender have soothing effect.
4110 
4111In order to transfer the flavor of one ingredient to another, they must be combined under suitable conditions:
4112 
4113🔸 mixed or blended together
4114🔸 chilled together
4115🔸 heated together
4116🔸 boiled together
4117 
4118WHAT CAN ONE INFUSE?
4119 
4120In mousses:
4121 
4122🔺 liquid base (cream/milk)
4123🔺 syrup for cooking the egg whites (for Italian meringue-based mousse)
4124🔺 mousses can also be flavored with pastes
4125 
4126📍only thick pastes which don’t change, but rather improve the texture of the dessert, are used: nut, praline and some fruit pastes
4127 
4128In ganaches:
4129 
4130🔺 cream
4131🔺 fruit puree
4132🔺 water
4133 
4134In berry fillings:
4135 
4136🔺 berries or berry puree can undergo cold or vacuum infusion process
4137 
4138In sponges and other desserts that are baked at high temperatures:
4139 
4140🔺 the flavoring is added directly to the composition of the product
4141part 2
4142 
4143In the previous part, we discussed with you what can be infused. Today I'll tell you about the most popular flavorings.
4144 
4145Most likely, the first thing that comes to mind is flavors. Both natural and artificial flavors will help to improve or change the taste of a dessert. This method is good for 3 reasons:
4146 
41471️⃣ it doesn’t require much time and effort - add a drop, mix and it’s done!
41482️⃣ it’s helpful when you don't want to add extra ingredients to the texture in order not to spoil it
41493️⃣ it’s convenient if you need an unusual flavor (bubble gum, California strawberry, Tiramisu)
4150 
4151Apart from flavors, the following ingredients can be used to create an infusion:
4152 
4153🔸 Vanilla (pod/paste/extract)
4154🔸 Spices and seasoning (pepper, cinnamon, cardamom, anise, coriander, star anise, cloves, ginger)
4155🔸 Herbs and flowers (tarragon, basil, dill, thyme, rosemary, lavender)
4156🔸 Tea (black, matcha, red, green)
4157🔸 Fruit pastes
4158🔸 Citrus zest
4159🔸 Freeze-dried berries
4160🔸 Tonka beans
4161🔸 Coffee beans
4162🔸 Alcoholic drinks (rum, brandy, liqueurs)
4163🔸 Syrups
4164🔸 Natural essential extracts and oils
4165 
4166And this is not all!
4167 
4168Now let’s proceed to the most interesting part - infusion methods. They are:
4169 
4170🔺 Cold
4171🔺 Hot
4172🔺 Decoction
4173🔺 Vacuum
4174 
4175COLD METHOD
4176 
4177Cold method consists in adding a certain flavor (spices, herbs, zest, etc.) to a liquid (cream, milk, berry puree), and then placing it in the fridge for at least 12 hours.
4178 
4179This is the longest method of infusion - the aromatization of the liquid occurs slowly and not as intensively as with the hot method.
4180It will take 3-5 days to almost completely extract flavor from fresh herbs into a liquid, and about 20 days for dry ones. In addition, a MORE flavoring component will be required.
4181 
4182But if you want to get an exquisite, sophisticated flavor and avoid heat treatment, this method will be the best option.

4187People often ask me how I come up with all these ideas and sometimes just mind blowing flavor combinations. Should one be born with this ability or it’s possible to learn how to do it?

Behind any ability, be it the ability to create a new musical composition or a new cake, there is experience and a huge theoretical base, which have long been gained and, voluntarily or involuntarily, take part in the creation of a new masterpiece.
4192 
4193Creating new flavors, I, first of all, rely on my inner instinct. And no matter how much I want to, I can’t teach it. But along with my intuition, I also actively use knowledge from the science of combining ingredients - food pairing.
4194 
4195I’d love to share this particular knowledge. Therefore, today I am presenting a new series of posts on flavor pairing. And since the season of APRICOTS is now in full swing, let's talk about flavor combinations of this fruit first.
4196 
4197🌿 Flavor description
4198🔸 sweet-tart
4199🔸 a creamy, floral character
4200🔸 a mixture of fresh notes of tropical fruits
4201🔸 pronounced floral notes, in particular lavender
4202 
4203🌿 Aroma profile
4204🔸 fruity notes
4205🔸 high concentration of creamy notes
4206 
4207Apricots match well with milky flavors as well as the floral components of other fruits. They perfectly pair with:
4208

4210

4214Pectin NH Nappage
4215 
4216There are plenty of articles about pectins, but this topic doesn’t seem to lose its relevance and the number of questions regarding this thickener and how to work with it is still huge:
4217 
4218❓ Why do I prefer pectin when I need to stabilize any berry or fruit filling?
4219 
4220✅ because pectin gives the most natural texture and, at the same time, it doesn’t affect the taste, smell and color of the finished product
4221 
4222❓Why do I use pectin NH Nappage and not any other pectin, what’s special about it?
4223 
4224✅ pectin NH Nappage is used for the preparation of a neutral gel and glazes, as it allows to obtain a more transparent gel
4225✅ it’s thermoreversible - it can be reheated and frozen as many times as you need
4226✅ it doesn’t require a lot of sugar and / or acid in the mixture
4227✅ thanks to the presence of additional stabilizers in the composition, it perfectly stabilizes juices
4228 
4229❓ What is the composition of pectin NH Nappage, apart from E440 pectin itself?
4230✅ diphosphate E450 - stabilizer, acidity regulator, emulsifier, leavening agent, water-retaining agent
4231✅ calcium phosphate E341 - stabilizer, acidity regulator, leavening agent, color fixer
4232✅ dextrose - pure glucose - a monosaccharide, one hundred percent carbohydrate
4233 
4234❓Which brands of pectin NH Nappage do I prefer?
4235 
4236✅ Spanish Sosa
4237✅ French Louis Francois
4238 
4239❓How much pectin NH Nappage do you need to perfectly thicken the filling and how to introduce it correctly?
4240 
4241✅ the recommended dose is 0.8-2.0 g per 100 g of the mixture, depending on the content of pectin in the puree, the amount of water and the desired thickness of the filling
4242✅ pectin should be mixed with sugar in 1:5 ratio and then gradually sprinkled into the warm mixture. While doing it, stir the mixture constantly to avoid lumps
4243✅ it fully dissolves within 15 minutes
4244 
4245❓Are there cases of pectin NH Nappage not setting the mixture?
4246 
4247✅ yes, this pectin practically doesn’t activate in mixtures containing alcohol
4248 
4249Which thickener do you prefer to use and why?
4250CRÈME ANGLAISE FROM A TO Z
4251As it turned out, not everyone can properly make the crème anglaise (French for "English cream") or custard. There’s nothing complicated about its preparation, but at the same time, to obtain a stable result, you need to follow a number of rules, as well as understand the processes that occur during its preparation.
4252 
4253So in order to avoid difficulties in working with the custard, you’ll need to know some tips and tricks which I’m going to share with you today.
4254 
4255Let's start with the concept of crème anglaise and custard.
4256 
4257Custard is a combination of water, fats and sugars which becomes possible thanks to an emulsifying agent.
4258 
4259Under normal conditions, fats and water don’t mix, but with the presence of an emulsifier it becomes possible.
4260 
4261Crème anglaise is a type of custard in which the emulsifying agent is the lecithin present in egg yolk.
4262 
4263Crème anglaise can be used as a standalone product, as well as a sauce (French version) or a base for making mousses, ice creams and creams (English version).
4264 
4265✅ Composition of crème anglaise:
4266 
4267🔸 70% - liquid (milk / milk + cream 1:1 / milk + fruit puree 1:1)
4268🔸 15% - egg yolks
4269🔸 15% - sugar
4270 
4271To make crème anglaise, mix egg yolks with sugar and boil the milk and/or cream. Pour 1/3 of milk into the yolk-sugar mixture, then pour everything back into a saucepan with the remaining milk. Bring to 82°C and remove from the stove.
4272 
4273Basically, you can use this recipe and get down to cooking. However, there’s a huge possibility that something will go wrong.
4274 
4275‼️ To properly make crème anglaise, you should follow a number of rules:
4276 
4277🔸 all ingredients must be fresh, as the enzymes contained in milk catalyze many biochemical processes
4278🔸 all tools used to cook the custard must be perfectly clean
4279🔸 use an appropriate tool at each stage of preparation: a whisk - for beating the yolks with sugar, a silicone spatula - for cooking the cream
4280🔸 stick to the right temperatures - bring the cream to 82-83 °C to pasteurize it and obtain the desired consistency
4281part 2
4282 
4283I received an interesting question:
4284 
4285How can one cook crème anglaise to 82°C and not get an “omelette”?
4286 
4287To answer this question, let’s analyze the composition and properties of some ingredients that are used to make the custard.
4288 
4289🌿 Yolk
4290 
4291Composition:
4292 
4293🔸 50-54% - water
4294🔸 30-33% - fats and lipids
4295🔸 16-17 - proteins (including lecithin)
4296🔸 1% - carbohydrates and minerals
4297 
4298It is thanks to lecithin that fats form an amalgam (emulsion) with a liquid. That is, an egg is, in fact, a natural emulsion.
4299 
4300‼️ Lecithin must not be heated above 70°C
4301 
4302🌿 Sugar
4303 
43041️⃣ Sugar is very hygroscopic, that is, it has a high ability to absorb moisture.
4305 
4306I suggest doing a little experiment. Place one yolk in a bowl, add a couple of tbsp of sugar and leave it like that without stirring.
4307 
4308After a while, you’ll find that the mixture turned grainy - the sugar absorbed all the liquid from the yolk.
4309 
4310Therefore, when you start making crème anglaise, as soon as you add sugar to the yolks, IMMEDIATELY start to stir the mixture vigorously with a whisk! If during this process you decide to set it aside and answer a phone call, for example, the custard won’t be of the right texture anymore - the emulsion will break, which is IMPOSSIBLE TO FIX! If you continue to cook the custard using this mixture, you’ll get a lumpy mixture in your saucepan.
4311 
4312Next, we face another interesting task:
4313 
4314❌ As you remember, lecithin, which is found in the yolks, must not be heated above 70°C.
4315 
4316✅ But crème anglaise must be brought to 82-83°C in order to achieve the desired consistency and, most importantly, to pasteurize the product.
4317 
4318Here the 2nd property of sugar comes to the rescue:
4319 
43202️⃣ Sugar prevents lecithin and proteins from overheating.
4321 
4322By adding sugar to the yolks, we thereby delay the destruction of lecithin, which allows us to bring the crème anglaise to 82°C and not cook it into an “omelette”.
4323 
4324📌 Even if you thoroughly stirred the yolks with sugar, there still is a risk of curdling (coagulation) of proteins.
4325part 3
4326 
4327As promised, today I’ll tell you why, when making crème anglaise, you still might cook it into an “omelette” after bringing it to 82 °C, even if you thoroughly whisked the sugar with yolks, protecting the lecithin from overheating.
4328 
4329The reason for this is quite obvious - the custard was brought to a higher temperature.
4330 
4331To measure the temperature correctly, follow guidelines:
4332 
4333🔸 When cooking the custard, stir it with a spatula and not with a whisk to avoid air getting into the custard. When dealing with the aerated mixture it is almost impossible to measure the temperature correctly!
4334 
4335📌 Cooking crème anglaise with a whisk is considered a gross mistake!
4336❌ Firstly, you compromise the very concept of the custard by letting the air penetrate into it - thus you’ll get a mousse, not a custard.
4337❌ Secondly, you won’t be able to accurately measure the temperature of the mousse.
4338 
4339🔸 Stir the custard carefully, making figure-eight motion with spatula - this will ensure uniform heating of the entire mixture and prevent it from scorching.
4340 
4341🔸 Do not use a pyrometer to measure the temperature of the custard - it is designed to read the temperature on the surface! Use a probe thermometer istead, immersing it into the mixture (avoid touching the bottom of the pan!).
4342 
4343🔸 However, if you are cooking on an induction stove, I do not recommend using a probe thermometer to check the temperature of the custard - no matter how hard you try, the device won’t read the temperature correctly, because:
4344 
4345❌ the bottom of the pans for induction stoves is metal, the probe is also metal, that is the induction heats it along with the pan - to put it another way, the probe thermometer will read not the temperature of the mixture, but the temperature the metal is heated to.
4346 
4347✅ Solution: If you have an induction stove, use a glass deep fry/candy thermometer
4348Part 4
4349 
4350Complete cooking instructions
4351 
43521️⃣ Whisk the egg yolks and sugar.
4353 
4354📌 Whisk thoroughly and quickly to avoid lumps. You need to ensure a good emulsion so that the yolks don’t separate.
4355 
43562️⃣ Heat the milk.
4357 
4358If you’re using pasteurized or UHT milk, bring it to 85°C.
4359 
4360If you’re using raw milk, bring it to a boil (100°C) to pasteurize.
4361 
4362📌 When the milk has boiled, a film forms on its surface - these are proteins that have coagulated. To prevent skin formation, add some sugar to the milk.
4363📌 It is best to use pasteurized dairy products to achieve a balanced taste in the final product.
4364 
43653️⃣ Temper the egg yolks - radually pour a third of the hot milk into the yolk mixture in a thin stream and mix well with a silicone spatula.
4366 
4367❌ If you pour the milk too quickly, the yolks can cook, while you only need to heat up the yolk mixture.
4368 
43694️⃣ Pour in the remaining milk, put the saucepan on low heat and pasteurize the mixture at 82-83°C, stirring continuously with a spatula.
4370 
4371❌ Do not cook it on high heat - the custard may burn!
4372 
43735️⃣ Remove the custard from the heat and pour it into a wide plastic vessel.
4374 
4375📌 If you used flavors (vanilla pod / zest / herbs), strain the custard first.
4376 
43776️⃣ Place the vessel in a shock freezer or over an ice bath for quick cooling.
4378 
4379It is vital to quickly cool the custard to room temperature, as it is made from milk and eggs, which spoil quickly.
4380 
43817️⃣ Cooled custard:
4382 
4383a) if you’re going to use crème anglaise as a standalone product or as a sauce, cover it with cling film in contact and place it in the fridge for further stabilization
4384 
4385b) if you’re going to use it as a base for frosting - add it to the remaining products (e.g. melted chocolate), cover with cling film in contact and place in the fridge
4386 
4387c) if you’re going to use it as a base for crémeux - first add melted gelatin mass, then quickly cool it, cover with cling film and place in the fridge
43885 PROBLEMS WHEN MAKING CRÈME ANGLAISE AND HOW TO SOLVE THEM
4389 
4390This is a final post about crème anglaise, in which I’ll tell you what problems you may face in the process of making custard, and how to solve them.
4391 
43921️⃣ Runny custard
4393 
4394❌ Reason
4395The custard hasn’t been brought to 82-83 °C.
4396 
4397Crème anglaise contains the alpha-amylase enzyme which can break down protein.
4398 
4399If the custard has been brought to less than 74 °C, the alpha-amylase won't get destroyed and will digest the proteins. In other words, the custard will be runny. Such custard can't be fixed.
4400 
4401✅ Solution
4402If the custard has been brought to a temperature above 75 °C, the product has been pasteurized (alpha-amylase is destroyed), it can be fixed - just add bloomed gelatin in order to make the custard more stable.
4403 
44042️⃣ Custard has curdled
4405 
4406❌ Reason
4407The custard was brought to a temperature above 82-83 °C.
4408 
4409✅ Solution
4410In the future, be sure to measure the temperature correctly following the rules described in the previous post (P3). You can find them by the hashtag #marusya_about_crèmeanglaise
4411 
4412Someone advises blending curdled custard. Thus, you will eliminate the "lump effect", but won’t get rid of the omelette taste. Therefore, if your crème anglaise has split, I advise you to remake it so as not to compromise the whole cake.
4413 
44143️⃣ Custard foams during cooking
4415 
4416❌ Reason
4417See what’s in your hand. I'm sure it's a whisk.
4418 
4419✅ Solution
4420After beating the yolks with sugar, put the whisk aside and switch to a silicone spatula. This is the appropriate tool to cook the custard with.
4421 
44224️⃣ There are yolk pieces in custard
4423 
4424❌ Reason
4425Poorly beaten yolks with sugar
4426 
4427✅ Solution
4428Remake the custard
4429 
4430I don’t advise you to strain it. Yes, of course, you’ll get rid of pieces of yolk floating in the custard, but this won’t help you to get rid of the yolk taste.
4431 
44325️⃣ Skin forms on the custard surface
4433 
4434❌ Reason
4435The custard wasn’t covered with cling film or wasn't covered tightly enough.
4436 
4437✅ Solution
4438Strain the custard or carefully remove the skin. Then cover the crème anglaise with cling film touching the surface.
4439HOW TO DIFFERENTIATE VEGETABLE CREAM FROM DAIRY?
4440 
4441#marusya_theory_tips
4442 
4443The previous post got more than 20 comments, so, as promised, I will tell you how to differentiate vegetable cream (or cream containing a mixture of non-dairy and dairy ingredients) from dairy cream at home.
4444 
4445Unfortunately, sometimes the name of the product or even its composition provided on the packaging is not really helpful in finding out whether it’s a dairy, vegetable or mixed imitation cream.
4446 
4447If you want to get quality pastry products with a rich taste, I advise you to carry out a small test before giving preference to a particular brand.
4448 
4449Put a small amount of cream in a saucepan and bring it to a boil.
4450 
4451❌ You’re dealing with vegetable / mixed imitation cream which contains a huge amount of thickeners and stabilizers, if after boiling it:
4452 
4453🔸 has become even thicker than it was (sometimes it may look like low-fat sour cream)
4454🔸 has not changed its original snow-white color
4455 
4456If you observe this with your cream, I can assume that it contains only vegetable fats or, at best, it has a mixed composition of milk and vegetable fats.
4457 
4458✅ You’re dealing with dairy cream, if after boiling it:
4459 
4460🔸 has become liquid, the consistency resembles full-fat milk
4461🔸 has acquired a yellowish tint, characteristic of dairy products (after heating, organic heavy cream acquires a well-defined milky or ivory color)
4462 
4463Similar processes are typical for cream of animal origin. You can trust a brand that has passed such a test.
4464 
4465I can share with you the brands that have passed this test in my kitchen.
4466 
4467The quality brands I trust:
4468 
4469🥛 Mlekovita, 30/36%
4470🥛 President, 33%
4471🥛 Elle & Vire, 35%
4472🥛 Debic, 32%
4473 
4474If you have read the post to the end, leave any emoji in the comments ⬇️ and bookmark the post 📎
4475 
4476P.S. Be sure to test the origin of the cream you use. You'll get valuable experience in a matter of minutes!
4477DAIRY AND NON-DAIRY WHIPPING CREAM
4478 
4479#marusya_theory_tips
4480 
4481My students often ask me which cream is better to use.
4482 
4483First of all, I’d like to say that in my recipes I only use cream of animal origin. I don’t work with plant-based creams for a number of reasons:
4484 
4485🔻 they contain a huge amount of stabilizers, flavors, preservatives and other synthetic components
4486 
4487🔻 contain palm or palm kernel oil. During the preparation of vegetable cream, this oil is partially hydrogenated, that is, the liquid emulsion turns into solid fatty acids.
4488 
4489This is done for the sake of extending the expiration date to ❗️12❗️months. But at the same time, during hydrogenation trans fats are formed, which might affect our health:
4490 
4491❌ raise of cholesterol levels, negative effect on the immune and cardiovascular systems
4492 
4493❌ a higher risk of developing diabetes, liver, kidneys, heart and nervous system diseases. Trans fats have also been hypothesized to be carcinogenic.
4494 
4495🔻 non-dairy cream cannot be heated as after that it may have an unpleasant taste. Such creams are usually used cold, which significantly narrows the range of their use in pastry
4496 
4497🔻and the last reason I don't work with vegetable cream is the low fat content
4498 
4499✅ The number one goal that I pursue when developing new recipes is to get the perfectly balanced taste. And these are the FAT MOLECULES that contribute to the FLAVOR! Therefore, I prefer to use dairy products with a high fat content.
4500 
4501It seems that to get the perfect dessert it is enough to pick any dairy cream in the supermarket and that's it. But it’s not! Unfortunately, quite often the information provided by manufacturers on packaging cannot be trusted, believe my own experience.
4502HOW TO CHOOSE THE RIGHT CHOCOLATE FLUIDITY
4503 
4504#marusya_theory_tips
4505 
4506Fluidity depends on the content of cocoa butter in chocolate - the more cocoa butter in its composition, the more fluid it is.
4507 
4508Chocolate with a higher fluidity contains more cocoa butter and when tempered properly, it allows you to create a hard, thin and crunchy chocolate coating with a glossy sheen.
4509 
4510Such chocolate contracts better during cooling, which makes it easier to remove the chocolate shell from the mold.
4511 
4512🌿 Chocolate with a lower fluidity contains less cocoa butter, which produces a less hard, less shiny and thicker chocolate coating.
4513 
4514Determining the fluidity of chocolate is not difficult, as manufacturers indicate it on the packaging with a drop symbol system. The more drops are drawn on the packaging that you hold in your hands, the more fluid the chocolate is in front of you.
4515 
4516There is a wide range of fluidity levels marked with 1-5 drops:
4517 
4518🔸 1 drop
4519✅ ideal for mousse and frosting
4520 
4521🔸 2 drops
4522✅ perfect for making large molded chocolate figures
4523 
4524🔸 3 drops
4525✅ versatile chocolate suitable for making any texture: frosting, ganache for coating, icing, chocolates
4526 
4527🔸 4-5 drops
4528✅ ideal for molded chocolates with a thin chocolate shell
4529 
4530That is, depending on the application, you should choose a chocolate with the right fluidity.
4531 
4532In my work, I most often use milk or dark chocolate with a fluidity of 3 drops - its properties are most suitable for making ganaches for coating, crémeux, as well as mousse and baked chocolate cheesecakes.
4533SEVERAL THICKENERS IN A FILLING. WHY?
4534 
4535In online courses and during live master classes, which I miss so much, students often ask me why they need to add gelatin if they have already added pectin to the preparation.
4536 
4537The question is really good and very interesting. Let’s find the answer now!
4538 
4539The thing is that each thickener has different properties:
4540 
4541🌿 Agar-agar
4542🔸 gives a delicate, "glassy", not elastic texture
4543🔸 activates at high temperatures (90-100°C). If added to a cold mixture, it starts to set at 40°C and form lumps
4544🔸 must not be frozen as it may lose its properties
4545🔸 if you process the gelled mixture with a blender, you’ll get a gel
4546 
4547🌿Gelatin
4548🔸 gives good elasticity and makes the fillings a bit “rubbery”
4549🔸 activates at low temperatures (can be added at 25-30°C)
4550🔸 retains its properties when frozen
4551🔸 it takes at least 6-7 hours for the filling to set
4552🔸 contains a large amount of protein, which allows one to whip it and get a thick foam
4553 
4554🌿 Pectin (NH, NH Nappage)
4555🔸gives a stable, jam-like texture and doesn’t affect the taste of the mixture
4556🔸 activates at high temperatures (80-100°C)
4557🔸 retains its properties when frozen
4558🔸 fillings made with pectin can be reheated
4559 
4560Sometimes when we want our filling to get the properties of 2 different thickeners at once, we add gelatin or agar-agar to the pectin.
4561 
4562For example, when making the Kiwi-Lime-Bergamot cheesecake, the photo of which you can see in the carousel, I used both pectin (to give light elasticity and natural taste) and agar-agar (for stability) to prepare the Mojito marmalade made from vermouth, lime juice and bergamot.
4563 
4564In this case, the gelatin can’t be used to stabilize (gel) the mixture. Can you guess why?
4565WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT PECTIN
4566part 1
4567 
4568We’ve already talked with you about gelatin, and today I’d like to start a new series of posts about the super-popular thickener - pectin.
4569 
4570So, let's start with finding out what pectin is.
4571 
4572Pectin (from the Greek "pektos" - ‘firm and hard’) is a polysaccharide (high-molecular weight carbohydrate) that is present in foods and has an important function, which is to ensure their quality and texture.
4573 
4574In the culinary arts, pectin acts as a gelling agent, stabilizer, thickener, and water-retaining agent.
4575 
4576Like some other thickeners (cornstarch, agar-agar, gelatin), pectin in small quantities thickens the mixture, and in large quantities - gellies it.
4577 
4578It is used to make jams, confits, jellies, marmalades, fruit pastes (pastila) and much more.
4579 
4580The main advantage of pectin compared to other thickeners is that it is able to form a sufficiently thick texture WITHOUT AFFECTING THE TASTE, SMELL AND COLOR OF THE FINISHED PRODUCT.
4581 
4582Pectin is obtained by aqueous extraction of the appropriate edible plant materials, mainly from citrus peel and apple pomace, sugar-beet pulp or sunflower heads, unlike, for example, gelatin, which is obtained by boiling skin, tendons, ligaments, and/or bones with water.
4583 
4584Interestingly, some fruits (apple, plum, currant) contain so much pectin that one can make jelly or jam from them without adding pectin.
4585 
4586📌I added a table which shows the content of pectin in fruits and berries to the carousel.
4587 
4588🌱The strength of pectin
4589 
4590To standardize pectins, the USA-SAG method is used: a gel is prepared from the pectin under study, sugar, water and acid. While hot, this pectin solution is bottled in standard cups and set aside until set. The single gels are removed carefully from the cups and transferred on a plate which allows one to measure the percentage of sagging of the gel cone under its own weight with the help of a ridgelimeter. From this value the °SAG can be calculated.
4591 
4592The majority of pectins are standardized to 150 grade USA-SAG (the strength of almost any pectin you find in pastry stores).
4593part 2
4594 
4595Thanks to your comments I decided not to put writing the continuation of the post on pectins on the back-burner. Today, as promised, I will tell you about the types of pectin.
4596 
4597There is no unified world classification of pectins. Each manufacturer creates its own unique product! However, pectins can be classified according to a number of properties.
4598 
4599So, here are the classifications of pectin:
4600 
4601🌱 According to the form it comes in
4602 
4603📌 POWDERED
4604 
4605Should be mixed with sugar and gradually sprinkled into the mixture at a temperature of 40-45°C.
4606 
4607📌 LIQUID (EXTRACT)
4608 
4609Should be diluted in hot water and only then added to the mixture. This type of pectin is less common as it can only be used if the mixture can hold the extra liquid. It is more suitable for large productions.
4610 
4611🌱 By chemical properties
4612 
4613During the production, pectin undergoes an esterification process — it interacts with alcohol and acid, which gives it various properties.
4614 
4615📌 Low ester (the degree of esterification (DE) is less than 50%) — is able to gel regardless of the acidity of fruits/berries or amount of sugar, but in the presence of calcium ions.
4616 
4617This type of pectin works with dairy-based and low sugar mixtures.
4618 
4619📌 High ester (DE is 50% or more) — is able to gel at high acidity (low pH) and high sugar content of the mixture. This is the most popular type of pectin — it dissolves faster in water and gels mixtures faster.
4620 
4621In the pastry business, high ester pectin is used to make zephyr, fruit pastes (pastila) and marmalade.
4622 
4623📌 Amidated pectin is a low ester pectin which undergoes ammonia treatment during the manufacturing process. Gelation requires less calcium in neutral pH mixtures and is less prone to setting (gelling) at high calcium concentrations.
4624 
4625Amidated pectin is often used to make fruit fillings for yogurts and thermostable fillings.
4626part 3
4627 
4628So, guys, today is Sunday, and it means that we’ll have a theory today. When our brain, body and soul are relaxed, they are best able to absorb information.
4629 
4630Today we continue to talk about the classifications of pectin. The pectin can also be classified according to:
4631 
4632🌱 Thermal reversibility:
4633 
4634📌Thermoreversible - such pectin becomes liquid when heated and then solidifies when cooled, that is, the mixture can be reheated and cooled again until set.
4635 
4636📌 Regular - once it gels, it cannot be reheated because pectin loses its properties at temperatures above 90-100 °C.
4637 
4638🌱Time of setting (gelling)
4639 
4640📌 Rapid set pectin
4641🔸 degree of esterification - above 70-76%
4642🔸 gelling temperature - 75-85°С
4643🔸 gelling time — 10-15 minutes
4644 
4645📌 Medium rapid set pectin
4646🔸 degree of esterification - 70-72%
4647🔸 gelling temperature - 60-70°С
4648🔸 gelling time — 15-20 minutes
4649 
4650📌 Slow set pectin
4651🔸 degree of esterification - 56-68%
4652🔸 gelling temperature - 45-60°С
4653🔸 gelling time - 20-25 minutes
4654 
4655If we try to analyze these numbers, we can notice a consistent pattern:
4656 
4657✅ The higher the degree of esterification is, the faster the gelling happens, and the higher the temperature is needed to form gel.
4658 
4659The speed of gelation is regulated by chemical compounds in the composition of pectin.
4660 
4661Each manufacturer "plays" with pectin compositions and forms its own assortment. On the one hand, this allows you to choose pectin for absolutely any confectionery purposes, on the other hand, it complicates the issue of their interchangeability.
4662 
4663Let's look at the main types of pectins:
4664 
4665🌱 Apple or citrus pectin
4666 
4667This is basic, regular pectin. It is obtained from the peels, seeds and pulp of citrus fruits. Has nothing but pectin in its composition. This pectin is classified as high ester, that is, to form a stable gel, this type of pectin needs a large amount of sugar in the composition, the presence of acid and high temperature.
4668part 4
4669 
4670I'm super happy that you’re so active in stories reacting to new flavors of cheesecakes
4671 
4672My photographer and one of the first tasters won’t let me lie to you - they taste like heaven! And I got so much pleasure from the process of creating these flavors! I wish I could spend all my time doing this!
4673 
4674Guys, I also ask you to support the new post about pectins as warmly🔥 as you did with cheesecakes. If you want to create new flavor combinations or just love to bake using recipes from famous chefs, this knowledge will definitely come in handy.
4675 
4676Today we continue to analyze the main types of pectins. In addition to the regular basic pectin, you also can find:
4677 
4678🌱 Pectin NH
4679 
4680Low ester thermo-reversible pectin that does not require a high content of sugar and acid in the mixture. It is also suitable for making fillings for mousse cakes - it tolerates freezing well and does not let the liquid leak out when thawed.
4681 
4682🌱 Nappage
4683 
4684Low ester thermo-reversible pectin that activates in mixtures with 60-70% sugar content and sour fruits and berries. Most often it is used to prepare a neutral gel and glazes.
4685 
4686📍 The manufacturers often treat NH pectin and pectin Nappage as the same product. You can even come across the name "Pectin NH Nappage". It's good for making glazes in particular.
4687 
4688The main difference is that pectin Nappage gives a more transparent gel than pectin NH, which is extremely important for the preparation of a neutral glaze.
4689 
4690🌱 Yellow pectin
4691 
4692In Europe, the term "yellow" refers to regular apple or citrus pectin, which can lead to confusion. It all depends on the specific manufacturer, so be sure to read the ingredients on a product label.
4693 
4694PatisFrance yellow pectin is more like nappage, while Sosa yellow pectin is regular apple pectin.
4695 
4696🌱 Pectin NH Plus
4697 
4698Medium ester pectin, which does not form a dense marmalade texture, but thickens well, producing an elastic texture. With this pectin mixtures set faster than with NH pectin. It is used to prepare all kinds of fillings and glazes.
4699part 5
4700 
4701In the previous posts we’ve already discussed such main types of pectin as regular pectin (apple / citrus / yellow), pectin NH, NH Plus and pectin Nappage. Today I suggest analyzing the remaining three types:
4702 
4703🌱 Pectin FX
4704 
4705Low ester pectin, which only works with calcium-containing foods; the level of acidity does not matter. Most often it is used to make milk and cream fillings, marmalade or confiture.
4706 
4707📍It also works with dairy products regardless of the amount of sugar in the mixture, which allows you to get a non-sweet, balanced taste of creamy fillings.
4708 
4709🌱 Acid-free pectin
4710 
4711In fact, this is an analogue of regular pectin, for which the level of acidity does not matter since it contains calcium sulfate which has good hygroscopic, stabilizing and emulsifying properties.
4712 
4713It is used when working with fruits and berries that do not contain acid, e.g. bananas, apples, coconuts, dates, watermelon, etc. It also works well with nut butters. For instance, the pistachio paste marmalade is made using acid-free pectin.
4714 
4715🌱 Slow set pectin
4716 
4717Another analogue of regular pectin. The key difference between them is that slow set pectin gels mixtures much more slowly. This is very convenient if, for example, you need to pour a filling or marmalade into small molds - it gives you 3-4 minutes for this, while any other pectin sets almost instantly.
4718 
4719Knowing the properties of each type of pectin, you can easily choose the right one for any of your confectionery experiments and thus, obtain the best result.
4720 
4721If this series of posts was useful for you, leave any smiley in the comments, and next time I’ll tell you how to decide how much pectin to use😉
4722part 6
4723 
4724Today we’ll talk about ways of adding pectin to the mixture being gelled and will choose the best of them.
4725 
47261️⃣Pre-dissolve pectin in boiling water
4727 
4728Add pectin powder to boiling water, carefully processing it with a hand blender. Then pour it in a thin stream into the hot mixture being gelled, stirring it with a whisk.
4729 
4730This method is used if the mixture can "hold" additional liquid. It's more suitable for large productions.
4731 
4732📍Proportions:
4733 
4734Pectin - 5-8%
4735Water - 92-95%
4736 
47372️⃣ Pre-dissolve pectin in sugar syrup
4738 
4739Add pectin to the sugar syrup and stir thoroughly until the pectin has completely dissolved. Then add it to the mixture being gelled.
4740 
47413️⃣ Mix pectin and sugar
4742 
4743This is the most common and convenient way. Vigorously stirring the warm mixture (35-50 °C) with a whisk, gradually sprinkle in the sugar, pre-mixed with pectin.
4744 
4745📍Bear in mind that at temperatures below 35 °C, pectin may start to set, form lumps, and thus, it will be difficult to stir the mixture and get a good texture.
4746 
4747❓ Why is it necessary to mix sugar and pectin, and not add them separately?
4748 
4749The whole trick is the interaction of pectin with water.
4750 
4751A particle of pectin powder, once in water, absorbs it like a sponge, increasing in size several times, and only after reaching a certain size starts to dissolve.
4752 
4753If pectin powder particles, when in contact with water, are close to each other, then, in the process of absorbing water and swelling, they stick together, forming a sticky lump that slowly dissolves in water. To avoid this, pectin should be pre-mixed with sugar in a ratio of 1:10 or more.
4754#marusya_about_pectin
4755 
4756part 7
4757 
4758You can know everything about pectins, but when it comes to creating some kind of filling or dessert using it, the question arises: how to decide how much pectin to use?
4759 
4760Among the huge number of questions about pectin, this is perhaps one of the most important.
4761 
4762Let's find the answer😉
4763 
4764To start the gelling process, you need 3 components:
4765 
47661️⃣ sugar
47672️⃣ pectin
47683️⃣ acid or calcium
4769 
4770Scientists consider the ratio 60 : 1 : 1 to be optimal
4771 
4772🔸 60 parts of sugar
4773🔸 1 part of pectin
4774🔸 1 part of acid (citric, tartaric, lactic, etc.)
4775 
4776However, this ratio may vary depending on:
4777 
4778🔺 the type of pectin you are using (yellow, NH, FX);
4779🔺 pectin formula, which each manufacturer keeps in strict confidence;
4780🔺 the initial content of pectin in berries or fruits that you are going to use;
4781 
4782📍 the more pectin the fruit you work with contains, the less pectin you need to introduce additionally
4783 
4784⬅️ photo 3 shows a table with pectin content in fruits, berries and vegetables
4785 
4786🔺 the type and density of the filling you want to get: runny sauce (coulis), stable and soft cake filling, dense marmalade, thick confiture...
4787🔺 the type of sugar you are using (sucrose, glucose or fructose).
4788 
4789📍 You can obtain the most dense texture by using sucrose (regular sugar), and the least dense - by using fructose.
4790 
4791⬅️ 4th photo shows the thickness of gel depending on the type of sugar used
4792 
4793Taking into account all the above factors, which the amount of added pectin depends on, it becomes clear that, unfortunately, it is impossible to create a single table with universal proportions of pectin.
4794 
4795But there is good news - most often it is enough just to read the recommendations on the ratio on the packaging, if you have one available.
4796 
4797If not, use the ratio 60 : 1 : 1, take into account all the influencing factors and experiment✨❤️
4798THINGS WE NEED TO KNOW ABOUT EGGS
4799part 1
4800 
4801#marusya_likbez
4802 
4803I haven’t posted theory for a while, so let’s revive this tradition again.
4804 
4805I’m planning to post some useful information on thickeners - pectin, gelatin, agar-agar, starch and much more. I feel that I need to speak out to eliminate your doubts concerning their interchangeability.
4806 
4807Feel free to suggest your topic for the next useful post in the comments. And meanwhile, let’s talk about ... EGGS.
4808 
4809We all know that an egg consists of a shell, and inside there is a yolk and white. But apart from them, there are many more other components inside the egg.
4810 
4811In fact, the anatomy of an egg is the following:
4812🥚 shell (+ cuticle)
4813🥚 outer and inner membrane
4814🥚 air chamber (air cell)
4815🥚 chalaza
4816🥚 vitelline membrane
4817🥚 yolk
4818🥚 egg white
4819 
4820SHELL is the first line of defense against air and bacteria. The shell weight makes up approximately 10% of the total egg weight.
4821 
4822Eggshell contains 7 to 17 thousand tiny pores. These pores are responsible for the movement of air and the penetration of bacteria into the egg, and let the carbon dioxide and moisture escape the egg.
4823 
4824You may ask where the extra water and carbon dioxide come from in the egg. We will definitely talk about this when it comes to egg aging and shelf life
4825 
4826CUTICLE is a natural coating that covers the surface of the egg and helps to fill the pores in order to protect the egg against air and bacteria.
4827 
4828❗️ Egg washing reduces this protection, leading to a more quick egg spoilage. The likelihood of bacteria getting into the egg increases.
4829 
4830OUTER AND INNER MEMBRANE - these are the layers that separate the shell from the egg white.
4831 
4832As the egg ages, the protective shell dries up and begins to flake off, allowing the outside air to enter the egg shell through the small pores and be stored in the AIR CHAMBER for chick to breathe
4833 
4834Why are fresh eggs so hard to peel? Until a few weeks have passed, the amount of air accumulated between the membranes won’t allow them to easily detach and peel the shell from the hard-boiled egg white.
4835 
4836❗️Therefore, by putting a hard-boiled egg into cold water, we do not make it easier to peel it, but significantly reduce its shelf life.
4837part 2
4838 
4839Let's continue on the useful topic of egg constituents. Today we'll talk about chalazes, the yolk membrane and, in fact, the yolk itself. We will devote a separate post to the topic of egg whites because this component is essential in the confectionery industry.
4840 
4841Please, support the release of the useful post with ❤️ and any emoticon in the comments 😉
4842⠀
4843⠀
4844When we break a fresh egg, we can see thin white ropes stretching from the yolk — these are called CHALAZES — twisted threads of egg white that anchor the yolk in the center of the egg white. Chalazes are usually only seen in very fresh eggs.
4845 
4846By the way, quite often I hear that someone does not like custards and curds because of the rather pronounced egg amber. The cause of the unpleasant odor is precisely the chalazes. Therefore, in order to get rid of the unwanted aroma of eggs, it is enough to simply strain the egg melange before use.
4847 
4848Chalazes can also cause breaks in eclairs. Here's another good reason for straining.
4849 
4850The YOLK MEMBRANE or vitelline membrane is a thin coating that helps to retain the yolk. It has as many as 4 layers.
4851 
4852In stale eggs, the membrane becomes thinner, since it for 97% consists of water, which, during storage, gradually evaporates through tiny pores in the eggshell.
4853 
4854The yolk does not spread when a fresh egg is broken due to the strong vitelline membrane, but every week the yolk membrane becomes thinner and weaker.
4855 
4856The yolk accounts for about 39% of the eggs, of which as much as 46% are dry matter. The yolk contains fat and cholesterol, as well as most of the calories in an egg. Most vitamins and nutrients are found in the yolk.
4857 
4858The yolk is an emulsion that contains the natural emulsifier lecithin, which performs an extremely important for pastry chefs function — retaining moisture in the product. That is why baked goods with only yolks come out more tender and do not stale much longer than with whole eggs.
4859part 3
4860 
4861#marusya_likbez
4862 
4863Finally, my hands reached the point of writing to you about the most intriguing component of the egg for us, confectioners — egg white.
4864 
4865Egg white (albumin) is actually consist of four parts: an internal thick white, an internal thin white, an external thick white and an external thin white. These layers are designed to protect and cushion the chick embryo as it develops.
4866 
4867The white of a freshly laid egg will be turbid, not quite transparent. This is because it contains carbon dioxide, which slowly escapes through the shell as the egg ages, turning the white turbid color to transparent.
4868 
4869❗️The fresher the egg, the thicker and turbidier the protein.
4870 
4871All layers of the egg white become more watery as the egg ages. Therefore, the old egg will spread in a thin layer on the pan, while the fresh one will be fried in quite thick layer. Noticed?
4872 
4873In general, during storage of eggs, all nutrients gradually decompose into water and carbon dioxide, which eventually come out through the pores in the shell.
4874 
4875❗️This is why old eggs always weigh less than fresh ones.
4876 
4877Egg white contains proteins, as well as niacin, riboflavin, magnesium, potassium and sodium. It is not as nutritious as the yolk, but it has only about 17 calories and does not contain fat and cholesterol.
4878 
4879Proteins found in eggs:
4880 
4881✅ Ovomucin
4882 
4883🔸 Responsible for the elasticity of the egg — accordingly, it has a direct impact on the volume and stability of the meringue. It is ovomucin that helps to beat the egg whites into a lush mass.
4884 
4885🔸 In fresh protein, the structure of ovomucin is dense. However, during storage, it becomes less stable, as a result the meringues on stale eggs are not so elastic with a less lush structure.
4886 
4887✅Ovotransferrin and ovalbumin
4888 
4889🔸 Responsible for coagulation and heat resistance of the egg, so the older the egg, the more time it takes to cook it. If you use too old eggs, the meringue may not bake properly at all.
4890 
4891Now egg white powder — albumin becomes more and more popular. There are 3 types of albumin, but only one of them is ideal for confectionery purposes.
4892part 4
4893 
4894We had a look at the anatomy of an egg, but did not talk about the egg as a whole. If I'm not mistaken, I promised to dwell upon the shelf life of the eggs.
4895 
4896So, let's start!
4897 
4898❗️ Fresh eggs storage
4899 
4900🔺Commercial industry
4901🔸 Eggs are stored at room temperature as the use of refrigeration equipment is impractical (space, electricity, depreciation)
4902🔸 Shelf life - up to 25 days
4903🔸 After disinfection, eggs are stored for no more than a day
4904 
4905🔺 Storage at home
4906🔸 At room temperature - up to 25 days
4907🔸 Storage in the fridge at 4-6°C extends the shelf life to 90 days, at 0-2°C - up to 125 days
4908 
4909⚠️ Types of eggs by freshness
4910 
4911🔸 with a shelf life of no more than 7 days after the egg was laid
4912🔸 with a shelf life of more than 7 days after the egg was laid
4913 
4914❗️Egg white and egg mixture storage
4915 
4916🔸 in the fridge at 2-6°C for making custard - no more than 8 hours
4917🔸 in the fridge at 2-6°C for making baked goods and semi-finished products - no more than 24 hours
4918 
4919❗️Egg melange storage
4920 
4921🔸In the fridge at 2-6°C - no more than 8 hours
4922🔸 Egg melange can be frozen - it can be put in a blast freezer and then stored in a low-temperature chamber at -18°C for up to 15 months
4923🔸 Storage of the defrosted melange - no more than 4 hours in the fridge
4924🔸 Before using, is should be strained through a tinned metal or stainless steel sieve
4925 
4926❗️ Pasteurized eggs storage
4927 
4928🔸 Once opened, they should be stored in the fridge for no more than 3 days
4929 
4930❗️Powdered eggs storage
4931 
4932🔸 Shelf life - up to 6 months
4933🔸 Once diluted with water + 20°C, the shelf life is no more than 24 hours

4935⠀
4936❓If you still have questions about eggs, ask them in the comments, I’ll be happy to answer them.
4937 
4938⠀
4939 
4940part 5
4941 
4942The beginning can be found by hashtag
4943#marusya_likbez
4944 
4945Russian @ma_rusya_manko
4946 
4947Today we will talk about the disinfection of eggs. This topic is important and interesting for all pastry professionals: both home bakers and those who work in production.
4948 
4949First of all, it is worth noting that the disinfection of eggs at home and in production is very different.
4950 
4951✅ Egg disinfection in production
4952 
4953In production, eggs are disinfected in special rooms. First, the eggs are sorted, randomly ovoscoped (using a special device with a beam of light passing through the egg so that one is able to see what happens inside), and then transferred to 4-section baths for disinfection. In each section, the egg goes through a certain stage:
4954 
4955🔸 1st section - the eggs are placed in warm water for 5-10 minutes
4956🔸 2d section - disinfection with a 0.5% sodium carbonate solution (at 40-45 °C) for 5-10 minutes
4957🔸 3d section - disinfection with a 2% bleach solution or 0.5% chloramine solution for 5 minutes
4958🔸 4th section - the eggs are rinsed with running water for 5 minutes.
4959 
4960✅ Egg disinfection at home
4961 
4962There are a large number of disinfectants that are suitable for disinfecting eggs at home. For example, Nettex, Battles, Defra and others.
4963 
4964Regular washing with baking soda will not fully protect against the risk of salmonella infection. Moreover, such a procedure will destroy the protective shell of the egg, and the risk of pathogens entering will increase significantly.
4965 
4966In addition, when cooking curds, custards and egg white creams made from raw eggs, the temperature is not high enough to sterilize the eggs. Therefore, there is always a small, but still possible risk of getting infected with salmonella.
4967 
4968However, if you are very meticulous in this matter, there are 3 ways:
4969 
49701. Do not cook homemade custards and curds made from raw eggs.
49712. Always use disinfectants.
49723. Switch to pasteurized eggs or dried powder (dried egg white or egg powder).
4973part 6
4974 
4975#marusya_likbez
4976 
4977Today I’d like to explain to you how to work with dried egg powders.
4978 
4979Most often they are used in mass production, but some home bakers also tend to use powders in order to zero out the risk of salmonella infection when preparing frostings made from raw eggs.
4980 
4981In order to understand how to substitute a regular fresh egg for dried egg powder, you need to figure out how much dry matter the egg itself contains.
4982 
4983Standard dry matter content:
4984 
4985🔸 Whole egg (egg melange) - 27% dry matter
4986🔸 Egg yolk - 46% dry matter
4987🔸 Egg white - 12% dry matter
4988 
4989There is a formula by which you can calculate the required amount of dried egg powder (G₂):
4990 
4991⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀G₁*a₁
4992G₂ = ⠀__________
4993⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀a₂
4994 
4995G₁ is the mass of the product being substituted - for example, you need to substitute 2000 g of fresh eggs for dried egg powder
4996 
4997a₁ is the dry matter content of the product being substituted - as you now know, the whole egg contains 27% dry matter
4998 
4999a₂ - dry matter of the substitute product, that is, the powder itself - as a rule, the percentage of dry matter content is indicated on the packaging, ~ 94%
5000 
5001Thus, you get:
5002 
5003⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀2000 g * 27%
5004G₂ =⠀ _______________ = 574 g
5005⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀94%
5006 
5007That is, in order to substitute 2 kg of fresh eggs, you need 574 g of dried egg powder.
5008 
5009Lastly, it’s necessary to find out how much water is needed to dilute the powder:
5010 
5011G₁—G₂
5012 
50132000 g - 574 g = 1426 g
5014 
5015Easy as ABC 😉
5016 
5017If you need to substitute only the egg white or egg yolk, then use the percentage of dry matter in each of them (46% and 12%, correspondingly) in the formula and get the required amount of dried egg white or egg yolk.

5019part 1
5020 
5021Bucha

5028
