5034The report on the funds raised for charity, as previously, will be posted in the stories. We express our deepest gratitude to everyone who took part in the sale.
5035⠀
5036⠀
5037Do you use food colorings?
5038 
5039I'm not a big fan of using synthetic food colorings in large quantities for two reasons.
5040 
5041The first is harm to health due to questionable substances of chemical origin in the composition. No matter how manufacturers convince us of 100% safety, this, alas, is far from always the case.
5042 
5043The second reason is less obvious, but no less important for me — the psychological aspect of the perception of colors by our brain — dividing them into edible and inedible.
5044 
5045Our brain is designed in such a way that subconsciously we will avoid eating food that is too bright and flashy, especially of cool inedible shades.
5046 
5047Despite the above reasons, in general I have nothing against the wise use of colorings in pastry. Especially when the addition of a coloring of a certain hue allows the client to identify the dessert with the fruit used for its preparation.
5048 
5049For example, one can use a small amount of orange coloring for orange desserts or yellow for banana desserts.
5050 
5051Let's try together to sort out the huge number of different food colorings present in the market, in particular let’s figure out how and where they can be used.
5052 
5053So, colorings are usually classified according to:
5054 
5055🔺 composition: synthetic and natural;
5056🔺 form of production: powdered, gel and liquid;
5057🔺 chemical basis: water-soluble and fat-soluble.
5058PART 2
5059 
5060check out the beginning by the hashtag #marusya_foodcolorings
5061 
5062Today we’ll talk about the classification of food colorings by their composition. As I’ve said, they are natural and synthetic.
5063 
5064🌿 Natural
5065 
5066Originate from a wide range of natural sources, mainly wild and cultivated plants, as well as waste from their processing.
5067 
5068In the pastry art, a wide variety of natural food colorings is used:
5069 
5070🔸 Teas: green or blue matcha, hibiscus
5071🔸 Freeze-dried berry, fruit or vegetable powders
5072🔸 Powdered plant, vegetable or fruit juices
5073🔸 Algae and spice extracts
5074🔸 Vegetable charcoal
5075🔸 Fermented plant foods (e.g red rice)
5076 
5077Natural colorings are suitable for coloring dough, frosting, culinary mastic, sugar flowers, meringue and zephyr.
5078 
5079Pros:
5080➕ Contain only natural ingredients
5081➕ a confectionery product gains additional useful properties
5082 
5083Cons:
5084➖ relatively short shelf life
5085➖ may be subject to microbiological deterioration (formation of mold, fungus)
5086➖to obtain a rich color, a large amount of coloring is required
5087➖ the color is quite difficult to predict (if the same plants grow on different soils, different shades can be obtained)
5088 
5089🌿 Synthetic
5090 
5091Synthetic colorings are chemical compounds that are synthesized in laboratories and used to color various confectionery products, such as frostings, syrups, culinary mastic, sponge cakes, macarons and chocolate.
5092 
5093Pros:
5094➕ stable and predictable result
5095➕ long shelf life
5096➕ a wide range of colors, that allows you to create very bright, saturated shades with a small amount of coloring
5097➕ resistance to technological processing (the color won’t fade in the oven and retain its initial shade in frostings of various properties)
5098➕ neutral taste
5099 
5100Cons:
5101➖ deprived of useful properties
5102➖ contain substances of chemical origin in the composition
5103➖ stain the oral cavity
5104 
5105Personally, I use both synthetic and natural coloring, depending on the situation, but I try to give preference to natural ones.
5106PART 3
5107 
5108#marusya_foodcolorings
5109 
5110Speaking about colorings we’ve come to a very important classification into water- and fat-soluble: I will tell you how they differ and how to choose the right type.
5111 
5112🌿 Water-soluble
5113 
5114This type of colorings is used to color foods with a high water content.
5115 
5116🔺liquid water-soluble clorings are suitable for water-based glazes, marshmallows, zephyr or fruit pastes (pastila);
5117 
5118🔺 gel water-soluble colorings, in which the percentage of moisture is lower than that of liquid ones, can be used to color sponges, low-fat frostings, as well as to create all kinds of patterns on confectionery products by applying the colorings to a pastry bag;
5119 
5120🔺powder (dry) water-soluble colorings are suitable for coloring products that are highly sensitive to moisture: for example, meringues or macarons.
5121 
5122🌿 Fat-soluble
5123 
5124Fat-soluble colorings do an excellent job of coloring high-fat mixtures without forming lumps and curdling, giving the desired color to products.
5125 
5126Are a good choice for coloring chocolate used for decoration, creating velour mixtures, chocolates design, etc. Everything containing cocoa butter and chocolate requires the use of fat-soluble colorings.
5127 
5128🔺 Liquid / gel fat-soluble colorings are represented by a very poor color range. Therefore, compatible colorings were created (when mixed with a water-soluble coloring, they turn it into a fat-soluble one).
5129 
5130🔺 dry (powder) fat-soluble colorings are added to chocolate in dry form and should be thoroughly mixed or processed with an emulsion blender.
5131 
5132📍 Which coloring is better for chocolate: liquid or powder?
5133 
5134Definitely powder! The liquid will inevitably break the structure of cocoa butter, and you might encounter issues when tempering the mixture.
5135 
5136The powder coloring will not affect the mixture in any way, but the weight of the coloring cannot exceed 0.02% of the weight of the product being colored in order to avoid staining the oral cavity.
5137INTERCHANGEABILITY OF GLUCOSE, GLUCOSE-FRUCTOSE SYRUP AND TRIMOLINE
5138 
5139#marusya_theory_tips
5140 
5141Almost every day I receive DMs with your requests for a piece of advice on this issue. Let’s figure it out in detail❤️
5142 
5143The main component in the carbohydrate composition of glucose syrup is glucose. Glucose-fructose syrup usually contains 50% glucose and 50% fructose. These syrups are almost 100% interchangeable.
5144 
5145The sweetness value of syrups is much lower than that of sugar. Just to compare: sugar has a coefficient of 1, while glucose-fructose syrup has a coefficient of 0.6-0.8 (depending on the amount of water in the composition).
5146 
5147📍 Glucose-fructose syrup is also an artificial substitute for honey. The only difference between honey and glucose-fructose syrup is that honey contains pollen and other substances carried by bees that can cause an allergic reaction.
5148 
5149Trimoline (inverted sugar) has a pasty consistency and looks like candied white honey. Its carbohydrate composition is also represented by glucose and fructose, but the percentage of water is significantly lower than that of glucose-fructose syrup.
5150 
5151Thus, the sweetness value of trimoline is much higher than that of sugar. As I’ve already mentioned, sugar has a coefficient of 1, while trimoline has a coefficient of 1.2-1.4 (depending on the amount of water in the composition).
5152 
5153Glucose-fructose syrup can not always be substituted for trimoline.
5154 
5155You can safely replace one with another if you are making ganache, frosting or chocolates filling. But if you are making various kinds of caramels, syrups or zephyr, meaning the products exposed to high temperatures during cooking, then the glucose cannot be substituted for trimoline due to its sensitivity to high temperatures - at temperatures above 100 °C it can have an unpleasant burnt smell and taste.
5156 
5157📍 When adding trimoline to baked goods (puffs, croissants, etc.), the baking temperature is usually reduced by 10 °C to avoid excessive browning.
5158ABOUT TASTES AND HOW THEY ARE CREATED
5159A basic knowledge of primary flavors and how they work is essential in the culinary arts.
5160 
5161Unfortunately, among amateurs, few people have this knowledge and even less consciously use it, combining ingredients and adding spices rather intuitively.
5162 
5163For example, when asked why I always add salt to honey dough, none of my students answered correctly.
5164 
5165Why do French chefs teach to cook bitter caramel, why salted caramel is tastier than unsalted, how to reduce the sweetness of a dessert without reducing the amount of sugar at the same time? — answers to all these, as well as many other questions, can be obtained with basic knowledge of flavors.
5166 
5167So, I propose to sort out the basic flavors, as well as talk in detail about how we perceive them and what influences our perception.
5168 
5169Our taste buds are able to perceive only 4 basic flavors: sweet, salty, sour and bitter. The art of culinary is to bring these 4 components into a balanced harmony and create a delicious taste.
5170 
5171It is equally simple and complex. After all, not only taste buds are responsible for the perception of taste, but also smell, touch, sight and hearing, including our emotions, thoughts and mood.
5172 
5173TASTE = TASTE QUALITY + TASTE IMPRESSION + AROMA + UNKNOWN "FACTOR X"
5174 
5175Taste quality is what is perceived by the taste buds.
5176 
5177A taste impression is what is perceived by the rest of the mouth.
5178 
5179Aroma is what is perceived by the sense of smell.
5180 
5181The unknown “X factor” is what is perceived by other senses, as well as by the heart, mind and spirit.

5193PART 2
5194 
5195#marusya_flavours
5196 
5197Judging by the number of "5" under the previous post, the topic turned out to be interesting for you. Knowing tastes and having the ability to understand and combine them skillfully is incredibly important both in creating desserts and meals in your kitchen!
5198 
5199As promised, today I will dwell upon the 4 main tastes.
5200 
5201⚠️ Sweet taste
5202 
5203To act on our receptors, a large amount of a sweet substance is needed, unlike other tastes. Nevertheless, we can appreciate the harmony and pleasant taste that even a subtle sweet taste gives to spicy dishes.
5204 
5205Sweet taste is combined with bitter (chocolate), sour (berries, lemon) and even salty (caramel). Sweetness can also bring out the flavors of other ingredients, like fruit or mint.
5206 
5207⚠️ Salty taste
5208 
5209Salt is a natural flavor enhancer and the most important ingredient for making spicy food delicious. By the way, sugar plays this role in desserts.
5210 
5211⚠️ Sour taste
5212 
5213As flavor enhancers, acidic components are second in importance only to salt in spicy dishes and sugar in sweet ones. Sour notes - whether it be a little lemon juice or vinegar - add brightness to the dish. The harmonious combination of sourness with other flavors is crucial to the finished dish.
5214 
5215⚠️ Bitter taste
5216 
5217Humans are most sensitive to bitter taste, and our body's defense mechanisms allow it to be recognized even in small amounts.
5218 
5219The bitter taste is harmoniously combined with the sweet, and sometimes can significantly reduce the richness of the dish. For some chefs, the bitter taste is more important than others, but some see it as a necessary savory flavor - one that makes you take another bite, and then another.
5220 
5221Every delicious dish you've ever tasted has been the result of these 4 tastes acting on your taste buds. We distinguish them both individually and in combination.
5222part 3
5223 
5224the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours
5225 
5226We continue to talk about tastes. Today I will tell you about the fifth little-known taste of umami, and also we will deal with such a concept as "taste perception".
5227 
5228⚠️ Umami (found in high-protein foods)
5229 
5230In addition to the four basic tastes, a growing body of evidence points to the existence of a fifth taste, umami.
5231 
5232It gives dishes a salty or meaty "rich" taste. It can be felt in ingredients such as anchovies, cheese, mushrooms, green tea, and flavorings such as monosodium glutamate, which is the main component of ready-made seasonings.
5233 
5234✔️TASTE PERCEPTION
5235Temperature, texture, piquancy, astringent taste.
5236 
5237In the oral cavity, in addition to taste, there are tactile receptors sensitive to temperature and texture, which are also very important in determining taste. Such perception of food is usually called taste perception.
5238 
5239They help our body digest food, get the most nutrients and pleasure from it. A crunchy dish should be appealing in both sound and texture.
5240 
5241⚠️ Temperature
5242 
5243Temperature is the main sensation perceived by the oral cavity. The temperature of food even affects how we perceive its taste. For example, cool temperatures suppress the appeal of sweetness.
5244 
5245The temperature of food can affect both how it is perceived and how much you enjoy it. The menu is always dominated by hot dishes in winter and chilled ones on hot summer days, precisely because they balance the temperature of our body and the environment.
5246 
5247⚠️ Texture
5248 
5249The texture of food plays a crucial role in its ability to attract and please. We consider mashed and/or creamy foods (soup, mashed potatoes) traditional while crunchy food is considered fun.
5250 
5251We like texture because it activates the senses of touch, sight, and sound.
5252⠀
5253⠀
5254part 4
5255 
5256the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours
5257 
5258Thank you for supporting new posts about tastes with likes and comments! I see that you’re involved and interested and it inspires me to write a continuation.
5259 
5260So, in addition to temperature and texture, the following 2 factors also affect the TASTE PERCEPTION:
5261 
5262⚠️ Piquancy
5263 
5264Our taste buds can also perceive what we mistakenly call spicy, meaning piquant taste, that is more pronounced in chili peppers and barely noticeable in cayenne pepper powder. Some prefer the piquant taste to others.
5265 
5266⚠️ Astringent taste
5267 
5268Our taste buds also recognize astringency, a dry mouth sensation caused by the tannin content in tea or wine, and sometimes cranberries, unripe persimmons, and certain types of nuts.
5269 
5270✔️ AROMA
5271 
5272Taste balance largely depends on the aroma. It is believed that it is the aroma that mainly determines the taste of the dish. This explains the popularity of such flavoring ingredients as fresh herbs, spices or lemon zest.
5273 
5274Adding flavoring ingredients will enhance the aroma of the dish and improve its taste.
5275 
5276Some foods are perceived with both our taste buds and our sense of smell.
5277 
5278⚠️ Strong spicy taste
5279 
5280Ingredients such as horseradish and mustard have a strong spicy taste and aroma. However, despite their piquancy, their taste and aroma irritate the receptors of both the nasal and oral cavities.
5281 
5282⚠️ Chemical senses
5283 
5284The term “chemical senses” refers to taste sensations that are pleasant to us (for example, the tingling sensation when drinking sparkling drinks) and at the same time irritate or deceive the taste buds (for example, chili pepper creates a false sensation of burning, while peppermint has a cooling effect).

5291Food has an effect on our entire body. We perceive it not only with the help of the 5 senses, but also emotionally, mentally and even spiritually.
5292 
5293Among the 5 senses, the first we resort to is sight. The visual representation of a meal can greatly enhance the enjoyment we derive from it.

5308PART 6
5309 
5310the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours
5311 
5312Let's talk more about FLAVOR today.
5313 
5314Why is flavor so important when it comes to pairing ingredients?
5315 
5316Flavor is a combination of taste that is perceived by taste buds and smell. It is assumed that in 90% of cases the taste we perceive is actually flavor!
5317 
5318For example, when you eat a pineapple, the flavor is actually perceived through the sense of smell. But if the pineapple is not ripe, it won’t be as flavorful. It may taste sweet, but it will no longer taste like pineapple.
5319 
5320There are two ways to add flavor to a dish: through aroma or a chemical reaction. In fact, culinary art is nothing more than a laboratory experiment: the moment a raw product is cooked, its state changes.
5321 
5322For example, when roasting meat, carbohydrates and amino acids interact with each other, which leads to caramelization of the product. This chemical reaction is called the Maillard reaction, and it contributes to the manifestation of flavor.
5323 
5324The aroma tends to disappear. If you enter the kitchen and you feel that it smells good, then the dish itself has already lost most of its flavor. The aroma has dispersed in the air.
5325 
5326To give the dish some flavor and keep it, it is best to use the low-temperature cooking method (products are packed in vacuum bags and cooked at a low temperature).
5327This method helps to preserve the flavor without letting it fade, but unfortunately it’s not suitable for home cooking.
5328 
5329Airtight cooking may do better for home conditions - put the product you want to cook in a ziploc bag filled with some water (if it is supposed to be cooked in the oven) or without it (if you’re going to cook it in a pot with water).
5330 
5331Meat cooked in this way, especially oven-baked ham, is very tasty and flavorful.
5332part 7
5333 
5334the beginning can be found by the hashtag #marusya_flavours
5335 
5336Why do some ingredients pair especially well together and others don't? This is a question that no doubt preoccupied many in the food industry to the point of keeping them awake at night.
5337 
5338A scientific explanation for this phenomenon was given by Bernard Lahousse in 2005. At that time, he actively collaborated with Michelin-starred chefs Korean Sang-Hoon Degeimbre and Belgian Kobe Desramaults.
5339 
5340They met regularly and discussed potential menu items they had in works. At one such meeting, Sang-Hoon asked, ‘Bernard, why is it that when I smell the kiwis, I also smell the sea? Is that possible?’
5341 
5342This question interested Bernard so much that he borrowed a mass spectrometer and a gas chromatograph from his scientist colleague and began to analyze the volatile substances that kiwis release into the air, thereby creating the recognizable aroma of an exotic fruit.
5343 
5344As it turns out, in addition to the fruity esters, kiwis also contain concentrations of green, grassy, ​​and fatty-scented aldehydes, which have a marine-like scent similar to that of oysters and other types of shellfish.
5345 
5346The discovery of an aromatic link between these two seemingly completely unrelated ingredients formed the basis for the very first scientific foodpairing, thus revealing to the world kiwitre, Chef Degeimbre's creation, which has since become a signature dish on the menu of L'Air du Temps.
5347 
5348Kiwitre is a dish in which a raw oyster is served over diced kiwi with croutons and lime-infused coconut cream.
5349 
5350Thus, the scientific foodpairing is based on the fact that the ingredients in order to combine well must contain the same aroma molecules, like kiwi and oysters, for example, share the same marine-like aroma note.
5351 
5352If you have read the article to the end, please, leave any emoji in the comments ❤️
5353 
5354Want to learn more about aroma molecules, key aromas, the main aroma types and how it all works?
5355WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GELATIN
5356 
5357#marusya_about_gelatin
5358 
5359The question of whether it is possible to boil gelatin provokes a lot of controversy and contradictions: some say that it can be done, others - by no means. Let's look into this issue.
5360 
5361So, first of all, I’d like to say right away that GELATIN CAN BE BOILED! However, not in all cases it is necessary to do this - it all depends on how and what you're using it for.
5362 
5363Let's have a look at a few cases.
5364 
53651️⃣ High concentration of gelatin in liquid
5366 
5367Let's take a no-bake cheesecake as an example. For its preparation, gelatin is diluted in a small amount of water, and then mixed with cream cheese or whipping cream. The gelatin mass turns out to be thick and viscous, and, accordingly, if heated for a long time and strongly, it:
5368 
5369🔸 may set too quickly
5370🔸 may clump up
5371 
5372Therefore, with a high concentration of gelatin in the liquid, it is not recommended to heat the mass for longer than it takes the gelatine to dissolve completely.
5373 
5374‼️ATTENTION‼️ If you still have brought the gelatin mass to a boil, do not throw it away - just let it cool down a little.
5375 
53762️⃣ Low concentration of gelatin in liquid
5377 
5378If you need to jellify the juice or fruit puree, you can safely bring the gelatin to a boil. The jellified mass will turn out to be quite runny, so there’s almost no risk of rapid setting and the formation of lumps.
5379 
5380However, do not forget that the liquid will boil down and the concentration of gelatin will increase, so keep the mass boiling for literally 1-2 minutes, no more. Excessive concentration of gelatin can negatively affect the taste, making the jelly too thick, and the gelatin flavor more pronounced.
5381 
53823️⃣ Making the jelly from raw materials of animal origin
5383 
5384If the jelly is made from fish or meat broth, it must be simmered over low heat so that there are no large bubbles, otherwise it won't be clear. But it is not the gelatin that becomes cloudy, but the broth itself!
5385 
5386You can safely use all of the above rules, but it should be remembered that the strength of gelatin is significantly reduced in an acidic environment (pH < 5) and in the presence of protease enzymes.
5387We’ve already talked about what gelatin is, its usage, types, as well as forms of gelatin and its strength. The beginning of the article can be found by the hashtag #marusya_about_gelatin
5388 
5389Today I suggest talking about conversion between different strengths of gelatin.
5390 
5391When you’re going to prepare any pastry product that contains gelatin, it is important to understand that its consistency and texture depends not only on the QUANTITY of the gelatin added, but also on its STRENGTH.
5392 
5393And if the strength of the gelatin in the recipe is different from the strength of the gelatin you use, there is a very high risk of ending up with an unstable confit, which flows out of the cake, or a too thick cheesecake ...
5394 
5395There are 2 ways of getting a stable, predictable result:
5396 
53971️⃣ Use gelatin with the same strength as indicated in the recipe
53982️⃣ Use any gelatin that you have on hand, converting between different strengths
5399 
5400‼️HERE’S THE FORMULA:
5401 
5402In order to convert between different strengths of gelatin, you need to divide the strength of the gelatin used in the recipe by the strength of your gelatin.
5403 
5404G1÷G2
5405 
5406You will get the coefficient by which you need to multiply the weight of gelatin, indicated in the recipe.
5407 
5408For example, a recipe uses 10 g of gelatin Dr. Oetker with a strength of 220 bloom. And you have gelatin with a strength of 180 bloom.
5409 
5410Calculate the coefficient:
5411 
5412220/180 = 1.22
5413 
5414Then multiply this coefficient by the weight of the gelatin in the recipe: 10 g x 1.22 = 12.2 g
5415 
5416If the strength of your gelatin is greater than in the recipe, you will get a coefficient which is less than one. This means that you will need to use a smaller amount of gelatin.
5417 
5418As you can see, conversion is quite simple to do, and at the same time, it helps to avoid undesirable difficulties when working with the textures of your fillings, cakes and desserts..
5419SHELF LIFE OF FROSTING
5420 
5421I hear this question all the time: both at live master classes, and at my online courses, as well as from clients who contact me in DM. The question is relevant, interesting and, without a doubt, extremely important for any pastry chef and everyone who just loves to bake.
5422 
5423✅ Making the frosting
5424 
5425🔸 I always tell my students that making the frosting is an integral part of assembling a cake.
5426 
5427Such frostings as buttercream, cream cheese frosting, custard and frosting made from egg whites, should be used immediately after their preparation.
5428 
5429‼️ THEY CANNOT BE STORED AND MUST NOT BE PREPARED IN ADVANCE.
5430 
5431🔸 Other types of frosting can be stored in the fridge at 2-6 °C for no more than 5 hours. After that, they must be used for coating and decorating a cake.
5432 
5433✅ Storage of frosting during coating and decorating
5434 
5435It should be remembered that:
5436 
5437🔸 From the moment you start applying the frosting to a dessert, you have no more than 1.5 hours - this is the period set for working with frosting at the workplace.
5438🔸 If you take breaks during this process, the piping bags filled with frosting should be put in a clean container and placed in the fridge till the end of the break.
5439 
5440✅ Storage of finished desserts with frosting
5441 
5442🔸 The container in which you place the pastry product must be clean, dry and without any foreign odors.
5443🔸 All finished pastry products should be stored in the fridge at 2-6 °C.
5444🔸 Shelf life of finished products according to governmental standard:
5445 
5446🔺 with frosting made from egg whites - no more than 72 hours;
5447🔺 with buttercream - no more than 36 hours;
5448🔺 with cream cheese - no more than 24 hours;
5449🔺 with custard - no more than 12 hours;
5450🔺 with crème fraîche or sour cream - no more than 6 hours.
5451CONVERSION OF RECIPES FOR ANY SIZE CAKE MOLDS
5452 
5453Every day I receive a lot of photo reviews about cheesecakes prepared using my recipes: you comment on their mind-blowing tastes, their beautiful appearance, the balance of flavors and textures....
5454 
5455But as soon as it comes to the slightest change in the recipe, such as using a mold of a different diameter, people often get confused and ask me to recalculate the ingredients to fit their mold.
5456 
5457I always try to answer all the questions you have. But I would really like each of you to understand how this can be done on your own, because in fact, if you know the conversion formula, it will be easy as pie.
5458 
5459‼️SO, HERE’S THE FORMULA:
5460 
5461In order to convert the recipe for another size mold, you need to divide the square of the diameter of the larger mold by the square of the diameter of the smaller mold.
5462 
5463d1²÷d2²
5464 
5465And you will get a number by which we need to multiply or divide all the ingredients that appear in the recipe.
5466 
5467For example, the recipe requires a mold of 18 cm in diameter. But you only have a mold of 20 cm in diameter at hand.
5468 
5469You calculate the coefficient:
5470 
547120*20 = 400
547218*18=324
5473 
5474400/324 = 1.23
5475 
5476Since you have a larger mold than indicated in the recipe, you MULTIPLY the amount of all ingredients by 1.23.
5477 
5478If, on the contrary, the mold in the recipe is larger, and yours is smaller, then you DIVIDE by this coefficient.
5479 
5480As you can see, the conversion is not difficult at all.
5481 
5482If this post was helpful to you, be sure to let me know with a ❤️ or a comment.
5483 
5484P.S. For those to whom all this simple mathematics still seems complicated for some reason, I’ve added a table with ready-made coefficients in the carousel, which is available on the Internet 🥰
5485 
5486➡️ Horizontally - the diameter of the form in the recipe, cm
5487⬆️ Vertical - the diameter of your form, cm
5488CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS
5489 
5490The leaking liquid from the assembled cake. Where does it come from?
5491 
5492In fact, in 98% of cases, the reason is very simple. It’s all about whey - the liquid residue of cheese, casein and yoghurt production.
5493⠀
5494It appears especially if dairy products do not contain thickeners and stabilizers, in the event of a violation of the storage temperature and a number of other factors.
5495⠀
5496❗️ My first leaking cake was a honey cake. In frosting recipe I replaced the sour cream with Greek yogurt. The whey flooded the entire cake base and part of the fridge. Can you imagine how wet the cake was inside?
5497⠀
5498Therefore, when asked whether it is possible to use yogurt instead of sour cream for frosting, I say"NO"! ❌
5499⠀
5500The frosting with the addition of even a small amount of yogurt (Greek or Turkish 10-12%) seems to be absolutely stable at first, but over time it liquefies a lot and becomes almost runny. It might be used in sponge cakes if they are not soaked at all, but for honey cakes this option won’t work at all.
5501⠀
5502❗️ The second cake leaked because of the poor quality sour cream - either the storage conditions or the process of preparation were violated.
5503⠀
5504The remains of sour cream and frosting having spent some time in the fridge released a huge amount of whey. It’s logical that the same thing happened with the frosting in the cake - the sponge layers absorbed most of it, but some of it leaked onto the cake base.
5505 
5506❗️ In the third case, the cream cheese was to blame. I usually use Cremette, which contains thickeners and stabilizers, so it releases a small amount of whey.
5507 
5508But one day, for lack of Cremette, I assembled the cake with another cream cheese, which contained neither thickeners nor stabilizers. Also, most likely, the technology of its preparation was also different. Now I understand why it cost almost half the price of Cremette.
5509 
5510That cream cheese released 3-4 times more whey than Cremette. As a result, in the morning there were several tablespoons of sweet liquid on the cake base.
5511CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS
5512 
5513Last couple of words about putting the press on the cake and we’ll proceed to the last part of the article - leaking liquid from the assembled cake.⠀
5514⠀
5515The cake won’t become less airy because of the press, believe me. A properly baked sponge should bounce well, that is, if you press on it, it perfectly returns to its original shape, so a press placed on top of a thick cake base does not affect it.
5516⠀
5517The same can be said about the fluffiness of the frosting. Micro air bubbles inside the frosting won’t be squeezed out of the cake in any way. The press only helps to get rid of large hollows inside the cake.
5518⠀
5519By the way, getting an ideal cut of a honey cake without a press can be a challenging task - the edges of the cake layers will always be slightly bent downwards.
5520⠀
5521Do I put weight on my cakes? Very rarely, to be honest. I simplified this process and quickly tamp the cakes tightly immediately after assembly.
5522⠀
5523❌ The last part of the article is leaking liquid from the assembled cake.
5524⠀
5525To my mind, very few of you have faced this problem. For example, in my entire practice, the cakes leaked only three times.
5526⠀
5527I really wanted to show you a photo that my student once sent me, but I had to give up this idea, since it is impossible to find the right one among the thousands of photos.
5528⠀
5529Therefore, at this point, imagine a coated smoothed cake with a pool of liquid leaked from it onto the cake base.
5530⠀
5531Why does this happen? Logically, due to the large amount of liquid in the cake. But where does it come from?
5532HOW TO MAKE A PERFECT CHEESECAKE
5533⠀ℹ️ If you open the oven, the cheesecake will collapse.
5534⠀
5535Stupidity! The cheesecake shouldn't rise at all. If it rises, it means that the temperature that you set for it is too high.
5536⠀
5537⠀
5538ℹ️ Never open the oven while the cheesecake is baking.
5539⠀
5540Also wrong. If the cheesecake is baking at low temperatures, then nothing bad will happen if you open the oven.
5541⠀
5542⠀
5543ℹ️ On average, any cheesecake should be baked for at least 1.5-2.0 hours.
5544⠀
5545In fact, the baking time will depend on many factors — the characteristics of your oven, the temperature you set (90-150 °C / 194-302 °F), the height of the cheesecake (4-6 cm), taste (chocolate, caramel, berry or fruit), and also the number of cheesecakes (two cheesecakes in the oven will bake 20-30 minutes longer, for example).
5546⠀
5547That's why there is only one way out — to check its readiness using a piece of advice from the next paragraph. Usually, a cheesecake with a diameter of 16-18 cm and a height of 5-6 cm is baked for about 100 minutes.
5548⠀
5549⠀
5550ℹ️ How to check the doneness of a cheesecake?
5551⠀
5552By touching the cheesecake’s top with your finger. It should remain clean, while the middle of the cheese should slightly jiggle, like jelly.
5553⠀
5554⠀
5555✅ Do not worry if some butter leaks onto the baking tray during baking. You used it to grease the ring, and it’s natural that it melts at high temperatures. It doesn’t affect the texture and taste of the base at all.
5556⠀
5557✅ Turn off the oven, slightly open the door and leave the cheesecake to cool down inside for about 30-40 minutes. Then take the cheesecake out of the oven and leave it at room temperature until it cools down completely. After that, put it in the fridge for 6-12 hours.
5558⠀
5559⠀
5560⚠️ If you put a still warm cheesecake in the fridge, the condensation may form and the base will soak from moisture.
5561⠀
5562⠀
5563✅ Coat the cheesecake with a variety of fillings and décor only after it has completely cooled down (at least 6 hours).
5564 
5565Now there are only a few questions left to deal with:
5566⠀
5567ℹ️ What can be used to decorate the cheesecake?
5568⠀
5569✔️ fresh berries, fruits
5570✔️ coulis, confit, berry / fruit sauces, jellies, etc., in short, everything that can be made from fruits / berries and their puree with the addition of a thickener
5571✔️ regular or whipped ganache
5572✔️ regular or whipped caramel
5573✔️ nuts, meringue, creams, namelaka, cremeux, gels, glazes, etc.
5574⠀
5575ℹ️ How long does a cheesecake last?
5576⠀
5577The shelf life of any product depends on the way it was made, the products used in it and the storage temperature. That’s why the only thing that I can say is that on average, the shelf life of baked cheesecakes is no more than 3-4 days in the fridge. Then, how long you will be able to resist this sweet temptation is up to you.
5578⠀
5579ℹ️ Can cheesecakes be frozen?
5580⠀
5581If it is dry and there are no foreign odors in the freezer, you can freeze a cheesecake and store it for not more than 20-30 days.
5582⠀
5583✅ I don't advise freezing cheesecakes if you are unsure about your freezer, but if you need to freeze a cheesecake without a cake base, make sure to wrap it in plastic wrap.
5584⠀
5585✅ You can turn any large cheesecake into individual cakes, and vice versa. Just keep in mind that the baking time will change. For individual cheesecakes it’s 50-60 minutes maximum at the temperature that is 10-20 °C / 50-68 °F lower than usual.
5586⠀
5587⠀
5588ℹ️ What texture does a perfect cheesecake have?
5589⠀
5590It’s like with zefir – everyone has their own preferences. First of all, YOU should like the cheesecake! It all depends solely on your taste and nothing else!
5591 
5592WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GELATIN
5593⠀
5594At first glance, the topic is very simple and there is not much to learn about, but, in fact, it’s not that easy.
5595⠀
5596Gelatin (French gélatine, from Latin gelātus "frozen") is a mixture of peptides and proteins produced by partial hydrolysis of collagen extracted from the skin, bones and connective tissues of animals. The collagen (protein) in these tissues is converted to gluten.
5597⠀
5598Gelatin is used in many areas. If we talk about cooking, then it is used to make jelly, confit, mousse, soufflé, marshmallows and so on.
5599⠀
5600Thanks to it, the mousse cakes keep their shape and tender creams in eclairs are not runny. Gelatin is also used for the production of ice cream to prevent sugar crystallization and reduce protein coagulation.
5601⠀
5602Edible gelatin used to be a product of animal origin, but with the development of the technologies there have appeared different variations of gelatin that no one could think about.
5603⠀
5604Notice, now gelatin can be made using a by-products of:⠀
5605 
5606📌beef
5607📌pork
5608📌fish
5609📌vegetables❗️
5610⠀
5611You will also find halal and kosher gelatin.
5612⠀
5613🌱 Types of gelatin
5614⠀
5615There are two types of gelatin – powdered and sheet.
5616⠀
5617📌 POWDERED
5618⠀
5619It is always soaked in cold water in a ratio of 1: 5 or, depending on the strength of the gelatin, 1: 6.
5620⠀
5621Mostly, it’s "instant gelatin" – it takes just a few minutes for it to swell. Then it is heated until it is completely dissolved.
5622⠀
5623It is important that the liquid to which the gelatin is added is warm, so that the gelatin disperses well in it, and does not form a huge lump.
5624⠀
5625Sometimes it is difficult and irrational to heat up the entire mousse or cream. For example, if it's a mousse made with cold sour cream, then a part of it, no more than 1/3, is taken, slightly heated over a water bath or in a microwave, then the gelatin is added to it, mixed well, and then both mixtures – the warm with gelatin and the cold one – are combined.
5626CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS
5627 
5628How to avoid air bubbles?
5629 
5630I’m sure you sometimes come across photos of assembled cakes with packs of butter or cream on top.
5631⠀
5632I hope you’ve already guessed why this is necessary, but in a few words I’ll explain what happens.
5633⠀
5634Being pressed down by some weight, all the air comes out of the cake, and in this way you let the layers tightly stick together for a more secure transportation of the product.
5635 
5636So let's see whether it is obligatory to put a weight on a cake, or such an idea is completely meaningless?
5637⠀
5638Let's start with where we started.
5639⠀
5640What to do if air bubbles appear on the side of the cake over time? Answer: put on a press!
5641⠀
5642There are some simple rules for placing a cake under the press:
5643⠀
5644📌 the press does not have to be put on for long - several hours will be quite enough, depending on the thickness of your cake and the weight of the press itself
5645⠀
5646📌 the press is never put directly on the cake, but always on a thick cake base to evenly distribute pressure over the entire surface
5647⠀
5648📌 the press should be put only on the cake which is tightly tightened with the acetate film, or, better with a cake ring for assembling.
5649⠀
5650📌 It’s better not to use a press for cakes with very delicate and moist fillings.
5651⠀
5652⠀
5653➕ Pros of putting a cake under the press:
5654⠀
5655✔️ no air inside the cake and, as a result, no bubbles on coating
5656✔️ perfectly flat cake surface, which greatly facilitates the smoothing process
5657✔️ perfectly even layers in the cut, which guarantees a wow effect when cutting
5658⠀
5659➖ Cons of putting a cake under the press:
5660⠀
5661✔️ if you put a too heavy press / leave the press for a long time / put the press on a very delicate filling, then you can end up with a too tamped cake.
5662⠀
5663⠀CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS
5664\Yesterday we discussed the possible reasons why the filling may get out of the cake in the form of bubbles on the coating:
5665⠀
5666❌ not verified, inaccurate recipe
5667❌ you deviated from the cooking instructions
5668❌ incorrect assembly of the cake.
5669⠀
5670But what if you are 200% confident in your filling and, nevertheless, a tricky bubble still has appeared on your finished cake? Or, let’s say, you have pierced the bubble and found that ... there is nothing there! 😳
5671⠀
5672But it cannot be empty, can it? There should have been something inside that pushed the buttercream and formed the bubble! And if this is not a filling, then, of course, it is ... air!
5673⠀
5674Let's figure out where this air comes from, what it does inside the cake and why it starts to get out at the most inopportune moment.
5675⠀
5676Air gets inside the cake, naturally, during the assembly process. When we assemble a cake, especially without a cake ring, we very carefully lower each sponge layer on top of the filling with frosting, trying not to press too much, otherwise we risk squeezing the filling with the frosting out.
5677⠀
5678It is quite natural that many hollows, filled with air, remain inside the cake. What happens next? In the process of maturing, thanks to the gravity, our cake "shrinks", pushing all the air out.
5679⠀
5680But it often happens that not all the air has time to get out and some of it remains inside the cake, looking for a perfect moment to put a pastry chef on edge. Usually, because it tries to get out when the chef is about to give the cake to the client.
5681⠀
5682A similar problem often happens with sponge cakes, especially if we don’t give the assembled cake enough time to rest before applying the coating.
5683⠀CAKE COATING. COMMON PROBLEMS
5684 
5685 
5686⠀
5687In the previous part of the article, we began to analyze the reasons why the filling may begin to get out in the form of bubbles on the side surface of the coating.⠀
5688⠀
5689So let's continue.
5690 
5691❌ You deviated from the cooking instructions. In this case you are to be blamed, if the first point has not been violated, that is, everything is alright with the recipe.
5692 
5693Probably, you didn’t reach the desired cooking temperature or didn’t give the filling enough time to stabilize. By the way, some chefs do not mention the latter in their recipes, taking it for granted.
5694⠀
5695Remember, most fillings require not only complete cooling, but also at least 6-8 extra hours for full stabilization. In some cases (with whipped ganache, for example), it takes at least 12 hours to stabilize in the fridge.
5696 
5697❌ incorrect assembly of the cake. Some recipes call for liquid fillings in the form of sauces, which flow seductively out of the cake when it’s cut.
5698⠀
5699How to ensure that such a filling remains inside the cake without leaking from it before the cake is cut?
5700⠀
5701Only by assembling the cake in the right way. Firstly, you should make a border of frosting for such a filling at least 2.0-3.0 cm thick. Secondly, it is better to use the same buttercream for the border with which you will coat the cake, that is, more stable, made with butter or chocolate ...
5702⠀⠀
5703In the continuation of the article, I will tell you about what else can cause bubbles, as well as why this happens and how to avoid such incidents. 🥰
5704⠀⠀
5705... Quite often, by the way, it is the filling itself that tries to get out and this can happen for several reasons:
5706⠀
5707❌ not verified, inaccurate or simply violated recipe. This could be a recipe author's mistake, or your own.
5708⠀
5709In the first case, I advise you to never ❗️ cook to order using a new recipe for the first time, no matter how much you trust its author. Think about your nerves.
5710⠀
5711Prepare at least half of the portion first. See how the ingredients behave in the process of cooking. Because the same, even seemingly most common products, such as butter and flour of different brands, can have completely different properties and characteristics.
5712⠀
5713You may make a mistake by allowing yourself small deviations from the recipe, in some cases, without even noticing it at all. How is this possible? I’ll tell you.
5714⠀
5715If you are a novice pastry chef, an amateaur, then it is likely that you do not understand much about the power of gel, collagen, or different types of pectin.
5716⠀
5717For example, in the recipe after the word "pectin" there are two capital letters NH, which tell you absolutely nothing. And you naively think that there is no difference between types of pectin and you put the one that you have at hand. The same may happen with agar-agar and gelatin. Then don't be surprised at the fillings that want to get out of your cake.
5718⠀
5719❌ You deviated from the cooking instructions. It's your fault if...
5720So, the first problem which we’ve already discussed was cracked coating.
5721⠀
5722How can you avoid this?
5723⠀
5724✅ do not violate the technological process and coat the cake with buttercream no earlier than 10-12 hours after assembly, especially if it’s a sponge cake
5725⠀
5726✅ do not make the buttercream layer too thin, unless it is required by the design
5727⠀
5728✅ use foolproof recipes in which the precise amount of frosting and soaking syrup sufficient to soak the layers are indicated
5729⠀
5730⠀
5731The second common problem that many pastry chefs might have faced is bubbles on the coated cake.
5732⠀
5733What’s that, what does it look like, why does it happen and how to deal with it?
5734⠀
5735It happens that you make a cake, keep it in the fridge, everything seems to be as it should be. You coat it with buttercream, decorate, and are waiting for the customer. And then 15-20 minutes before their arrival, you open the fridge, and, gosh, you see a large "tumor" that has formed on the perfectly smooth surface of the cake, as if something is trying to get out of the filling.
5736⠀
5737Well, it is. Something is trying to get out, but what? ⠀
5738 
5739The first problem is a cracked coating. Why does this happen?
5740⠀
5741Cracks in the buttercream appear due to the fact that the cake layers have absorbed a lot of moisture from the buttercream, it became too dry and cracked.
5742⠀
5743The most common causes of cracking are:
5744⠀
5745📌 coating the cake a few hours after assembly
5746⠀
5747Remember, the technological process of making cakes requires keeping the cake after assembly for at least 10-12 hours in the fridge before proceeding with the coating.
5748⠀
5749Why? First, the fillings and the frosting need to stabilize and second, which is more important, the cake needs to mature – the cake layers need to soak with frosting.
5750⠀
5751I prefer to leave the cake in the fridge overnight after assembly and coat it with buttercream only the next day. Thus, the layers will be perfectly soaked and will no longer absorb moisture from the buttercream.
5752⠀
5753⠀
5754📌 too thin layer of coating
5755⠀
5756Even if you left the cake in the fridge overnight and it soaked well, parts of the layers that do not come into direct contact with the frosting inside the cake (sides of the layers and the top layer) can still absorb some moisture from the buttercream.
5757⠀
5758If the coating is very thin and the layers are not sufficiently soaked, it results in cracks on the coating.
5759⠀
5760⠀
5761📌 insufficient amount of frosting or soaking syrup in the recipe
5762⠀
5763Finally, the third option that may cause cracks on coating is lack of frosting or syrup inside the cake. Consequently, you get too dry cake layers which actively absorb moisture from the buttercream.
5764⠀
5765In this case, even though you kept the cake in the fridge overnight after assembly and covered it with a proper layer of buttercream, cracks may appear on the second day after smoothing.
5766⠀
5767If the article was helpful, leave 👍🏻 in the comments and I'll write a continuation.
5768WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GELATIN
5769⠀
5770Judging by the number of questions that I often receive, articles with an analysis of the ingredients are very relevant for many of you.
5771 
5772So here is the continuation about gelatin and I kindly ask you to support it with any comment.
5773⠀
5774A bloomed mixture of water and powdered gelatin is called a gelatin mass.
5775⠀
5776This term is often found in the recipes of famous chefs. It's very convenient: you make gelatin mass in advance and put it in the fridge, and when you need it, you take it out, weigh the required amount and use it.
5777⠀
5778The gelatin mass can be stored for up to a week. We’ll talk about it in more detail a little later.
5779⠀
5780📌 SHEET
5781⠀
5782The gelatin sheets are soaked in a fairly large amount of very cold water – so that the sheets are completely covered with water.
5783⠀
5784This type of gelatin will absorb just as much water as it needs. Usually it takes about 5-10 minutes.
5785⠀
5786Before use, squeeze the excess liquid and add the gelatin to the hot mass that you want to gell.
5787⠀
5788🌱 Gelatin strength
5789⠀
5790The strength of gelatin is measured according to 2 main classifications:
5791⠀
5792📌 in Bloom (in honor of Oscar Bloom). The most common gelatin is 125-265 Bloom.
5793⠀
5794📌 in categories - silver, gold, bronze and platinum (this system is used in America and Australia).
5795⠀
5796Bronze - 125-135 Bloom
5797Silver - 160-170 Bloom
5798Gold - 190-220 Bloom
5799Platinum - 235-265 Bloom
5800⠀
5801As for calculating the amount of gelatin, this is an ambiguous question, because it all depends on the density and properties of the mass that is being gellyfied (chocolate in the contents or juice), on the effect you want to get – thick, slightly jiggling jelly or delicate mousse, and on the strength of the gelatin itself.
5802⠀
5803But, on average, it is assumed that for 500 g of liquid, 10 g of gelatin with a strength of 180-200 Bloom is used.
5804⠀
5805And then you should decide whether the gelatin is enough, the product is too tender or, on the contrary, thick. Draw conclusions, make adjustments and adapt to the properties of your products.
5806LET'S FIND OUT MORE ABOUT CREAM CHEESES
5807⠀
5808I often receive questions about replacing Philadelphia with Mascarpone in recipes, difference between them and the reasons the frosting suddenly breaks after adding cream cheese to it.
5809⠀
5810In order to achieve a harmonious combination of ingredients, and sometimes even for the recipe to work, it is extremely important to add the cheese specified in the recipe. Soft cheeses are not always interchangeable!
5811⠀
5812Let's see what difference is between cheeses and why the interchange doesn't always work.
5813⠀
5814Cream cheese is classified as fresh cheese. Depending on the technology, there are acid, acid and heat, and acid and enzyme coagulated cheeses.
5815⠀
5816The most common fresh cheese is cream cheese.
5817⠀
5818An important feature of classic cream cheeses is their high fat content, which is 60-80%, as well as a pronounced characteristic creamy taste.
5819⠀
5820📌What is the difference between Mascarpone and cream cheese?
5821⠀
5822The fat content of Mascarpone reaches 80%, the taste of the product is slightly sweet and resembles thick sour cream or butter.
5823⠀
5824❗Classic Italian Mascarpone, in fact, is not cheese, since special enzymes and lactic acid bacteria are not used to make Mascarpone. Therefore, the name "cheese" is nominal.
5825⠀
5826📌How is Mascarpone different from Philadelphia?
5827⠀
5828✔Initially, Mascarpone was made in Italy. And it appeared much earlier. Philadelphia, in its turn, was made in the USA.
5829⠀
5830✔The fat content of Mascarpone is up to 80%. In Philadelphia it’s up to 69%.
5831⠀
5832✔Vinegar, tartaric acid or lemon juice are used to make Mascarpone. To make Philadelphia traditional ferments are used.
5833⠀
5834Before proceeding to the analysis of the interchangeability of soft fresh cheeses Cream Cheese and Mascarpone, let's have a closer look at the process of their manufacturing.
5835⠀
5836✔Cream cheese (Philadelphia) is made from two types of milk: skim and pasteurized. In order to denature the protein, a special ferment and heavy cream are added. As a result, this soft fresh cheese acquires a delicate and creamy aroma.
5837⠀
5838✔Mascarpone has one of the highest fat contents among soft fresh cheeses. For its manufacture, pasteurized buffalo milk or heavy cream is used, to which the required amount of tartaric acid and white wine vinegar is added. After that, the process of fermentation and coagulation begins. The last stage is cooling. After that, the cheese is packed in tight bags and hung to remove the excess whey.
5839⠀
5840As you can see, the raw materials for making these two cheeses (skim and pasteurized milk vs heavy cream or buffalo milk), as well as the technologies (ferment vs tartaric acid and vinegar) are completely different.
5841⠀
5842❗That’s why let me express this thought one more time - Mascarpone and Cream Cheese are not the same !!!❌
5843⠀
5844Now let's see if they are interchangeable. Well, to some extent yes, and to some extent - no. How is it possible?
5845⠀
5846Let's say, if it is really important for you to keep the texture and the taste of the frosting originally suggested by the author of the recipe, then these cheeses cannot be called interchangeable at all.
5847⠀
5848In fact, Mascarpone cannot be replaced by anything without affecting the taste of the product, as it has a unique viscous and creamy texture.
5849⠀
5850But what if keeping certain texture and taste of the frosting becomes a low priority, since now you have only Mascarpone / Cream Cheese at hand and you need to assemble the cake right away?
5851⠀
5852Let's have a look at exactly why the cream cheeses and mascarpone are relatively interchangeable.
5853 
5854I kindly ask you to support the post with ❤ and any emoticon in the comments, since you won’t find this information anywhere. I share with you my own experience, which has been accumulated over the years.
5855 
5856📌So, what are the cases in which it is possible to replace cream cheese with mascarpone and vice versa?
5857 
5858In principle, in any frosting or sour cream frosting that is used as filling of a sponge or honey cake.
5859 
5860If in your recipe, for example, there are 2 parts of cream cheese and 1 part of cream (2:1) or 1 part of cream, 1 part of cream cheese and 1 part of sour cream (1:1:1) you may easily replace the cheeses with each other and nothing bad will happen.
5861 
5862❗Important!
5863Consider the different texture and fat content of the cheeses. It might affect the taste of the pastry to some extent.
5864 
5865❗❗Much more important!
5866Work with mascarpone extremely carefully! Unlike frosting with cream cheese, frosting with mascarpone is very easy to overwhip - it may break in the blink of an eye and you’ll end up with lumps and curdled texture.
5867 
5868All this happens due to the capricious mascarpone.
5869 
5870Therefore, when working with the latter, keep an eye on the process of whipping the frosting, do not get distracted.
5871 
5872📌When are these cheeses completely not interchangeable?
5873 
5874In any buttercream, most often for coating the cake.
5875 
5876I can't count how many times students have sent me photos of broken buttercream with a request to help them fix it! After a look into the issue, it turned out that mascarpone was used instead of cream cheese. Please, remember, you should not do that!
5877 
5878Why?
5879 
5880Mascarpone does not tolerate temperature differences. If you add cold cream cheese to room temperature butter, nothing bad will happen. But if you add cold mascarpone, the buttercream will curdle in a wink.
5881 
5882Therefore, if you want to replace cream cheese with mascarpone in buttercream, change the recipe and the cooking technology, that's the only way.
5883So, we figured out the difference between cream cheese and Mascarpone and learnt that they are not always interchangeable.
5884⠀
5885Now let's talk about different brands of cream cheese. For example, are the most popular of them - Cremette and Philadelphia - interchangeable?
5886⠀
5887Well, yes and no.
5888⠀
5889Why? The taste and texture of these cheeses are similar, therefore, in this regard, they are completely interchangeable. But as for the consistency (thickness), the cheeses from different manufacturers can be absolutely different.
5890⠀
5891Will this somehow affect the final appearance / taste of the dessert? Certainly!
5892⠀
5893So, where does this fundamental difference in the consistency of cream cheeses come from, if the cooking technology is almost the same?
5894⠀
5895Different compositions. And first of all, it is the qualitative and quantitative content of thickeners and stabilizers.
5896⠀
5897For example, Philadelphia contains only locust bean gum which is a stabilizer, while Cremette contains starch (thickener), locust bean gum and guar gum (stabilizers).
5898⠀
5899Everyone who has worked with these cheeses should have noticed that frosting made with Cremette is always more stable and holds its shape better.
5900⠀
5901This is a key factor of the incomplete interchangeability of cream cheeses by different manufacturers. You should always make adjustments to the recipe based on the consistency of the cheese used.
5902⠀
5903I’m extremely grateful for the likes and comments under the posts. Their number gives me the opportunity to see how useful the article is, because I alway try to share relevant information with you.
5904 
5905So, let’s continue.
5906⠀
5907If you work with thicker cream cheese like Cremette, for example, then frosting can be prepared with a 2:1 ratio – two parts of cream cheese to one part of cream, and buttercream with a 3:1 ratio – three parts of cream cheese to one part of butter.
5908⠀
5909If you want to replace the cream cheese with a softer one, but at the same time keep the same stable consistency, then you need to change the proportions, for example, to the following: frosting 3:1 – three parts of cream cheese to one part of cream, buttercream 2:1 – two parts of cream cheese to one part of butter.
5910⠀
5911But sometimes the consistency of the cream cheeses are so different that you have not only to change the proportions, but even to change the cooking technology.
5912⠀
5913I’ll share with you one story from my practice.
5914⠀
5915It happened at a master class in Kuala Lumpur, the capital of Malaysia. An important thing to mention – it's Southeast Asia with an equatorial monsoon climate. You know, summer is all year round and the average temperature is about 32-33 °C / 90-91 °F.
5916⠀
5917So when, on the second day of the master class, the students started making the buttercream to coat the cakes, strange things began to happen.
5918⠀
5919To tell the truth, they did happen before too. Just then they took a new turn, let’s say. Just kidding.
5920⠀
5921The butter, as I asked, was taken out of the fridge in advance, so it was at room temperature, but the cream cheese, according to my instructions, was taken straight from the fridge.
5922⠀
5923"Chef, there are lumps in the buttercream, what should we do?" – in half an hour I hear a nervous sounding voice of the organizer, who took an active part in the process ...
5924⠀
5925⠀
5926If you are curious to know the end of the story, leave an emoticon in the comments. If there are a lot of your comments, I'll post a continuation soon!
5927Russian version 👉🏻@ma_rusya_manko
5928 
5929 
5930 
5931Since you supported the continuation of the story with comments, I am posting its end. Let’s create a win-win situation: I share an article, you leave likes ❤️
5932⠀
5933⠀
5934..."Chef, there are lumps in the buttercream, what should we do?" - in half an hour I heard a nervous sounding voice of the organizer, who took an active part in the process ...
5935⠀
5936"Whip it more until the lumps disappear." - I answered.
5937⠀
5938In 15-20 minutes:
5939⠀
5940“Chef, nothing has changed. The buttercream is still lumpy.” - said one of the students.
5941⠀
5942What nonsense, I thought, and went to check the buttercream by myself. As it turned out, it wasn’t nonsense, but a disaster! The buttercream contained solid lumps of cream cheese, which did not want to combine with the butter into a homogeneous mass.
5943⠀
5944I started to analyze the situation. As it turned out, due to the hot climate, the manufacturers add such a quantity of thickeners and stabilizers to cream cheese and other products, that even at room temperature the local cream cheese remains stone-like compared to the Cremette, which I usually work with.
5945⠀
5946As a result, we started to heat the cream cheese in the microwave until warm, process until smooth in a mixer, and then added whipped room temperature butter. Only in this way we succeeded in achieving a uniform texture of the buttercream.

5951WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW ABOUT GANACHE
5952Today is Monday and I am here with a useful post. I'm starting to think about making Monday and Thursday, for example, useful information days. What do you think?
5953⠀
5954Let's start with the elementary. What is ganache?
5955⠀
5956I am sure, who is not closely connected with the confectionery world, does not even have the slightest idea what we are talking about. For example, all my friends, every time I say "ganache", are sure that this is some kind of terrible curse 😅
5957⠀
5958So, ganache is a cream that usually consists of chocolate (white, milk or dark) melted in a liquid (milk, cream, fruit juice, or even crème anglaise).
5959⠀
5960Ganache can be supplemented on various occasions with sugar, butter or flavors, after which it is cooled and used as intended.
5961⠀
5962In fact, ganache is a stable and balanced emulsion-like chain in which fat molecules are distributed in an aqueous medium.
5963⠀
5964There are different types of ganache, depending on its texture:
5965📌 more and less dense (regulated by changing the amount of the liquid component)
5966📌 whipped and prepared using Chantilly cream technology (determined by the nature of stirring).
5967⠀
5968Proportions for a thick ganache suitable for macaron fillings:
5969⠀
5970📌 On dark chocolate 54.5% - 1 part chocolate to 1 part cream (33%)
5971📌 On milk chocolate 33% - 3 parts chocolate to 2 parts cream (33%)
5972📌 On white chocolate 28% - 2 parts chocolate to 1 part cream (33%)
5973⠀
5974Ganache is used for filling tarts, for classic French cakes, for interlayers in modern cakes, in pastries, in desserts. Ganache is the main filling for chocolate sweets.
5975⠀
5976It would seem that it’s very simple — heat the heavy cream, poured it over the chocolate, punch it with a blender, cool and it's done. But there are difficulties and nuances that you have to know about...
5977⠀
5978⠀
5979In the continuation of the article, I will tell you the possible causes of stratification, how to properly stabilize and store ganache.
5980⠀
5981Let's also take a look at all the possible additives and consider how they affect taste, texture and shelf life of ganache.
5982⠀
5983Today we will figure out how to properly make the ganache using different methods.
5984⠀
5985📝 Weigh the liquid ingredients (milk / whipping cream / puree / juice), sugar (trimoline, if any) and chocolate exactly by grams.
5986⠀
5987📝 If you don’t use professional chocolate callets (which I highly recommend), you need to prepare the chocolate.
5988⠀
5989📝 In order to do this, break a chocolate bar into small pieces. The smaller the pieces are, the easier it will be to melt the chocolate.
5990⠀
5991⠀
5992📍1 Method
5993⠀
5994📌 Heat the liquid ingredients with the sugar, if it is in the recipe, in the microwave oven or on the stove until very hot and pour over the chocolate. Leave for 20-30 sec and then mix thoroughly with a silicone spatula until homogeneous texture.
5995⠀
5996📌 If there are small pieces of chocolate in the ganache, you may slightly heat it up in the microwave oven or over the water bath. In no case put it directly on the stove - the mixture will start to burn.
5997⠀
5998⠀
5999📍2 Method
6000⠀
6001📌 Pour the liquid ingredients (with or without sugar) into a container with chocolate broken into pieces and heat everything together in the microwave oven several times for 5-15 seconds.
6002⠀
6003📌 The most important thing here is not to overheat the chocolate! While it is completely hard - check it every 15 seconds - take it out and mix. Once the chocolate starts to melt - every 10 seconds. When only small pieces remain - every 5 seconds. Take it out and mix each time.
6004⠀
6005⠀
6006📍3 Method*
6007⠀
6008📌 Heat the liquid part (with sugar, if any) to 85 °C / 185 °F and cool it to 35 °C / 95 °F. While the liquid is cooling, melt the chocolate to 32 °C / 90 °F.
6009⠀
6010📌 Add the liquid to the chocolate in three steps and mix thoroughly with a silicone spatula each time.
6011⠀
6012📌 The last step - process the emulsion with a hand blender, trying to prevent the air bubbles from entering the ganache.
6013⠀
6014📌 If everything was done correctly, the ganache prepared in this way will be stable and ready to be used almost immediately. Also, it will not separate.
6015🌿 Broken ganache. Possible reasons and way outs:
6016⠀
6017📌 insufficient amount of liquid - in this case you need to add more whipping cream, milk or puree
6018⠀
6019📌 poor dispersion of fats in water - process the mass with an emulsion blender
6020⠀
6021📌 too high indoor temperature – process the mass with a blender and cool it down.
6022⠀
6023🌿 The obtained ganache can be used immediately - pour it hot into a tart base or onto a sponge, for example. Or you may let it stabilize to the desired texture. The basic principle in the latter case is to prevent the air from entering into it.
6024⠀
6025🌿 If you want to leave the ganache to stabilize to the desired thickness, it is best to do this at 15-18 °C / 59-64 °F. Pour the ganache onto a baking sheet previously covered with cling film or guitar sheet so that the ganache layer is as thin as possible - this allows the mass to cool faster and more evenly.
6026⠀
6027🌿 For storage and cooling, you should cover the ganache with cling film or guitar sheet in contact – they should touch the surface of the ganache. Thus, the ganache is protected from air, microorganisms and the evaporation of aromatic substances.
6028⠀
6029🌿 Transfer the stabilized ganache into a piping bag using a scraper or a spatula. Do not mix or whip it. The texture of the ganache should be stable, but at the same time pliable.
6030⠀
6031🌿 Store it at 4 °C / 39 °F and use within 2 days. Ganache can be frozen when added to the products in order to streamline the production process.
6032⠀
6033🌿 It is not advisable to cool the ganache until it is completely stabilized in the fridge. It is better when it sets completely after piping (for example, in macarons).
6034⠀
6035🌿 If the ganache completely sets, and you start to mix it with a spatula, heat it in a microwave oven, partially knead it in a bag with your hands, then it may result in matte, not beautiful and even broken ganache.
6036⠀
6037P.S. The photos show Prague cake decorated with a fluffy cloud of dark chocolate whipped ganache.
6038Today we’ll analyze the components of ganache, their effect on its texture and taste, as well as dosages.
6039⠀
6040🌿 Chocolate
6041⠀
6042📎 You may use white, milk (33-41%) or dark chocolate (50-79%) as well as cocoa paste (in some cases).
6043⠀
6044❗️ Chocolate should be stored in a waterproof hermetic container. When water gets into the ganache, its shelf life and quality decrease.
6045⠀
6046📌 Texture
6047• due to the cocoa butter in its contents, ganache becomes runny when heated and thick when cooled.
6048• the lower the cocoa butter content and the higher the sugar content, the softer the ganache consistency.
6049⠀
6050📌 Taste
6051• it has a peculiar taste of white, milk and dark chocolate
6052• the typical chocolate bitterness is enhanced (70% of cocoa products or more) and leveled out (50% of cocoa products or less).
6053⠀
6054🌿 Liquid
6055⠀
6056📎 Fresh or pasteurized cream with the fat content of 35-40%. Due to the minimal heat treatment, the rich taste is preserved, but the shelf life of the ready ganache is reduced, so professionals hardly ever use such cream.
6057📎 Sterilized or ultra-pasteurized cream with the fat content of 35-37% (preferred).
6058📎 Milk. Can be used to replace some amount of cream or instead of it. Concentrated milk (sugar free) is often used to reduce the amount of water added, which negatively affects the shelf life of the ganache.
6059📎 Fruit purees for making fruit ganaches.
6060⠀
6061📌Dosage
6062• milk: maximum 40%
6063• cream: 20-50%
6064• puree: maximum 50%
6065⠀
6066📌Texture
6067• fat content of 35% and more makes the texture delicate and melting when tasting
6068• saturation with liquid - water (about 60%) dissolves sugar and makes the consistency of the chocolate less dense.
6069⠀
6070📌Taste
6071• due to fat molecules it has aroma-retaining properties
6072• makes the bitterness of chocolate with a high content of cocoa products (for example, more than 70%) milder.
6073⠀
6074⠀
6075To be continued 👉🏻
6076Please support this article with any emoticon in the comments 🙏🏻
6077⠀
6078 
6079In continuation of the article let’s continue analyzing the components of ganache, their effect on its texture and taste, as well as dosages.
6080 
6081❗In some cases (for example, when making whipped ganache), whipping cream is a necessary ingredient for increasing the volume (due to the protein content of 1-2%), and surface activity of proteins allows to retain and stabilize air bubbles in the cream.
6082⠀
6083🌿Fats
6084⠀
6085📎The butter with the lowest melting point (32-34 °C / 90-93 °F) is used.
6086Butter with fat content of 82% and more can be used (the lower the moisture content of the butter is, the longer the final product can be stored).
6087⠀
6088❗With the excess of fat, ganache may destabilize (emulsion may break).
6089 
6090❗If the fat content is insufficient, the ganache will be too thick.
6091 
6092📌Recommended dosage of butter: 5-15%
6093📌Recommended dosage of fat (butter together with cocoa oil): 25-40%
6094 
6095📌Texture
6096• if butter is added to the cream before it is brought to a boil, the fat molecules will be evenly distributed in the mass. This heat treatment prolongs the shelf life of the product.
6097• if you add butter at the end of cooking (at 30-35 °C / 86-95 °F) without pre-melting it, the ganache will acquire a smooth and creamy consistency, glossy shine, as well as a delicate and melting texture (felt when tasted)
6098• when butter is heated, the consistency of ganache becomes runnier, and when butter cools, it becomes thicker.
6099 
6100📌Taste
6101• due to fat molecules it has aroma-retaining properties
6102• makes the bitterness of chocolate with a high content of cocoa products (for example, more than 70%) milder.
6103⠀
6104⠀
6105To be continued 👉🏻
6106Please support this article with any emoticon in the comments 🙏🏻
6107⠀
6108The continuation of the article where I analyze the components of ganache, their effect on its texture and taste, as well as dosages.
6109⠀ ⠀
6110🌿 Sugar
6111⠀
6112📎 Sugar (sucrose), inverted sugar and/or glucose syrup (high in dextrose) can be used.
6113⠀⠀
6114📌 The recommended dosage of sugars is at least 25% of the total weight.
6115⠀
6116📌 Storage
6117• due to hygroscopic properties, it retains water, binds components and increases the thickness of the mass, giving it stability.
6118• combining with water molecules from whipping cream, sugars reduce the number of unbound water molecules, thus limiting the environment for the growth of microorganisms (mold) (this is especially important when storing food at subzero temperatures).⠀
6119⠀
6120📌 Texture
6121• gives softness and plasticity to the cream
6122⠀
6123📌 Taste
6124• gives sweetness
6125⠀
6126🌿 Additives
6127⠀
6128📎 Stabilizer: Sorbitol E420
6129Sorbitol crystals are capable of binding free water molecules.
6130⠀
6131📌 Recommended dosage: from 1.5 to 2.5% of the total weight.
6132⠀⠀
6133📌 Texture
6134• it's a powerful hygroscopic agent that retains and stabilizes moisture.
6135⠀
6136📌 Storage
6137• prolongs the shelf life by reducing the activity of unbound water molecules.
6138⠀
6139🌿 Aromatic additives
6140⠀
6141❗️All additives sensitive to microflora (cocoa powder, kitchen herbs, spices, etc.) are to be boiled for several minutes with whipping cream.
6142⠀
6143📌 Impact factor
6144• taste as well as color
6145⠀ ⠀
6146Hot whipping cream or milk can be infused with spices, herbs and citrus zest in a saucepan covered with a lid. Extracts, essential oils and alcohol can be used as well.
6147At the end of the article as promised, I post a basic ganache recipe:
6148⠀
6149180 g of dark chocolate 54.5%
6150200 g of whipping cream 33-36%
615170 g of butter 82-83%
615220 g of inverted sugar (optional)
615315 g of sorbitol
6154⠀
6155Total weight: 450g
6156⠀
6157📌 Chop the chocolate to make it easier to melt when it comes into contact with the cream. Transfer it to a bowl large enough to add the cream to the chocolate.
6158⠀
6159There is no need to chop chocolate callets.
6160⠀
6161📌 For better storage, bring the cream, butter and inverted sugar to a boil. Then add sorbitol.
6162⠀
6163📌 Gradually pour the cream onto the chocolate. First, a small amount of cream is added. You have to obtain a homogeneous mass by stirring it from the center. The emulsion should not be broken when adding the remaining cream in several steps.
6164⠀
6165📌 Stir the mass thoroughly, but gently with a whisk, then process it with a blender or in a robot-coupe. This stage is necessary to distribute fat molecules evenly throughout the mass and achieve a uniform consistency.
6166⠀
6167❗️Process the ganache with a blender while it is hot to avoid air bubbles getting into the cream. This is important due to the fact that:
6168👌🏼 air is a favorable breeding ground for microorganisms
6169👌🏼 oxygen causes fats to go rancid.
6170⠀
6171📌 The ready ganache can be used immediately (while it’s still hot, pour it into a tart base or onto a sponge), or you can let it thicken and then pipe it with a pastry bag. The basic principle in this case is to try not to let the air get into the ganache.
6172⠀
6173📌 For further storage and cooling down, cover the ganache with cling film touching the surface. Thus, you protect it from drying out, microorganisms and the evaporation of aromatic substances.
6174⠀
6175Store it at + 4°C / 39°F and use it within 2 days. You can freeze it in the products in order to rationalize the production process.
6176⠀
6177🖋The article is based on the book Pâtisserie Les Clés de la Réussite, Herrero et Etienne
6178⠀
